About this text

The Extensive Commentary on the Condensed Meaning of the Hevajra Tantra, known in Sanskrit as Hevajrapiṇḍārthaṭīkā and in Tibetan as Kyé Dorjé düpé dön gyi gyaché drelpa, is a commentary on the Hevajra Tantra, one of the major texts of the Buddhist tantric tradition. It opens with homage to glorious Hevajra and a verse of praise to the deity, then moves into an explanation of his symbolism, in which the deity's eight faces, sixteen hands, and other features are read as embodiments of liberations, emptinesses, and compassions. As an exegetical work, it aims to draw out the meaning of the root tantra's condensed teachings and to guide practitioners toward realizing the deity's nature. It is Toh 1180 in the Tengyur, the canonical collection of Indian treatises and commentaries, where it belongs to the tantra commentary class.

Translation

In the Indian language: Hevajrapiṇḍārthaṭīkā. In the Tibetan language: The Extensive Commentary on the Condensed Meaning of the Hevajra Tantra.

Homage to glorious Hevajra.

He has set aside all worldly conduct; the very nature of the Victors, he is ever present and all-pervading. He is without any duality, free of stain, beyond the senses, and the knower of all bliss. He is Evaṃ, the supreme, the peaceful. His mind is not a mind, and that mind is not insentient matter; he is the sovereign of all faculties. He is the very essence of all the buddhas, the nectar body. To realize him I pay homage.

The eight qualities arise from the utterly vanquished; the eight liberations are the eight faces of the principal deity. The emptinesses are the sixteen hands, and the compassions are these skull cups. The fifty unchanging syllables, which express suchness, are arranged as his garland of heads, strung upon his shoulders. In his crown is Akṣobhya, and he bears a half moon, a garland of vajras, and the nature of the skull cup. He stands trampling the māras beneath his fair feet, together with Maitreya and the others and the Vimokṣas.

The afflictive and cognitive obscurations are gathered together; his chest bears the mudrā of union and a tiger skin. The māras and the other obscurations are like a mere fragment to him. He is adorned for the sake of sentient beings. Having bowed to him, I shall write this commentary that explains the secret of his tantra.

Wishing to benefit all sentient beings, the glorious Vajragarbha, sovereign of the ten grounds, drew forth from the great tantra of five hundred thousand verses a brief tantra, well extracted, so that yogis might gain the path. This tantra, which holds many vajra words in seven hundred and fifty verses, was requested by Hevajra himself. The text is six thousand verses long. Whatever follows the root tantra, this serves to clarify the meaning of this tantra.

In this age of the five degenerations, there will arise teachers who proclaim the conduct of yoga but do not abide on the true path. Without a commentary they teach the brief tantra, with evil practice, desiring the wives and wealth of others, and are strongly bent on going to hell. Some, lacking the five clairvoyances and the like, will compose commentaries. Through the pride of logical treatises they will despise the practice of yoga. Buddha Vajrasattva himself, they say, has taken hold of them through the empowerment with great effort. Some will tell people, "I am a vajra holder." They will not bow to any monks who keep the vows of moral discipline. Standing on the ground, these so-called vajra holders will say that they themselves, white-robed householders, deserve homage. Merchants, people of low class, and those who delight in the work of farmers, the deluded who sell the sacred Dharma and enjoy what should not be enjoyed, belonging to the family of Māra, will become the teachers of disciples. Using the pretense of yogic conduct they hide their own faults, and to remove blame from themselves they will certainly come to speak. A disciple should never find fault with the qualities a teacher is to be held to possess. Fault-finding here brings no accomplishment; of this there is no doubt whatever. A wise person should not take as teacher one who is without compassion, cruel in anger, arrogant, unrestrained in desire, and self-praising. These words should not be spoken to disciples in any way.

Those who are merciless in their deeds, wrathful, and wounding to the vital points, who are cruel, who hate monks and people, who are stiff and puffed up with evil knowledge, who have great desires and seek what is not to be sought, who drink liquor to excess and are unrestrained, who praise themselves here and find fault with those of true qualities: they do not understand what is meant when it is said that the Tathāgatas, dwelling in the world realms of the ten directions, come in the three times and pay homage to the vajra teacher who has received the supreme empowerment. Deluded by mere names, they slander the lords of the Victors and will go to wailing.

The one who received empowerment from Vajrasattva at his crown, beneath the tree of awakening, and who has gained the supreme empowerment, is the vajra teacher, to whom the Tathāgatas pay homage. The bodhisattvas of the ten directions, in as many bodies as there are world realms, dwelling there with their five aggregates, come in the three times and pay homage. Then the precious crown, and the various objects of the world realms, and the buddhas who dwell there pay homage: that is the yogī vajra teacher.

As is explained in symbolic language: Vairocana is urine and semen; likewise Akṣobhya and Amitābha. In this way the vajra holder, the teacher, bestows the mudrās of feces, urine and semen, just as the teacher gives the accomplishments of body, speech and mind. In this way the vajra holder bestows them. Among disciples and yogis, conventionally and ultimately, there is the empowerment in the maṇḍala and beneath the tree of awakening, the earlier and the later. There are teachings with intention and without intention, with literal sense and not literal; likewise definitive meaning and provisional meaning. The tantra has the characteristics of the six alternatives.

Whatever qualities of the teacher are shown here in conventional terms are the qualities of Vajrasattva: the characteristics such as the six clairvoyances. He is steady, tamed, and wise; patient, upright, without deceit; he knows the application of mantra and tantra; he is compassionate and skilled in the treatises. He fully knows the ten aspects of suchness, is skilled in the work of drawing the maṇḍala, is of serene nature, and has subdued his faculties. Such is the teacher who holds the secret mantra. One who has these qualities that have been explained becomes a monk holding the vajra. Novices and householders are not like these; the ten are not equal to the monk here. It is explained that those who have the ten bases of training, those who have the five bases of training, and those who have the limit of the bases of training are not equal, these three.

As was taught in the root tantra, the state of the monk who holds the vajra, and whatever pleases the teacher, should be told to the disciple. Questioned by Nairātmyā, the Vajra One spoke these words in due order of stages, in order to give the bases of training.

First, the five bases of training, together with the restoration ceremony and the rest, are to be given along with the complete abandoning of the ten non-virtues; having given them, one makes the person a lay practitioner. Then, having given the ten bases of training, one makes him a novice. Then, having given the countless bases of training, one makes him a monk, one who takes up the discipline of conduct. After that he should come to know the Prātimokṣa, then the system of the Vaibhāṣikas, then, having learned the sūtra tradition, the Vijñānavāda's own teaching. Then he should know the Perfection of Wisdom and the Middle Way, the intended meaning of the Buddha. Having thus listened to other tantras, he should then come to know the Hevajra.

From the definitive emergence of the three vehicles, one rests in the fruit of a single vehicle. The śrāvaka, the solitary buddha, and here the Great Vehicle are three. A fourth and a fifth vehicle among Buddhists are not the intention of the Sage.

Emptiness, endowed with the supreme of all aspects, is first the cause; then, through aspiration, comes compassion; and afterwards the fruit is working for the welfare of beings. One who, having accomplished his own aim, then works for the aim of others, is a wise person. One who is himself afflicted by suffering and poor, how could he give to others? One who is endowed with happiness and sovereign abundance exerts himself out of compassion for the sake of beings. For those who seek cause and fruit: as long as the cause has not been made manifest, until then, from what could a fruit arise at the start without a cause?

Other vehicles are spoken of here alongside the Great Vehicle; this is for the sake of rest, namely the practices of giving, moral discipline, and the like, which are not themselves the goal. The emptiness of all phenomena, I have taught, is what brings about definitive emergence. From that cause which brings about emergence, and then by the power of aspiration, the great fruit whose nature is compassion arises without any focal object. Compassion is taught as threefold: with sentient beings as its object, with phenomena as its object, and without an object. The compassion that does only a little for the welfare of a lifetime takes sentient beings and phenomena as its object. The accomplished bodhisattvas, who abide on the ten grounds, have it as their very nature. The Sugatas, whose twelve links have ceased and whose nature abides on all the grounds, are said to work for the welfare of beings without a focal object.

Whatever the cause is like, such is the fruit. Even in some kodrava, śāli rice will not arise. As the seed, so the tree; as the tree, so the fruit. In the beginning, the middle, and the end, the word of the Sage is virtuous. At first, from a cause accompanied by conceptual thought, a fruit accompanied by conceptual thought arises; for all Buddhists there is conceptual thought, and in the end the fruit is not emptiness. It is as when, at first, to purify fields, one sows kodrava, and afterwards sows the seed of śāli in the purified field. Beings born on the ground of karma are said to be impure fields. Those born in the six impure destinies are spoken of as the fruit of virtue and non-virtue. In the pure field of human birth, because the seed of compassion without a focal object is sown, there springs up the wish-fulfilling tree of emptiness. From the wish-fulfilling tree of emptiness, the fruit of objectless compassion arises, once all beings have been thought of. There is no doubt about this.

To a person who lacks the fortune for the profound and vast teachings, I have taught whatever is pleasing, such as mudrās, maṇḍalas, and mantras. Those future teachers who, through mudrās, maṇḍalas, mantras, and the like, would point out the buddha Vajrasattva himself are of the lineage of Māra. Therefore, for a monk of the Great Vehicle, the teacher is said to be the Vajradhara, one who fully understands the three vehicles and who shows the compendium of the Dharma.

Among the Vedic and other systems and the yogas made by Īśvara and others, and among the mantra tantras of the Buddhists, there is a difference in regard to emptiness. Whoever, deluded in his nature, does not know this causes all his disciples to fall. Without an agent there is no action; without action there is no fruit. "The great illusion, the great fierce one, who brings forth and withdraws, himself the seizer, himself the doer, himself the king, himself the chief": thus the illusion of Īśvara was also taught by Īśvara, in the tenets of the non-Buddhists who hold peace. Viṣṇu, through the teaching of songs, tames the difficult to tame, and he too protects well: "Whenever on this earth the Dharma becomes weak and what is not Dharma advances, then I take birth, in order to protect the good and to destroy the evildoers. For the sake of establishing the Dharma, I come forth from age to age." Knowing in this way all those who teach the Dharma wrongly, the Hevajra himself urged me to compose a commentary.

Homage to the supreme body of intrinsic nature, who is worshipped by the three worlds, stainless; to the one called Dharma, the enjoyment body, the teacher who has truly abandoned happiness and suffering; and to the emanation, who in the sky shows manifold miraculous displays, like an illusion or a conjuror's trick. Having bowed, I shall explain briefly the definitive meaning of the secret words of this condensed tantra.

This brief Hevajra tantra of the buddhas, which is here made especially clear, is the first of the five hundred thousand, the tantra assembly of one hundred thousand verses; and it is the great compilation known as the Abhidhāna of the thousand-fold redactions, and the assembly of the tantra of the Kings of tantras. Elsewhere too, where the Victor gave teaching for humans, the meaning is understood through commentaries. Whoever relies on the brief tantra without a commentary and teaches its secret words is like a blind man, after a snake has entered the water, gazing at the trail long after it has gone. In all the tantras the Victor taught not only the definitive meaning but also the provisional meaning. If the provisional meaning were made clear by a commentary, there would be no point in realizing the definitive.

The Victor's tantras, all of them, are taught with intended meaning and without intended meaning. Others are taught in accordance with the sound, and not in accordance with the sound; and both definitive meaning and provisional meaning are shown.

The aggregates, the elements, and the like, so long as they are stained, are not themselves the deity here. The deity's faces and hands are not perfect buddhahood; they are deliberately imputed by conceptual thought.

The tantras that the Blessed One taught, the Guhyasamāja and the others, encircled by the six alternatives, humble the pride of the learned in the treatises. How could they be grasped by mere reasoning?

This knowing of the three times, exact as a reflection in a mirror, is a wisdom free of the net of concepts of existence and non-existence; it is nonduality itself.

The Blessed One spoke of the thirty-two channels through which the seminal essence descends in great bliss, reaching the throat, and likewise what is in the heart, the navel and the secret place. And what of the descent reaching the joints of the hands and feet and the joints of the fingers? The Victor never spoke of these to me, the Essence of Hevajra.

What was taught to the monks and people of the monastery was likewise in no language other than Māgadhī. What the Blessed One taught in the three collections accords with the tradition of the Sthaviras, for a particular remedy suits a particular illness, so as to cure that particular disease.

These are the four reliances, beginning with reliance on the meaning, and not on the letters and words. The Blessed One, who teaches all phenomena at once in the languages of the gods, asuras, nāgas, yakṣas and others, wondrous and beyond all worldly comparison, spoke in that voice. So, too, with "Thus have I..." and the like, well constructed and possessing the proper characteristics: why should the learned not marvel at the claim that it was taught in the monastery?

The body of the Blessed One, Ḍākinī-like, endowed with every supreme quality, like a conjuring trick, like an illusion: the child and the wise grasp it differently, and its definitive explanation is this. It is adorned with the thirty-two major marks and the eighty fine minor marks. The aggregates, elements, sense faculties and objects dwell together within the maṇḍala.

Fools always want to know dependent origination as arising and coming forth. Reckonings of destruction and birth, of lunar days and planetary movements, are taught in other tantras. Some children want curd without milk, and some want milk with no cow having been born; where there is no application, what fault lies with the one who does not know?

By "Hevajra" the Cakra of Great Bliss and the four seats should be understood. By "Hevajra" and the Cakra of Great Bliss, this short text should be understood through the four seats. By the supreme first Buddha-word and by the Great Bliss, the definitive meaning should be understood. As for tantras of this kind, and other tantras, they are always to be known through teaching and extensive explanation; by commentary, for those who desire the path.

This is the first complete verse, praising the deity desired through the six-thousand-verse commentary on Hevajra and entering into the tantra.

Now the teaching of the tantra is to be explained. Here, first, the Blessed One, clearly seeing the subject to be expressed, the expression, the connection, the purpose, and the purpose of the purpose, grants buddhahood in this very life to beings to be trained who are without certainty, according to the disposition of their own minds. Dwelling in the syllables E and VAṂ, encircled by a net of ḍākinīs, absorbed in the samādhi of Hevajra, petitioned by Vajragarbha, and also questioned by Nairātmyā, he taught this seven-hundred-verse tantra, a short tantra with its half, extracted from the five hundred thousand verses of the Hevajra. The subject to be expressed here is the Blessed One Hevajra together with Nairātmyā. The king of tantras, the collection of chapters that realizes this, is the expression. The relation of subject and expression between them is the connection. Giving the empowerments, such as entry into the maṇḍala, is the purpose. Having empowered, making suchness clear is the purpose of the purpose. Clearly seeing these, he taught to beings to be trained who are without certainty, that is, who are without the vows of the śrāvakas. "According to the disposition of their own minds" means with the mind that partakes of the five desires. "Grants buddhahood in this very life" means repeatedly in a human birth, and not in the birth of gods, asuras and the like.

"Dwelling in the syllables E and VAṂ" means dwelling in the ground of all bliss. "Encircled by a net of ḍākinīs" means by those having the characteristics of aggregates, elements and sense sources. "Absorbed in the samādhi of Hevajra, petitioned by Vajragarbha, fully questioned by the wisdom Nairātmyā": from the root tantra of Hevajra, in five hundred thousand verses and thirty-two great sections, the short tantra was fully awakened to. The tantra gathered within the measure of a text of seven hundred verses with its half, comprising twenty-two chapters, the second section, characterized by the net of illusion, was completely taught by the Blessed One Vajrasattva. By the worldly and supramundane truths, for the sake of worldly and supramundane accomplishments, he taught that the wheel of the maṇḍala is to be meditated upon through the generation and completion stages, and through meditation on suchness.

In this tantra the subject to be expressed is Hevajra, and the tantra of Hevajra is the expression.

That which is to be expressed also abides in the very nature of what expresses it. First it abides in the nature of the chapter on the vajra family; then in the nature of the chapter on mantra; then in the nature of the chapter on the deities; likewise in the nature of the chapter on empowering the deities; in the nature of the chapter on suchness; in the nature of the chapter on conduct; in the nature of the chapter on the symbolic language and the settling of the topics; in the nature of the chapter on the gathering of the yoginī wheel; in the nature of the chapter on purification; in the nature of the chapter on empowerment; and in the nature of the chapter on the king of tantras, the manifest and complete awakening. These are the eleven summary verses, and they belong to the first chapter.

Next comes the chapter on the Net of Illusion. "Illusion" means the ḍākinī, and "net" means the assembly. "Saṃvara" means the gathering, and the phrase "together with the Herukas" is a statement of certainty.

In this very tantra, first the following abide in the nature of their chapters: consecration, fire offering, hearth, the various rites, and the chapter on offerings. Then there is the chapter on settling the accomplishments. Likewise there is the introduction to all the tantras, together with the chapter on the symbolic language; the chapter on the meaning of the condensed mudrās of all the tantras; the chapter on the manifestation of Hevajra; the purification of the mudrās, together with the determination of how to draw painted images and write books; the chapter on the rite of eating; the characteristics of the lotus bearer, together with the chapter on taming disciples; the purification of the deities, together with the chapter on gathering the mantras; the chapter on reciting the mantras; and the introduction on yoga through the meaning of the coemergent, together with the granting of permission. Thus the tantra abides in its own nature as consisting of twenty-two chapters.

The Blessed One Hevajra addressed Vajragarbha, and there the Blessed One declared: The essence of the body, speech and mind of all the Tathāgatas is wisdom, which is great bliss. That itself, noble one, is more secret than the secret, and must be hidden, indeed carefully hidden, from those who delight in conceptual meditation.

"Eh-ma! Vajragarbha, one of great compassion, excellent, excellent! Vajrasattva, bodhisattva, great being, essence of the samaya being, listen to what is called Hevajra." This the Blessed One spoke, and it is stated at the end of the place of dwelling. Therefore the place where the mantra dwells is to be stated as what the Blessed One spoke. Afterwards come the Blessed One's words of urging and the request made by Vajragarbha.

"Thus did I hear at one time: the Blessed One dwelt in the bhagas of the vajra ladies, the body, speech and mind of all the Tathāgatas." This is the sentence about dwelling.

Now, regarding the words "Thus did I hear at one time," whose words are they? Are they those of the speaker, the omniscient one, or of the compiler who makes the request? In either case there is a fault with regard to the provisional meaning. If the speaker were to teach what he himself had heard in a past time, then he would lack wisdom concerning the future, since he knows only by having heard it from another. If they belong to the compiler, then the words "thus" and so on would be pointless, since Vajrasattva did not speak them. Therefore this sentence is to be understood as of definitive meaning by way of another symbolic language.

Its definitive meaning is this: The syllable E, the excellent divider, adorned in its center with VAṂ, is the abode of all bliss, the jewel casket of the buddhas. This is great bliss, Vajrasattva. He himself is the speaker, "I," who is great bliss. "Heard" means known. "By me" means by myself. "At one time" means at the moment of manifest and complete awakening in a single instant. "The Blessed One" is great bliss. "All the Tathāgatas," in symbolic language, are the five aggregates. "Their body, speech and mind, the vajra ladies": the functions of body, speech and mind, the vajra ladies, are the elements such as space, which are the qualities of the object. Through the distinctions of the life-breath moving in and out, and of the one, two, three, four and five qualities, they dwell in the bhagas of the lotuses at the crown, the forehead, the throat, the heart and the navel. And through the distinction of three qualities, they abide at the navel, the secret place and the vajra jewel. By the distinctions of joy and the rest, the awakening mind dwells in these.

In reverse order, the reverse of this: when the qualities are exhausted, it dwells in the vajra jewel and the secret place; when the objects are exhausted, it dwells in these: the navel, the heart, the throat, the forehead and the crown. This is by the distinctions of the concordant cause and so on. Thus the elements, aggregates and so on are to be expressed completely, through the absence of obscuration, as the activity of great bliss.

The syllable E is Locanā. The syllable VAṂ is Māmakī. The syllable MA is Pāṇḍaravāsinī. The syllable YĀ is Tārā. The syllable ŚRU is Vajradhātvīśvarī. The syllable TAṂ is Prajñāpāramitā.

Next come the aggregates. The syllable E is Vajrasattva. The syllable KA is Akṣobhya. The syllable SMIN is Amoghasiddhi. The syllable SA is Ratnasambhava. The syllable MA is Amitābha. The syllable YE is Vairocana. The syllable BHA is Gandhavajrā. The syllable GA is Rasavajrā. The syllable VĀN is Rūpavajrā. The syllable SA is Sparśavajrā. The syllable RBA is Śabdavajrā. The syllable TA is Dharmadhātuvajrā. The syllable THĀ is Sarvanīvaraṇaviṣkambhin. The syllable GA is Avalokiteśvara. The syllable TA is Kṣitigarbha. The syllable KĀ is Ākāśagarbha. The syllable YA is Vajrapāṇi.

The letter vāk is Samantabhadra. In this way the twelve sense sources are complete. The letter tsi is the goddess who makes stiffness. The letter tta is the goddess of pride. The letter ba is the goddess who makes dullness. The letter jra is the goddess of intense diligence. The letter yo is the goddess of deep blue. The letter ṣit is the goddess of fierce eyes. The letter bha is Gaṇḍa's beauty. The letter ga is the crown protuberance. The letter ṣu is the Ender of Obstructors. The letter bi is the Ender of Wisdom. The letter ja is the Ender of the Lotus. The letter hā is the Ender of Yama. The letter ra is Vajrasattva, whose nature is wisdom and means, the thirty-seventh chief figure.

Thus the thirty-seven factors of the side of awakening are completely purified, each in its proper order. They are the six elements, the six aggregates, the six objects, the six faculties, the six activities of the faculties of action, the six faculties of action, and great bliss. This is what is meant by "from the exhaustion of the fourth." Therefore they are the four establishments of mindfulness, the four right abandonments, the four bases of miraculous power, the five faculties, the five powers, the noble eightfold path, and the seven limbs of awakening.

Thus from great bliss, from essential nature, arises the Dharma body. From the exhaustion of the fourth, deep sleep is exhausted. From the exhaustion of deep sleep, dream is exhausted. From the exhaustion of dream, waking is exhausted. From the exhaustion of these, in due order, the coemergent body, the Dharma body, the complete enjoyment body, and the emanation body come to be, and wisdom, mind, speech, and body become free of obscuration. Therefore:

Everywhere hands and feet,
everywhere eyes and heads and faces,
everywhere ears, in the world
he abides, enveloping all.

And therefore:

Partless, all-pervading, all-going,
subtle, undefiled, the seed;
free of dust, dust-less, stainless,
rid of faults, without flaw;
fully awakened, the self of awakening,
all-knowing, all-cognizing, the supreme one,
beyond the nature of consciousness,
upholding the way of nondual wisdom.

Thus the Blessed One Hevajra, who is freed from all obscurations and whose nature is the four bodies, is the teacher, the lord of the twelve. The bodhisattva Vajragarbha, the lord of the ten, is the one who makes the request. This is the second section, which settles the teacher, the petitioner, the expresser, the expressed, the connection, and the place of dwelling in the Hevajra commentary of six thousand lines.

Now the petition of Vajragarbha and the clear reply of the Blessed One are to be set out. Vajragarbha petitions with the words beginning "By what."

By what does one become a vajra-being?
How does one become a bodhisattva?
Why is one a great being?
How is one the chief of the samaya-being?

Having heard Vajragarbha's petition, the Blessed One declared:

"Indivisible vajra" is the term spoken.
The being is the three existences as one.
By this method of wisdom
one is called a vajra-being.
One who abides in awakening
should be called a bodhisattva.
One filled with the flavor of great wisdom
should be called a great being.
Because one always abides in samaya,
one is declared a samaya-being.

The extensive explanation of this summary is made fully clear in the Hundred Thousand, the fifth section. Therefore one should turn to that very text. There the Blessed One declared:

What is taught as "indivisible vajra"
is renowned as the letter vam.
The body, speech, and mind that are indivisible
are expressed by the letter vam.
The being who is the three existences as one
is to be expressed by the letter e.
The letter e is together with wisdom;
the letter vam has the three families as its nature.
The hero who abides in the center of the navel,
the stamen, has the secret as his domain.
Joined to a wisdom like himself,
he is to be called Vajrasattva.
With two hands he is called Heruka.
He himself is the one who shakes the three worlds.
He himself is said to be the essential nature.
That buddha body is great bliss.
He himself becomes the vajra of wisdom.
He himself is coemergent joy,
and he himself has the nature of the congruent cause.
He is called the single-pointed vajra.
That too is the exhaustion of the fourth state,
supremely pure and unchanging.

And the one who abides in awakening,
who abides in the center of the lotus of the heart,
is declared a being by the letter hūm alone.
He is none other than Akṣobhya.
He is called the vajra of mind.
He is Viṣṇu, four-handed.
He is jvala jvala bhyo.
He is declared to be the Dharma body.
Abiding in the wheel of Dharma, holding the vajra,
he bears the manner of special joy.
He is also declared to be karmic maturation,
the exhausting of deep sleep.

One filled with the flavor of great wisdom
is to be called a great being.
He is truly expressed by the letter āḥ.
At the throat, lord of the enjoyment wheel,
he is to be expressed as Amitābha.
He is declared the vajra of speech,
the Tathāgata with the lotus in hand.
He is declared supreme joy,
to be expressed as the maker of persons.
Conventional and ultimate,
that too is the exhaustion of the dream state.
He becomes the complete enjoyment body;
buddhas teach the Dharma.
He is expressed as the maker of bliss,
three-faced, six-armed,
kiṭi kiṭi vajra.
He becomes the third Heruka.

One who always abides in samaya,
the one who abides is to be expressed:
the samaya is the hare-bearer, the seminal essence,
great bliss abiding at the forehead.
The being who abides well in the nectar
is fully declared by the letter oṁ.
He is called Vairocana.
He is none other than the vajra of body.
Brahmā, holder of the vajra of body,
is himself to be fully expressed.
Joy is the nature of Brahmā;
he is to be expressed as the form body.
He is declared to be the wheel in hand.
The body has the mark of emanation.
By reversal it too is stainless;
he himself is the exhaustion of waking.
He becomes piṭu vajra,
called eight-faced Heruka,
the great hero, four-footed,
fully endowed with sixteen arms.
Thus by the divisions of the body,
Heruka is declared to be fourfold.
From the essential nature, the Dharma body arises;
from the Dharma, the complete enjoyment body;
from that, the emanation body arises.
In this way there are four bodies.

Brahmā holds the vajra of body; the Great Lord, Īśvara, holds the vajra of speech; Viṣṇu holds the vajra of mind; and Vajrasattva is great bliss. Thus it is said.

In the Root Tantra it is explained at length: the families are to be spoken of as three; because they possess an essential nature, they are said to be four families. The classification of the families rests on the division of the four activities. One who wishes for the path of awakening, endowed with yoga, should know this.

The letters Evaṃ are Vajrasattva. The syllable Hūṃ is the vajra of mind. The syllable Āḥ is the vajra of speech. The syllable Oṃ is the vajra of body. As for Evaṃ, the meaning to be realized through the name of each of the letters Hūṃ, Āḥ, and Oṃ is not to be grasped by the letter or the name. Here there is no substantial reality of the name; for if there were, it would then be something grasped by the mind and the eye, and that is not so. Therefore the ear grasps only the name, whereas the meaning to be realized through it is what is grasped by the mind and the eye. The things meant by words such as pot and cloth, produced from clay, wheel, stick, water, thread, loom, place, yarn, and so forth, are not brought into being by the name. By the mere sound of a name, one does not produce them as clay, which is the cause of qualities such as smell and so on of earth and the rest. This is the Blessed One's definitive statement in reply.

Now Vajragarbha makes his request again, and the Blessed One gives his answer.

Vajragarbha asks:
By what, O Vajra of Kye, is this name in brief given?
What is explained by the syllable He?
And what is to be expressed by Vajra?

The Blessed One declared:
By the syllable He is meant great compassion;
and by Vajra, wisdom is to be expressed.
The tantra whose nature is wisdom and means,
that I shall explain; listen.

The extensive explanation of this brief teaching is in the Root Tantra, and one should enter into that very text. Thus the Blessed One declared:

Compassion without object, toward beings and phenomena, is explained as threefold.
From the word "great," it is without object.
By the syllable He it is expressed.
Through the union of body, speech, and mind,
it is that very syllable Vaṃ spoken of before.
By the word Vajra too is meant the syllable E,
the emptiness of meaning in all aspects.
Whoever enters into these in union
is spoken of by the word yogin.
The tantra whose nature is wisdom and means
is to be called a yoga tantra.
It is not by the word "means" that it is a yoga tantra,
nor does it become so by "wisdom";
this is called the yoga tantra,
the nondual Vajra of Kye.
All the tantras such as the Guhyasamāja
are yoga tantras by that nature of wisdom and means,
through the names of wisdom and means,
wherein means is well established
and the yoginīs engage in all conduct.
On the conventional level, as yoginī tantras,
I have taught them to the childish.
Where means is transferred,
and wisdom abides truly,
that is explained as the tantra of means;
as it is, so it is conventionally.
As for those who meditate on mudrās and maṇḍalas,
to whatever degree they analyze,
to that degree they are broken down,
for they are marked by arising and perishing.
The union of wisdom and means,
the three vajras marked by inseparability,
the knower and the knowable being one,
is emptiness and compassion nondual.
Emptiness, signlessness,
likewise wishlessness,
and the unconditioned liberation:
these four are proclaimed throughout.
Compassion, loving-kindness, joy,
and the fourth, equanimity:
these are the four abodes of Brahmā;
from the nonconceptuality of all phenomena,
by a phenomenon without obscuration,
it is designated as the nondual.
Wisdom and means are likewise one nature.
The vajra bodies are not two.
The six aggregates, likewise the elements,
the objects and the faculties too,
the faculties of action,
thus all abide as nondual.
The vows of all the buddhas
are well established in the letters Evaṃ.
Afterward, by the division into three families,
from the division into five families,
by the division into six families,
and likewise by the division into eight,
the tantra whose nature is wisdom and means,
I shall teach you; listen.

This is the Blessed One's definitive statement in reply, showing how it is established as a yoga tantra.

Now the cause of the arising and abiding of the yoginīs is to be stated. In this tantra, the yoginīs are Nairātmyā and the others, according to the division of the fifteen portions of the moon, being the five aggregates, the four elements, and the six sense sources. The cause of their arising and abiding is the syllable Hūṃ, which is the second Heruka. Therefore, as to the meaning of these: how do the equality wisdom, the consciousnesses, and the deities come to arise?

Likewise:
The glance, the summoning, and the great sign,
know the many kinds of powers;
the paralyzing and the expelling,
the paralyzing of a host, and the fierce rites,
and others besides, I shall explain.

"Listen, Vajragarbha of Kye": this is the definitive statement on the cause of the arising and abiding of the deities.

Now the cause of their further production is to be stated.

First, Evaṃ comes to be.

The letters Evaṃ are the first cause of Heruka; the syllable Hūṃ is the cause of the second production. Thus it is determined.

From that, things arise, through the division into apprehended and apprehender; through the union of object and faculty, the consciousnesses are each separate. One is bound by the bondage of things. By that bondage of things, the consciousnesses and the eye and the other faculties are bound.

By fully knowing that, one is freed. By halting object and faculty, through fully knowing them. Therefore, by things themselves, one liberates. By seeing past and future objects with the divine eye and the like, the six consciousnesses are freed; for the Blessed One said, "That is another consciousness." Likewise there are other forms and so forth. Therefore:

Everywhere hands and feet and the rest,
everywhere eyes, heads, and faces,
everywhere hearing, in the world
he dwells, having pervaded all.

Now the ultimate meditation is to be stated, through the wisdom and means explained before, beginning with the words "meditate on things."

Here, in space, the object of meditation is like a dream; that is, it lies beyond the nature of even the subtlest particle. Fully knowing this, one should meditate on the absence of any real thing: with a mind free of conceptual thought, one sees the reflection-like appearance. What is free of conceptuality and unmistaken is to be called direct perception. This itself is of four kinds: sense direct perception, mental direct perception, yogic direct perception, and self-aware direct perception. That very cognition is said to have the defining mark of experience, since it makes known, brings one into engagement, and brings attainment.

That reflection-like appearance is established by nine examples, namely: a mirage, an illusory city, an echo, a dream, an illusionist's magical contrivance, a hallucination, an image in a mirror, the moon in water, and space. Wisdom becomes the syllable E.

In the same way, Heruka also is to be meditated on as without real thing; this is the wisdom of insight. Heruka is great bliss. As wisdom is, so too is means. Fully knowing this, one should meditate on the absence of any real thing, and this becomes means. From the word "suchness" it is said that the result is just as the cause is. The letters E and VAṂ are the body that has the nature of wisdom and means, abiding, that is, dwelling in the navel.

Thus the great wisdom that abides in the body is accomplished through the abandoning of all conceptual thought. The wheel of the maṇḍala, the activities, and the wisdom seal are free of conceptuality, because the wisdom of the Perfection of Wisdom is free of the net of concepts; this is what is certain. This itself is Vajrasattva, Heruka who shakes all three worlds, the body of essential nature. "Pervading all things" means that this body of wisdom pervades these things: the six aggregates, the six elements, the six sense faculties, the six objects, the six faculties of action, and the six functions of the faculties of action. "Though dwelling in the body, it is not born of the body" means that it has no arising or perishing.

Here, there is a passage that will be explained in the chapter on settling accomplishment, in the second chapter of the Examination of the Ḍākinījālasaṃvara. There Vajragarbha makes his request, and the Blessed One's reply teaches, in a conventional way, the bliss of the two organs, as a means of generating understanding in ordinary beings. It runs thus. Vajragarbha asks:

Through the yoga of the completion stage,
the supreme bliss is great bliss.
Completion is without meditation;
what need then is there of generation?

Here "completion stage" means being free of conceptuality and of the bliss of the two organs. If that alone bestows buddhahood, then what need is there to generate a maṇḍala or a seal through conceptual thought? That is the meaning. Having heard these words, the Blessed One Hevajra declared:

This great bodhisattva
falls, alas, through the force of faith.

This is on the conventional level.

For whom is there bliss in a body without real thing?
Bliss cannot be spoken of.
By the nature of the pervaded and the pervader,
bliss is the pervader of beings.
Just as the scent that dwells in a flower
is not understood apart from the flower, a non-thing,
so, since form and the rest are non-things,
bliss will not become an object of apprehension.

This too is on the conventional level. Then, once more, he refutes it on the ultimate level, thus:

The thing, and the non-thing too...

This means wisdom in all its aspects.

...for realizing things, the buddha...

This means means, which has the six higher knowledges.

The deluded do not know me.

Whoever is thus, even if overcome by laziness, they are those utterly overcome by the bliss of the two organs. With these and similar words, the provisional and the definitive meaning, the earlier and the later, are explained at length in that same chapter.

Here, what was taught in the chapter on the lineages is to be explained. Because it pervades the beings of the three realms, those that move and those that do not, and the past, future, and present, then if the buddha's wisdom were born from the body, it would be through a relation of support and supported, as with the scent of a flower: just as without the flower there is no scent, so when the body is lacking, it would perish. But this is not so. Therefore, because it pervades both the outer and the body, it is called "dwelling in the outer" and "dwelling in the body"; but since when the body is lacking it does not cease to be, this is the certainty expressed by "not born of the body." So it has been said:

Though a vase may be broken,
there is no destruction of space.
So too, though the body be destroyed,
there is no destruction of wisdom.

Here ends the third section of the Extensive Commentary on Hevajra, in six thousand verses: the ascertainment of the wisdom that is nondual wisdom and means.

Now the channels in the vajra body are to be set forth. Here Vajragarbha asks: "Blessed One, how many channels are there in the vajra body?" The Blessed One declared: "The channels are thirty-two; they are the ones through which the thirty-two awakening minds descend and drip into the place of great bliss." Here, because it is that which fully experiences great bliss, it is to be called great bliss. The channels are the secret wheel's thirty-two, having the nature of the thirty consonants of the six groups beginning with the letter KA, together with the two characterized by being indestructible. Among these, space, wind, fire, water, earth, and the element of wisdom correspond to the nature of the letters E and VAṂ.

Thus: kā, khā, gā, ghā, ṅā are the elements of space. The letters a, ku, ha, and the visarga arise from the throat; this is known by the letter ha. What falls within them is to be taken by mentioning it, as established by the full explanation. The kā group is the element of space.

In the same way, cā, chā, jā, jhā, ñā: the letters i, cu, ya, śa arise from the palate. This is the element of wind, known by the letter i.

In the same way, ṭā, ṭhā, ḍā, ḍhā, ṇā: the letters ṛ, ṭu, ra, ṣa arise from the crown of the head. This is the element of fire, known by the letter ra.

In the same way, the syllables pā, phā, bā, bhā, mā, which are the u, pu, hya, va sounds of those who have lips, arise from the lips; they are the water element, and are known by the letter va. In the same way, the syllables tā, thā, dā, dhā, nā, which are the ḷ, tu, la, sa sounds, arise from the teeth; they are the earth element, and are known by the letter la. Among these, sā, vā, ṣā, śā, thkā is said to be the sixth, the nature of the five, namely earth, water, fire, wind, and space, the self of the five aspects, the element of wisdom.

The letters E and VAṂ are the two unchanging ones. Since the letters E, VAṂ and the others draw the awakening mind to themselves, they are taught as the ones that cause the awakening mind to descend. "Dissolving in the place of great bliss" means urine, feces, and the flowing of seminal fluid. In the midst of these, below and above, are the three channels, which are the principal ones. Below, they cause the feces, the urine, and the seminal fluid to flow; that is, below the navel. Above the navel are the rasanā (the right channel), the lalanā (the left channel), and the avadhūtī, called "the shaker."

This is as the verse says: The lalanā abides by the nature of wisdom; the rasanā abides rightly as method; the avadhūtī is in the central direction, free of grasped and grasper. Here, by the word "wisdom" the moon is meant, and its nature is the breath flowing in the left nostril. Therefore "by the nature of the moon" means that the lalanā is where the breath flows. By the word "method" the sun should be understood, and its nature is the breath flowing in the right nostril; by that nature the rasanā abides. As it was said: By day it is the Blessed Vajradhara; by night it is renowned as wisdom. Just as the sun is called peaceful, so the moon is explained as having breasts. The five maṇḍalas of consciousness, formations, feeling, perception, and form abide in the lalanā in the order of emanation. The five maṇḍalas of earth, water, fire, wind, and space abide in the rasanā in the order of withdrawal. Moving round through the twelve junctions of time, the lalanā and rasanā travel in the uneven and even junctions, and enjoy thirty maṇḍalas.

Here the maṇḍalas of the junctions beginning with Capricorn are as follows, in order: kā, khā, gā, ghā, ṅā. Then, reversing the direction, in the Aquarius junction the short letters arise in the order of emanation in the left channel: ṅa, gha, ga, kha, ka, which are space and so on, and consciousness and so on. In the same way, on the right too, there are form and so on, and earth and so on, through the connection of grasped and grasper according to the distinction of body and intention. Likewise, when the sun rises in Pisces, the syllables cā, chā, jā, jhā, ñā are in the right channel in the order of withdrawal. Then, when Aries rises, ña, jha, ja, cha, ca are in the left channel in the order of emanation. Likewise, when Taurus rises, ṭā, ṭhā, ḍā, ḍhā, ṇā. Likewise, when Gemini rises, ṇa, ḍha, ḍa, ṭha, ṭa. Then, when Cancer rises, pā, phā, bā, bhā, mā. Likewise, ma, bha, ba, pha, pa for Leo; tā, thā, dā, dhā, nā for Virgo; na, dha, da, tha, ta when Libra rises; sā, vā, ṣā, śā, thkā when Scorpio rises. Then tha, ka, śa, ṣa, va, sa, with the junction of Sagittarius arising. In this way there are sixty maṇḍalas of day and night.

Likewise, the maṇḍalas move in the inner channels. What is born from these, which make them known, should be spoken of in the joints of the hands and feet. In the joints of the right shoulder and upper arm is the junction of Capricorn, and the maṇḍalas that are its distinct selves, the maṇḍalas of earth and so on, are in the lower joints of the thumb, index finger, middle finger, ring finger, and little finger, in order. Then, for the junction of Aquarius, in the joints of the left shoulder and upper arm, the maṇḍalas of space and so on are, in order, at the lower joint junctions of the little finger, ring finger, middle finger, index finger, and thumb. Likewise, the junction of Pisces is in the joints near the right upper arm, and the maṇḍalas are at the junctions of the second joints of the thumb and so on. Likewise, the junction of Aries is at the junction near the left upper arm, and the maṇḍalas are at the junctions of the second rows of joints of the little finger and so on. Then the junction of Taurus is at the junction of the right hand and forearm, and the maṇḍalas are at the junctions of the third rows of joints of the thumb and so on. Likewise, the junction of Gemini is at the junction of the left hand and forearm, and the maṇḍalas are at the junctions of the third rows of joints of the little finger and so on. This is the determination of the course toward awakening, the northward movement.

Then, for the southward movement: the junction of Cancer is in the joints of the right waist and thigh, and the maṇḍalas of earth and so on are, in order, at the junctions of the first rows of joints of the toes of the right foot beginning with the big toe. Then the junction of Leo is at the junction of the left waist and thigh, and the maṇḍalas of space and so on are, in order, at the junctions of the first rows of joints of the left foot beginning with the little toe. Likewise, the junction of Virgo is at the junction of the right knee and thigh, and the maṇḍalas are at the junctions of the second rows of joints of the right foot beginning with the big toe. Likewise, the junction of Libra is at the junction of the left knee and thigh, and the maṇḍalas are at the junctions of the second rows of joints beginning with the little toe. Likewise, the junction of Scorpio is at the junction of the right foot and knee, and the maṇḍalas are at the junctions of the third rows of joints beginning with the big toe. Likewise, the junction of Sagittarius is at the junction of the left foot and knee, and the maṇḍalas are at the junctions of the third rows of joints beginning with the little toe, in order.

Thus the sixty maṇḍalas belong to the emanation wheel, and the four are the places of emptiness, the nature of the letters E and VAṂ. Therefore the emanation wheel is a lotus with sixty-four petals.

By the word "petal" are meant the channels that bring down the sixty maṇḍalas. "Four" refers to those that bring down emptiness. Thus the five abodes belong to the channels of each conjunction of time: in the center is the channel that brings down emptiness; in the east, the channel that brings down wind; on the right, the channel that brings down fire; in the north, the channel in which water flows; in the west, the one in which earth flows. In the center, Avadhūtī brings down the maṇḍala of wisdom. As it is said: "In the center of the navel the hero abides, in the realm of the secret filament." Thus the sixty tubes are what bring down the sixty maṇḍalas, and four are what bring down emptiness. Just as the sixteenth part of the moon is only an instant, so one should understand that it also brings down the fully complete maṇḍala. This is the wheel of emanation.

The conjunction of time that is unequal, having the nature of the moon, is the Lalanā. The conjunction of time that is equal, having the nature of the sun, is the Rasanā. Avadhūtī is the equal one, free of apprehended and apprehender, in the central direction; it is to be known in the maṇḍala of wisdom.

Thus in the wheel of emanation the shiftings of the tubes are sixty-four, four of which are emptiness. Then the wheel of Dharma has a lotus of eight petals, and on these eight petals there are eight shiftings, according to the division into watches. These have the nature of the conjunctions of time with their halves, belonging to the Lalanā and to the Rasanā.

Then the throat is the wheel of enjoyment, the Saṃbhogacakra. There, through the division of the short consonants, there are thirty-two channels. The enjoyment wheel is a lotus of thirty-two petals, according to the oral instruction of the guru; the tantra states it in reverse order. In it, by the division into moments, there are thirty-two shiftings. At the forehead is a lotus of sixteen petals, and in it, by the division into half-watches, there are sixteen shiftings. At the crown is a lotus of four petals, and in it, by the division into junctures, there are four shiftings.

Here, the breath moving outward: when the breath goes out, the maṇḍala of the tube-channels in the wheel of emanation comes to appear in the nostril. In the two nostrils, whether the left or the right, at the very end of the first, when the maṇḍala of space descends, then it moves in the center. When the maṇḍala of wind descends, then it touches the upper part of the nostril. When the maṇḍala of fire descends, then it touches the nostril on the right. When the maṇḍala of water descends, then it touches the left, though it may be on the left or the right, directed toward the nose. When the maṇḍala of earth moves, then it touches the lower part. Thus the yogin should know the five abodes in the nostrils, through the distinction of the unequal and equal conjunctions of time appearing on the left or the right.

Here, what was explained extensively in the Kye Dorje of five hundred thousand lines is not stated, because of its great length; those of little learning would be frightened by a lengthy exposition. This is the Blessed One's decision. In the household wheel, which has the nature of the twelve conjunctions of time, Makara and the rest are to be known as the four seats; they are also the marks of the signs of death. The tantra is to be understood through another tantra, as it is said. Rohita, Mohita and the others are the channels through which the unequal and equal conjunctions of time descend; this was spoken in another, intentional language. By these channels good and bad are taught, and also the signs of death and death itself.

For that reason: "The Lalanā brings down Akṣobhya; the Rasanā brings down blood most excellently; wisdom, the moon, brings down excrement; that is renowned as Avadhūtī." Here Akṣobhya is the water element; the water element flowing in the left nostril is the Lalanā. Blood is the fire element; the fire element descending on the right is the Rasanā. Thus on the left is the lotus born from water, and on the right the jewel born from fire, the supreme deity. Wisdom is Avadhūtī; the moon is the drop, the semen. That very thing, having the nature of the five, brings down great emptiness. Thus emptiness descends, and in the center is the supreme deity of the vajra family, renowned as Avadhūtī. Likewise the moon, the sun and Rāhu bring down, in due order, the Lalanā, the Rasanā and Avadhūtī, in the all-pervading life-wind.

As for the downward-clearing wind, it is the channels bringing down excrement, urine and semen, below the navel: the wheel, the sword, and the single-pointed vajra. It is said that there are the upper doors and the lower doors. Of these, the thirty-two channels of the lower doors, which bring down water, will be explained: Mi phyed ma and the others.

Here, in the bright fortnight there are the fifteen vowels, and the sixteenth is the drop. These channels abide encircling the hanging one. They are as follows: A, I, Ṛ, U, Ḷ are, in due order, the first, second, third, fourth and fifth lunar days. Likewise the guṇa vowels: Ar, E, Ar, O, Al, the sixth and so on, in due order. Then the vowels turned into semivowels, Ha, Ya, Ra, Va, La, are, in due order, the eleventh and so on. In the middle of the drop is the hanging one, the sixteenth. Thus above the palate, at the forehead, nectar having the nature of a portion of semen drips; outwardly it is cooling, and inwardly it is stuka.

Likewise the individual channels: "Mi phyed ma and Phra gzugs ma, Mchog ma, G.yon ma, Thung ngu ma", these five being the first day and so on. Then: "Rus sbal skyes sgom, Dbang bskur ma, Skyon ldan ma and Bshang ba ma", these being the sixth and so on. "Mtshan mo ma, Ma mo, Bsil sbyin ma, Drod ma and Lalanā", these being the eleventh and so on, in due order. The Lalanā has the nature of the fifteen; Avadhūtī belongs to the sixteen.

Then the first day of the dark fortnight and so on belong to the Rasanā, in the order of gathering the fifteen days, by the nature of the long vowels. They are as follows: Lā, Vā, Rā, Yā, Hā are the first, second, third, fourth and fifth, in due order. Then, by the division of increasing guṇas, Āl, Au, Ār, Ai, Ā are the sixth and so on, in due order. Then, by the division of the long vowels, Lī, Ū, Rī, Ī, Ā are the eleventh and so on, in due order. At the tip of the tongue is the sixteenth, the distinct one.

"In the midst of these" refers to Romā and the others. They are Romā, Jalmā, Varṇasthā, Spyimā, and Rgyusbyinmā, that is, the first and the following lunar days. Then Saṃyogavirahā, Sdugumā, Siddhā, Pācikā, and Manobhadrā, that is, the sixth and the following. Then Trisaṅkarā, Icchā, Gṛhā, Caṇḍī, and Kātyāyanī, that is, the eleventh and the following. In this way there are fifteen.

In the center of the tongue is the conqueror of Māra, in the form of a black thread, standing as far as the tip of the tongue, hanging down, dripping, short, and tasting flavor.

Again Vajragarbha made his request: "Blessed One, what are these channels like?" The Blessed One declared:

"All that has become the three realms of existence..."

This means the aggregates, the faculties, and the faculties of action, all that has become so. "...abandoning grasped and grasper" means that one enters fully into the central channel. Or again: "By every means, they are imagined as the defining marks of things." This means that the elements, the objects, and the activities are imagined as the defining marks of the things that are consciousnesses.

This is the fourth chapter, on determining the channels in the vajra body, the complete analysis, in the six-thousand-verse commentary on the Hevajra.

Now I shall explain the divisions of the compendium. Here, whatever has been taught in accord with the system and in reversal of the system should be clarified by the commentary in due order.

Here, "the compendium of all the buddhas is established in the letters E and VAṂ." It is in the secret lotus of thirty-two petals and in the four-petaled lotus at the crown. The rest are gathered together into the wheels of emanation, of truth, of enjoyment, and of the innate. These are the lotuses of sixty-four petals, of eight petals, of thirty-two petals, and of sixteen petals, at the navel, the heart, the throat, and the forehead. At the forehead is Āli. At the throat is Kāli. At the heart is wisdom. At the navel is method.

The innate body is at the navel. The truth body is at the heart. The enjoyment body is at the throat. The emanation body is at the forehead.

The fourth state is at the navel. Deep sleep is at the heart. Dream is at the throat. Waking is at the forehead.

The vajra of body is at the forehead. The vajra of speech is at the throat. The vajra of mind is at the heart. The vajra of wisdom is at the navel.

The letter E is at the navel. The letter VAṂ is at the heart. The letter MA is at the throat. The letter YĀ is at the forehead.

Locanā is at the navel. Māmakī is at the heart. Pāṇḍarā is at the throat. Tārā is at the forehead. These are the four maṇḍalas.

As to the four moments: the variegated is at the forehead. The ripening is at the throat. The vanishing is at the heart. The one without characteristics is at the navel.

Likewise for the joys: joy is at the forehead, supreme joy at the throat, joy of cessation at the heart, and innate joy at the navel.

Likewise for the truths: the truth of suffering is at the forehead, the truth of origin at the throat, the truth of cessation at the heart, and the truth of the path at the navel.

As to the four realities: the reality of self is at the forehead, the reality of mantra at the throat, the reality of the deity at the heart, and the reality of wisdom at the navel.

At the forehead is the making of "I." At the throat is the uttering of sound. At the heart is the thinking of everything. At the navel is the experiencing of bliss.

As to the four schools: the Sthaviras are at the navel. The Sarvāstivādins are at the heart. The Sammatīyas, honored by all, are at the throat. The Mahāsāṃghikas are at the forehead.

The moon is at the forehead. The sun is at the throat. Rāhu is at the heart. The fire of time is at the navel.

At the forehead are the sixteen transitions. At the throat are the thirty-two. At the heart are the eight. At the navel are the sixty-four transitions.

The four junctures explained earlier are these: the morning juncture is at the forehead, the midday juncture at the throat, the evening juncture at the heart, and the midnight juncture at the navel.

At the forehead is enjoyment. At the throat is empowering. At the heart is dissolution. At the navel is lordship itself. Thus everything is fourfold.

The thirty-six factors of the side of awakening and the thirty-six elements are to be divided into the fourfold division explained earlier. All of them, by fours, should be applied to the four wheels according to their number and the divisions of the family.

Following the root tantra, as it is taught in the manner of other tantras, the compendium is made into six wheels by the division into six families, into five wheels by the division into five families, into four wheels by the division into four families, and into three wheels by the division into three families. This is the determination.

"The tantras of three families and of four families, likewise of five families and six families, arise from the division into pervaded and pervader, and from the distinction between the supported and the support."

Here, by the division of the support into one family, the supported has one face. By the division of the support into three families, it has three faces. By four families, four faces. By five families, five faces. By six families, six faces. By eight families, eight faces.

Here, by the division of the fire of time, it is explained as having one face. By the division of Rāhu, it has two faces, through the nature of wisdom and method. By the division of body, speech, and mind, or of moon, sun, and Rāhu, it has three faces. By the division of the moon, the sun, Rāhu, and the fire of time, or of body, speech, mind, and wisdom, it has four faces. By the division of the five aggregates, it has five faces. By the division of the six aggregates, it has six faces. By the division of the moon in two aspects, the sun in two, Rāhu in two, and the fire of time in two aspects, these eight, it has eight faces.

Here, the division of the moon is the wheel of the forehead and of the hanging channel. The division of the sun is the wheel of the tongue and the throat. The division of Rāhu is the wheel of the heart and the crown. The division of the fire of time is the wheel of the navel and the secret place.

As it is said: "Above Rāhu the sun abides; above the sun, the moon."

Above the moon is Rāhu, and by him the manifold is set in motion. By him, the one who sets it in motion, is the fire of time. It abides in two aspects, in the navel and in the secret place. This itself is called the vajra sun, where the fierce fire (caṇḍālī) dwells. Where the true nectar abides, there abides the syllable haṃ. At the crown, in the lotus, the rain descends: the vajra moon, of stainless light. This concludes the determination of the divisions of the summary.

Now the yoga of the fierce fire is to be explained, beginning with the words "the fierce fire." The words are these:

The fierce fire blazing in the navel burns the five Tathāgatas. It burns the eyes and the rest completely. When all is burned, haṃ, the one bearing the hare, drips down.

This is the verse of brief indication. For the extended explanation of this brief indication, one should enter the root tantra. There the Blessed One declared:

More secret even than the secret, listen to the explanation of the vajra heart. It is the tantra of all accomplishments; it bestows liberation on the yogin. What Vajrasattva has declared is the body of wisdom, free of dualistic thought. The Protector in the center of the three channels abides within the navel, in the secret place. From there, below and above, it extends, with a radiance like a rod of lightning. It awakens the consciousness, the faculties and the elements that dwell in the body.

That Lady of the Earth abides in the navel, as the central peak in the middle. In the secret place the right peak also, descending, is like lightning. It is called wisdom of the same kind; here it is explained to be the fierce fire. It has three peaks pointing downward, and likewise three pointing upward. The peak of Brahmā abides in the middle, the peak of Viṣṇu on the left, and on the right the peak of the Fierce One (Rudra). Through these flow feces, urine and semen.

The peak of Rāhu abides in the middle, the peak of the moon on the left, and on the right the peak of the sun. Through these flow water, fire and space.

The rkyang ma (lalanā) is rightly taught on the left, and on the right it is called the ro ma (rasanā). In the middle is the Avadhūtī. That is to be called the Yungmo.

From the navel to the center of the heart, it makes three turns and then proceeds. From the abode of the heart to the center of the throat, it makes three turns and again extends. From the forehead and the throat, in the center, it makes three turns and again proceeds. From the forehead to the center of the crown, it makes three turns and again proceeds. From the crown to the nose, it makes three turns and again proceeds. In the left nostril the rkyang ma abides, rightly established. On the right the channel is the ro ma. Between the eyebrows is the Avadhūtī. The five wheels, the navel and the rest, have three paths each, in all fifteen. From the navel the Avadhūtī goes and comes through the center of the navel. From petal to petal the rkyang ma and ro ma are taught.

In the navel is Rāhu, and from him the moon, marked with the moon, pierces through and reaches the crown. That is the Avadhūtī channel. When a man is intoxicated with desire, through the union of the rkyang ma and the ro ma, the fierce fire is made certainly to descend. It blazes in the navel wheel and surely issues from the path of the Yungmo. Rāhu, marked with the moon, pierces and touches the syllable ha. By that touch the syllable haṃ then melts into white nectar. It descends through the five maṇḍalas, burning the aggregates, elements and the rest. When objects and faculties cease, joy and the other bliss-states arise, and from the moon the drop (bindu) drips. The syllable haṃ abides rightly at the crown. The forehead is the moon, the throat the sun. Then it goes to Rāhu. When the fierce fire has entered the navel, it then proceeds to the wheel of the secret place.

In this wheel of the secret place this falls. This is explained as the great "cattle." Below, by the three peaks and the channels, it is shown to be in a hollow place. The seventh birth, the cattle themselves, bestows the accomplishment of moving in the sky. The Victor seizes diseases. By melting the aggregates and elements, it bestows even the status of a wheel-turning ruler. Having slain the faculties and objects, it bestows precisely sky-going. It possesses qualities such as subtlety. Through the subsequent melting, awakening and the rest occur. It swiftly bestows buddhahood. Just as outwardly mercury is bound, so too this essence of wisdom brings about the great accomplishment that surely destroys all suffering.

In the same way, Brahmā, Viṣṇu and the cattle fall surely into the wheel of the secret place. For as long as it is burned seven times, it grants a broad life in this very life. Above, Rāhu is called the cattle. The moon and the sun are the great cattle. Having fallen into the hole of Brahmā, they give the fruits of enjoyment and liberation. Blood and sinews likewise, by descending in the rkyang ma, are made to fall. These too are called cattle. Other external objects are not cattle. The seven births, the cattle, are altogether the supreme sovereigns.

Listen as I explain how the seven births are in the body. All the flavors of food and drink, the six kinds of eating and drinking, become essence through ripening. That is the first birth of each. Then it becomes blood; in the second it becomes clear. In the third it becomes flesh. In the fourth it goes into skin. In the fifth it becomes channel. In the sixth it becomes sinew. In the seventh it becomes bone. This becomes so in seven turns.

Through the application that relies on this very process, the six treasuries are to be called the family. The family is explained to be the body. What arises from the family is called semen. By that, potency is called the elemental particle. The body is the cause of generating. Consciousness, the pervader, awareness, abides in the middle of these. Relying on the moon and the sun, there is Heruka, whose nature is the three existences. The heroes who abide above and below are the chiefs of the wheel of the ḍākinīs.

By four kinds of channels, the wheel at the crown is to be described. At the forehead there are sixteen; at the throat, thirty-two; at the heart, eight wheel-spokes; and sixty-four in the navel wheel, rightly explained. In the secret place there are thirty-two. The channels are explained as the spokes of the wheel, or else the petals of a lotus. The yoginīs rely on these and perform many kinds of activity. By the nature of object and faculty they stir consciousness. The abodes, the near abodes and all the rest are the petals of the channels. In the crown of the four goddesses it is to be called the great illusion. This appears in the first maṇḍala of the navel wheel. At the forehead the vajra drop will be endowed with eight channels. There are the two eyes, likewise the ears, the nose, the tongue, and the hanging parts.

The goddesses beginning with Gaurī, the forms and the rest, the earth and the rest, divided by the portions of the moon, are the selfless ones. These are declared here in this very place. Three faces, six arms, at the throat: these, with the thirty-two, and at the heart as well the eight goddesses, and this four-armed one, dwell perfectly. Viṣṇu the great, as the vajra of mind, arises from the family of Akṣobhya. At the navel, which agitates the three worlds, with sixty-four, these are taught as the five ḍākas. The ḍākinīs and the five elements, these abide in the five wheels. The sixth is at the secret place, the great lord, together with the thirty-two ḍākinīs, the sovereign of the three realms.

The goddesses in the six wheels number one hundred and fifty-six. This is called the Wheel of Time, the six classes of beings gathered into one.

The channels at the forehead and the crown, which are twenty, cause phlegm to flow down. The forty at the throat and heart likewise cause bile to flow down. The sixteen at the navel and the secret place, and the eighty, cause wind to flow down. Those whose nature is aggregation and the rest are the ten channels of the secret wheel. Whatever three channels there are above and below, they obstruct the six wheels. The subsidiary channels are many, and from them the body is sustained wherever it abides. Those number seventy-two thousand, and in the body there are just as many goddesses. The elemental beings are as many as the pores of the hair, three and a half crores, and those established as many as the hairs abide there.

As the outer, so the body. Six breaths make one pala. Sixty palas make one ghaṭikā. A ghaṭikā-measure of moments, less four by four, makes one half-watch. Five of them make a season-junction. Two halves of a season-junction make a watch. Two watches make one period of the day. Four of these are declared the day and night of embodied beings. And with twelve such junctions, by six hundred breaths beyond twenty-one thousand, equal to the sun's staff, there are twenty-four half-months, and likewise twelve months, and the six seasons are taught as they are. By the four equal junctions of the time of union, by the three times, by the two passages, thus the day and night come to be in the body.

Nine hundred breaths make a half-month. By eighteen hundred months, and by thirty-six, the seasons are taught. By five thousand four hundred, the time of union; by seven thousand two hundred, the time of the three; by ten thousand beyond eight hundred passages, and likewise by two, one year is to be spoken.

At transference, the breath in the central channel is fifty-six, with the quarter, exceeded by seventy-five, six hundreds, for twelve. By the breaths of those hundred years, three directions and three years, it becomes the time of the avadhūtī, which bestows death on living beings. In the rasanā, if the wind descends by day and night, then one dies in three years, from the signs of the moon's death. When the life-breath of embodied beings descends in the sun for five days, then one dies in three years, from the sign of the death of the right, the sun.

When the life-breath moves in the left channel for three days, it casts off the petals of the house at the navel, and the passage is exhausted from three years on. For the second, six days likewise; it surely descends in three months. Thus by three months and three months, from the increase of three days each, these eleven petals are cast off in order over three years. As long as thirty-three days, it descends from the left without interruption. By the distinction of the three qualities, it then enters the sun. Two days, and then it enters the center, the navel. Life-breath for one day, and then from death the breaths are cast off.

When the wind descends in the right channel for ten days, it is at the second petal of the house; then one dies in two years. At the third petal of the house, at the lunar day, one dies in one year. At the fourth, descending for twenty days, one dies in six months. At the fifth, if it descends for twenty-five, life is three months. At the sixth, for twenty-six days, life is two months. At the seventh, for twenty-seven days, life is one month. If it descends without interruption from this for thirty-three days, then the eighth, ninth, tenth and eleventh petals in order, with the five lunar fortnights and the qualities, are cast off in a single day. Then, having abandoned the right channel, the wind goes to the left for two days; then for one day it goes to the central place. Thus, having exhausted the breaths, this is the transference from death.

Below, the semen is exhausted; the mother's blood rises upward. Bound by its own habitual tendencies, consciousness together with the wind arises again after death. Without that, there is no later life.

From a teacher, knowledge; from a lamp, a lamp; from a seal, another seal; from a face, a face in the mirror; from a sound, an echo arises; as fire from a crystal lens, which does not arise without the sun; as the tongue waters from eating something sour, which is not sour and does not arise without sourness; as the sprout from the seed, without that it does not arise. The result has the nature of the cause. It is not from itself, not from another, not from both, not without a cause. Whatever things there are, none whatever arises.

The white and red of father and mother, the life-wind and the downward-clearing wind likewise, together with the vajra of mind, are the cause that produces the body. Amitābha and Ratnasambhava are explained, the life-wind and the downward-clearing wind in order. Body, speech, mind and attachment are the gates of the one with the vajra of mind.

From semen, the half-moon; from blood, the sun arises. From the life-wind Rāhu is established; the fire of time is from the downward-clearing wind. From semen the channels are born; bone is born of semen. From the mother's blood, blood arises; from blood, flesh is born everywhere; from flesh, skin is born; from bone the legs arise.

Semen is the vajra, the dust is the bell; the channel is the lotus, the great jewel; blood is the wheel, and so is bone; flesh is likewise taught as the sword. The utpala and the white lotus, the skin and the legs of embodied beings: these eight, by their own mind, are in order the faces.

The eastern faces become the legs. The moon and the sun are without darkness. From blood arises the nature of clay, the earth; it is to be declared the element of flesh. From semen arises hardness, the earth, whose nature is stone and bone. From the moon the water is accomplished. Fire is accomplished from blood. From Rāhu the life-force is accomplished. The downward-clearing wind is said to come from the fire of time.

The tongue of all embodied beings, together with the uvula and the semen, is generated. The left and right eyes are produced by the dust. The two openings of the nose are likewise produced by the life-force. Produced from emptiness alone, in the same way, are the two openings of the ears. What is produced by the downward-clearing wind is the lower three channels in the same way. What Rāhu in turn produces are the two openings of the breasts. By the downward wind, from the intestines, comes the pair of testicles. In this way the elements of the body come to be. By semen alone the three elements arise; by dust too there are here three kinds. This is what is called the sixfold treasury.

The body and the body's faculty, these two: the left is produced by water, and the right is produced by fire. By the life-force itself it becomes nectar. Above that, three channels are taught. The left is produced by wind; the central one is the lower part of the earth. The right channel is produced by the downward-clearing wind; here there is no doubt.

At the forehead is the disc of the moon, where the five perfect awakenings abide as the body. In the region of the throat is the vajra of speech, in the full disc of the sun. In the middle of the disc of Rāhu is the consciousness of all embodied beings. In the tail of Rāhu, in the earth, the precious one abides well, without doubt. In the disc of the fire of time, Amoghasiddhi abides above the crossed vajra.

Wheel, lotus, and vajra, jewel and sword make the fifth; the vajra of the man, the jewel, and the buddha at the crown are taught as the sixth. Thus these six families of all the buddhas are of great power.

Smell is to be produced by earth. Taste comes about through ripening by fire. From water come sprouts and the like. Touch comes from wind. From the life-wind sound arises. From the downward-clearing wind comes the element of phenomena, in that very faculty of mind. All embodied beings are to know this.

Yamāntaka is produced from bone. From blood, Prajñāntaka is taught. Padmāntaka comes from the channels. From flesh, Vighnāntaka is taught. From the legs, Acala is declared. Ṭakkirāja arises from the skin, Nīladaṇḍa from the body hair, and Mahābala from the nails. From the hair comes Uṣṇīṣacakravartin. From the lower hair comes Sumbharāja.

The joints of the hands and feet are the houses, the four beginning with earth. The divisions of the three qualities are taught as twelve. The joints of the fingers are the seasons and the sixty days and nights. The constellations are those that abide in embodied beings, above and below. Dhruva, by the semen, reached the crown; the dust of the Seven Sages reached below. Just as it is outside, so in the body it is surely born from the womb.

The truth of meaning has twelve aspects. Heruka has twelve arms, through blocking the twelve limbs of dependent origination. According to the stages he has four faces. The sixteen arms are the portions of the moon; know that these are likewise of sixteen aspects. By blocking the eight openings he is the supreme lord with eight faces.
