When a child is born, the mothers give the empowerment of bathing with water. Placing a lock of hair on his crown is imputed as the diadem. Piercing his ears and the like is taught as the empowerment of the pennant. The child's speech and laughter are the empowerment of bell and vajra. The fifth, enjoyment, is the observance of the vow. A name is the empowerment of the name. Reading and the like are the permission, which the fathers always give. Thus in seven ways the seven empowerments are renowned. The one who takes the bride is said to be the higher empowerment. Her emptiness of wisdom is said to be the taking of his hand. The union of wisdom and means becomes bliss from dying. The generation of the body becomes approach, and the arising of the life-breath is accomplishment. The twice-born and others being born again is called near-accomplishment. Through the union of wisdom and means, the great accomplishment is supreme.

What is called birth becomes the generation stage; death should be called the gathering in. The aggregates, the junctures, and transference are absent. Ignorance has the nature of transference. The formations and all the rest likewise come to expand and to be gathered in. This is in the root tantra.

Here the Blessed One has said: the chapter on the rite of empowering the deity will be explained.

From the seed syllable in one's own heart, rays of light are sent forth, black and blue, in the form of hooks. By these one summons the buddhas dwelling in the three realms, makes offerings to them together with the eight mothers, and makes the request. This is done with oṃ abhiṣiñcantu māṃ sarvatathāgatāḥ.

Then those buddhas, in the form of Herukas, consecrate one with vases of nectar, whose nature is the five tathāgatas. While the empowerment is being given, a rain of flowers falls from the sky. The gods sound great drums. A rain of saffron falls. Rūpavajrā and the others make offerings. Locanā and the others sing vajra songs. At the diadem of the empowerment one becomes lord of the family. Through this, Heruka is fully accomplished.

Then the blessing mantra of exhortation and the rest should be spoken: oṃ sarvatathāgatakāyavākcittavajrasvabhāvātmako 'haṃ. Having thus meditated, in three sessions, on the blessed state, one rises, and at all times one should remain in the form of the deity.

In the Commentary of Six Thousand on the Hevajra, the chapter teaching the empowerment of the deity is complete; this is the ninth section. The chapter on the rite of empowering the deity is finished.

Without beginning or end, at peace, the lord pervading what exists and what does not, emptiness and compassion inseparable, this is taught as the awakening mind. Virtuous in the beginning, the middle and the end, to this very jewel of great bliss all things pay homage. The commentary on the fifth chapter, showing this very reality for the benefit of all sentient beings, and for attaining the fruit of the great seal, will be written by Vajragarbha.

Here the Blessed One has said: then from this the chapter on reality will be explained.

Let me now make clear what is meant by "intrinsic nature" and the rest. The intrinsic nature is that which knows itself, the wisdom of the Tathāgatas. Because this intrinsic nature grasps, and because all that is grasped and all that grasps are free of the transformations of the eighteen constituents, for yogins there is no external form, no seer, no eye, and no eye-consciousness. In the same way there is no hearer, no sound, and no ear-consciousness. Likewise there is no smell, no one who smells, and no nose-consciousness. Likewise there is no taste, no one who tastes, and no tongue-consciousness. Likewise there is no tangible object, no one who touches, and no body-consciousness. Likewise there is no constituent of phenomena, no one who apprehends, and no mind-consciousness. Thus the eye and the rest also do not exist. The yogin should meditate on this jewel-like reality, free of the eighteen constituents, because ignorance and the rest are made to cease.

The Blessed One declared in the root tantra:

By the cessation of changing ignorance,
the formations certainly cease.
By the certain cessation of the formations,
consciousness ceases.
By the cessation of consciousness,
name and form cease.
By the cessation of name and form,
the six sense sources cease.
By the cessation of the six sense sources,
contact too ceases.
By the cessation of contact,
feeling certainly ceases.
By the certain cessation of feeling,
craving too ceases.
By the certain cessation of craving,
grasping ceases.
By the cessation of grasping,
becoming too ceases.
By the cessation of becoming,
birth certainly ceases.
By the cessation of birth,
old age and death certainly cease.
When the aggregates and constituents have certainly ceased,
that very state is taught to be complete peace.
Thus, by the cessation of the body,
the vital wind too ceases.
By the certain cessation of the vital wind,
the awakening mind ceases.
By the cessation of the awakening mind,
emission certainly ceases.
By the cessation of the instant of emission,
all yogins become buddhas.
In the same way the afflictions cease,
and the karmas too cease.
By the cessation of the karmas,
sufferings too cease.
By the cessation of sufferings,
affliction will no longer arise.
When there is no arising of affliction,
karma itself does not arise.
When these do not arise,
sufferings too do not occur.
The yogin, free of suffering and endowed with bliss,
always accomplishes the aims of beings.
Beings conceptualize things
as pleasant and unpleasant;
from that the afflictions arise,
and from that karma arises.
From karma suffering arises.
Without bliss, then, from that again,
having wholly abandoned the ten virtues,
from what could bliss come to be?
From a mind of suffering, without bliss,
from where could the accumulation of merit come?
The childish yogin who fabricates
deities, mantras, mudrās and the like,
without merit, in whom could
the accumulation of wisdom truly arise?
Without these there is no buddhahood,
and no supreme Vajrasattva.
Without that, neither one's own aim
nor the aim of others truly comes about.
The pure Dharma body and the complete enjoyment body
emanate those emanation bodies.
Waking, dream, deep sleep,
and the fourth state, these four states:
when cause and effect cease,
then there is no doubt of buddhahood.
Free of dust, free of stain, without blemish,
having abandoned faults, without defect,
fully awakened, the thoroughly awakened self,
omniscient, all-knowing, the supreme one:
from the cessation of cause and effect
comes the body of the lord of the Victors, which is other.
The body, speech, mind and bliss too are other,
the collection of the aggregates is also other,
the elements such as earth are other,
and so too the objects and the faculties.
The activity of the faculties of action is other,
and likewise the faculties of action themselves.
Everywhere hands and feet and the rest,
everywhere eyes, heads and faces,
everywhere with hearing, in the world
he abides, having pervaded all.

This is the certainty about reality in the root tantra.

Now, for the sake of accomplishing this, here is what is taught conventionally in the Short Mudrā Tantra:

The mother who gives birth, the sister,
the daughter, and the sister's daughter,
by the rite of wisdom and means,
the yogin who knows should always worship.

"Yogin" means that the minds of the apprehended and the apprehender are one, that the rasanā and the lalanā are one, and that the caṇḍālī and the seminal fluid are one. From that, one is endowed with the mastery of sure accomplishment. One should worship through the union of wisdom and means, the unchanging bliss of chaste conduct. Externally, in terms of country and family usage, this refers to women such as the mother and the rest.

Likewise:

The dancer, the Cauṇḍālī, the Yavanī,
the Caṇḍālī, and likewise the brahmin woman.

"The yogin who knows" refers to the assembly explained earlier, and:

By the rite of wisdom and means,
the one who knows that very reality should always worship.

One should rely on the rite with a bliss that does not change at any time. It is that it does not come apart. "Coming apart" means, in the definitive sense, the emission of the seminal fluid; in the provisional sense, it is being reviled by people outside. Therefore the yogin should earnestly rely on the mother and the rest. This is the Blessed One's certain declaration. When one relies on them openly, then royal families and others inflict suffering on childish yogins. This is what is meant.

"The mudrās of the five families" means: "One should speak of them as the cause of liberation." Here the mudrā that is the cause of liberation belongs to the families of the five Tathāgatas. "With the vajra, that is, with this awakening mind, one should seal." This is the mudrā, Avadhūtī.

Here, although the mudrās of the mother and the rest are taught in a jumbled order beginning with "vajra," they should be taken in their proper sequence. The mother belongs to the Tathāgata family; she is the brahmin woman and Locanā, at the lotus of the navel. The sister is Māmakī, at the heart, belonging to the vajra family, the Yavanī. The daughter belongs to the fire element and the lotus family, the dancer, at the throat. The sister's daughter belongs to the wind element and the action family, the Cauṇḍālī, at the forehead. The wife is the sovereign lady of the vajra realm, the space element, the Caṇḍālī, belonging to the jewel family, at the crown of the head.

The Mahāmudrā consort born at the end is the element of wisdom and belongs to the family of Vajrasattva; she is at the secret lotus. In this way, upward from the navel, these are the five families of the seals. "Briefly, this is to be stated. The families are declared in six kinds." That is to say: at the crown, the family of the space element; at the forehead, the family of the wind element; at the throat, the family of the fire element; at the heart, the family of the water element; at the navel, the family of the earth element; and at the secret place, the family of the element of wisdom.

Thus the principal quality of space is the element of phenomena. The principal quality of wind is the tangible. The principal quality of fire is taste. The principal quality of water is form. The principal quality of earth is smell. The principal quality of the element of wisdom is the element of sound.

In the same way, there are the faculty of the ear, the faculty of the nose, the faculty of the eye, the faculty of the tongue, the faculty of the body, and the faculty of the mind. Likewise there are the faculty of the bhaga, the faculty of speech, the faculty of the hands, the faculty of the feet, the faculty of the anus, and the supreme faculty. In this way the seals are purified: the discharge of feces, the discharge of urine, walking, taking, speaking, and the discharge of semen. Thus the families of the thirty-six come to six families. In this way the thirty-six elements are to be understood.

Likewise there are the six consciousnesses, the six formations, the six feelings, the six perceptions, the six forms, and the six wisdoms. Thus the aggregates and elements come to seventy-two. These, too, are the deities taught in the Dākinījālasaṃvara in its doubled form: the thirty-six maṇḍalas, and the thirty-seventh, Heruka together with Vajravārāhī. Their families are three: the wheels of body, speech, and mind are Saṃvara, and the doubled form is the Saṃvara of the six wheels. In this tantra of Hevajra, the Blessed One made this clear through the two words "wheel" and "saṃvara", as it is taught: "Then the mantrin makes the doubled offering; the net of ḍākinīs is Saṃvara."

The petals, the fields, and so on are doubled. These are the four places and the four near places; the four fields and the four near fields; the four chandohas and the four near chandohas; the four gatherings and the four near gatherings; the four upper-and-lower rendings and the four near rendings; the four charnel grounds and the four near charnel grounds. Thus there are twelve grounds. For each of these there are also four heroic mistresses together with the hero. In this way the six wheels have forty-eight yoginīs. In the wheel of space there are eight vajra ḍākinīs; in the wheel of wind, eight sword ḍākinīs; in the wheel of fire, eight jewel ḍākinīs; in the wheel of water, eight lotus ḍākinīs; in the wheel of earth, eight wheel ḍākinīs; and in the wheel of wisdom, eight curved-knife ḍākinīs. In the vajra jewel there are eight ḍākinīs of emptiness, who hold the single-pointed vajra. The others are the sixteen ḍākinīs born from the sixteen joys, in the sixteen charnel grounds: two at the two ears, two at the nostrils, two at the eyes, two at the tongue and uvula, two at the openings of the crown, two at the door of the anal passage, two at the door of urine, and two at the door of the transference of semen. These are the sixteen yoginīs.

In the midst of them is the chief, the Blessed One Hevajra with twenty-four arms, embraced by the innate joy that is emptiness, as in the doubled Cakrasaṃvara. He is present distinguished by the divisions of time. The year, purified, is the blue body. Passage, purified, gives the white and red feet. The three times, purified, give the black, red, and white throat. The four months beginning with Mṛgaśiras make one season. The four seasons, each made of the three months beginning with Makara, being purified, give the four faces: black, red, white, and yellow. Likewise the six seasons, each made of two months beginning with Mṛgaśiras, being purified, give the six shoulders: blue, red, and white in nature, on the right and on the other side. Likewise the twelve months, each of a nature of four, give the twelve upper arms, black, red, and white, right and left. Likewise by the division of the fortnights there are the twenty-four arms. By the division of the individual lunar days, there are the veins of the joints of the left and right hands, the sixty-odd joints of the fingers, three hundred and sixty in all, according to the division of the days of the year.

Thus Heruka, whose nature is the six families, is the Wheel of Time. He is also to be called the primordial buddha, suchness. His hundred faces, the garland of heads, are the hundred limbs of the Veda, and he is the Blessed One, the great Vajra Skull-bearer, who cut off the head of Brahmā.

The doubled Net of Ḍākinīs, the Saṃvara, has the nature of the six families. In other tantras, it has the nature of the five families. Therefore: "The families are taught in six kinds; in brief, there are five kinds. By the division of body, speech, and mind, they later become three."

By this order of explanation: "Its nature is the families, the five elements, and the five aggregates. By this, the one who counts the Veda should declare it to be called 'family'." This is the determination of the six families, the five families, and the three families.

Now the definition of the Tathāgata is to be stated: "The glorious one gone to suchness, come to suchness itself; by this possessing wisdom, the Tathāgata is to be named." Here wisdom is emptiness, and its possessor is that which is together with the form of emptiness. "To suchness" means the following: in the secret lotus, at the ground of nirvāṇa that does not abide in the final limit of the vajra jewel, the glorious awakening mind, the vajra, has come to dwell in the bhagas of the vajra consorts, whose natures are wind, earth, water, fire, and wisdom, in the lotuses of the forehead, throat, heart, navel, and secret place, from the space element at the crown.

"In the same way he also came." Just as he went below in the descent, so too he came forth from the vajra jewel, from the secret lotus, from the realm of wisdom: that is, by way of the seminal essence rising upward to the navel, the heart, the throat, the forehead, and the crown of the head. In this way, as he went, so he came, and this is established. "The cessation of the twelve limbs" means the attainment of the twelve grounds.

Just as, by way of the worldly, conventional joy, supreme joy, special joy, and innate joy, the essence descended from the forehead downward, so by reversing this, the consequences that accord with the cause, the karmic maturation, the effect of the person's act, and the stainless are made to travel from the secret place up to the forehead. As it was also said:

At the navel, the outflow in accord with the cause is proclaimed.
At the wheel of Dharma, the karmic maturation.
At the enjoyment wheel, the effect of the person's act.
At the crown, the great bliss that is stainless.

This is the definitive meaning, and it will be explained later.

Thus, just as on the first day of the lunar month and so on, through the division into fifteen parts, the mind of wisdom conventionally moves downward from the crown to the secret lotus, with the essence going down in the nature of the full set of parts; so also, by the power of yoga, it is reversed from the secret lotus of the vajra jewel. By the division into parts, the first day and so on, the essence goes upward on the foundation of the nature of the full parts and reaches the lotus of the forehead, and at the crown it does so in the nature of the sixteenth part. In this way, for ordinary beings of saṃsāra, the dark element of the red constituent goes from the secret place to the crown by the division of the first day of the dark fortnight and so on, and at the end of the sky-empty new moon it is the waning of the sixteenth part. Buddhas reverse this very process: from the crown, by the division of the parts of the dark fortnight beginning with the first day, through the particular waning of the sixteenth part of the moon at the end of the sky-empty new moon, it reaches the secret lotus.

As it was also said, with reference to the conventional first buddha:

When all embodied beings die,
the moon, the nectar, goes downward.
Above is the sun, Rāhu, the dust;
this is the defining mark of the object, consciousness.
Therefore the moon, the nectar,
goes upward, the unshakable one.
Below are the sun and Rāhu;
consciousness goes to unchanging bliss.

And ultimately, what will be explained right here:

The awakening mind becomes the moon,
having the nature of fifteen parts.
Great bliss is the form of the vowels.
The yoginīs are its parts.

This is the definitive account of the sixteen joys. Likewise in the chapter on the families the Blessed One said:

How the family, in the yoginī's
birth and in her abiding, is the cause,
how power, wisdom, consciousness,
and the deities arise.
The cause of generating Heruka
is first of all E-VAM.
Innate joy and great bliss,
the letters E-VAM, the great unchanging.

This is the definitive meaning.

For the non-conceptual mind,
there is no meditator and nothing to meditate on.
There is no mantra and no deity.
From the nature free of elaboration,
mantra and deities truly abide.

By this very fact, it is in the essence of the setting of all the turbidity of conceptual thought, which is the nature of innate joy.

Now the division of the six aggregates into the conventional and the ultimate is to be stated, beginning with "Vairocana," and so on. Here Vairocana leads into the earth wheel. Akṣobhya leads into the space wheel. Amitābha leads into the water wheel. Ratnasambhava leads into the fire wheel. Amoghasiddhi leads into the wind wheel. The one who possesses the bodhisattva nature leads into the wheel of wisdom. And as for these, in their respective order:

Brahmā, Viṣṇu, and Śiva (the Peaceful One),
and all, are said to be this very thing.

Conventionally, these are sentient beings whose essence flows downward; ultimately, they are buddhas whose essence flows upward. "Brahmā is the nirvāṇa of the buddha," because the element of excrement does not flow out. "Because he enters, Viṣṇu is to be spoken of": because the element of urine enters one's own body and nothing emerges outside, the buddha is to be called Viṣṇu. "Because he is always virtuous, he is peaceful": here virtue means unimpaired moral discipline, and because of the bliss of the essence not being shed, this buddha is to be called Peaceful.

"Because he abides in all, as the lord of all": "all" is the blood element of Ratnasambhava. When this is not shed there is no suffering, and because of its absence, "he dispels pleasure and pain, and at the end arises; his body, free of attachment, is exhausted." "Abiding in the lord of all" is so because, by the divine eye and the knowledge of others' minds, he pervades everything. The buddha is to be called "the All."

"Without apprehension, he is fully awake." Because there is no apprehension through the sense faculties such as the eye, Amoghasiddhi is the flesh element. Because this is exhausted, the eye and the other faculties have no active formation, and because of that absence he is fully awake. As it is said:

Knowing oneself and knowing others, in all respects,
the supreme person who benefits all,
transcending the world's examples,
the supreme lord of knowing and the knowable.

By this the buddha is "free from concept, fully awake." This is the definitive meaning.

"By supreme bliss he becomes that very thing." Here supreme bliss is the undefiled: emptiness, signlessness, wishlessness, and the unconditioned. It is the four liberations blended into one, in the nature of compassion, loving-kindness, joy, and equanimity without any object of focus. That suchness which has the nature of wisdom and skillful means as its objects of focus is said to be the buddha. Thus:

Know the truth in its twelve aspects,
and that very truth in sixteen aspects.
Awakened through twenty aspects,
know the pure, the supreme, the unchanging.

This refers to the buddha, the Blessed One. From the stages thus taught: "the three families, and the five families, being one in nature, are the hundred families." So in the Recitation of Names it is also said:

The king of wrathful ones, six-faced and terrifying,
six-eyed, six-armed, endowed with power,
with skeleton, gnashing his fangs,
Hala-hala, the hundred-faced one.

And likewise:

Blazing vajra, with beautiful eyes,
blazing vajra born from the head,
the vajra that brings down, the great bringer down,
the hundred-eyed one, the vajra eye.

This is the definitive account of the hundred families.

Thus, by the division into conventional and ultimate, the reality of the worldly and the transworldly is with obscuration and without obscuration, with sign and without sign, with conceptuality and without conceptuality, with superimposition and without superimposition, with pride and without pride. Because sentient beings and buddhas stop the twelve limbs of dependent origination, there is no arising, and no entering either. This is the definitive statement of the Buddha, the Blessed One, in all the other tantras, and the wise should understand it.

Now, further, the deities of the conventional Dharma and so on are to be explained, beginning with the words "it arises in the body." Here, because conventional bliss truly arises in the body, whose nature is the five elements, I shall call it "god." The Buddha possesses bhaga, and so he is called the Blessed One (Bhagavān). Bhaga is explained as sixfold: all the qualities beginning with lordship. Lordship, splendor, wisdom, renown, glory, and good fortune as a quality: these are what the word bhaga expresses. Or else he is called Bhagavān because he vanquishes the māras, such as the afflictions. "By the ultimate" is the definitive statement of means.

Now the wisdom is to be explained, in the passage beginning with "mother." Here the means is the father, the seminal element. Wisdom is the mother, the element of the menstrual blood. The one who is produced is the gandharva, a being impelled by karma, at the end of dying and belonging to rebirth, the all-base consciousness. This itself is what the word "migrator" denotes. For the Blessed One said, "O sons of the Victor, these three realms are mind only." The migrator is just this. "She generates the migrator" means that wisdom is to be called the mother, the element of the menstrual blood. Likewise the means of the fortunate one is the seminal element.

Since wisdom shows the divisions of the body, she is to be called the sister. This refers to the transformations of the elements: the body hair, the skin, the flesh, and the blood. Likewise the means is the brother, since it shows the divisions of the channels, the bones, and the limbs; therefore it is the seminal element. Because she draws out the qualities, wisdom is also to be called the daughter. The qualities are the objects of sound, touch, form, taste, smell, and phenomena; she draws these out because she grasps them. Likewise the means is the son, since it is the very engaging of the eyes and the other sense faculties; thus it is the element of means. Likewise wisdom is the sister's daughter, because the consciousnesses engage their objects. The means is the brother's son, because it engages the eyes and the rest. Likewise, because she enters into the bliss of emission, wisdom is called the wife, and the means is called the husband, since the two depend on each other and both arise from the Dharma of the bliss of emission.

Free of attachment, wisdom is called the untouchable one, the outcaste woman. The means likewise is so called. This is the definitive statement of the six mudrās. Because she bewilders all the elements, she is to be called the brahmin woman. Because she cuts, wisdom is the butcher woman. Because she stirs the elements, she is the dancer. Because she burns, she is spoken of as the dark one, the mātṛkā. Because she is untouchable, she is spoken of as the outcaste woman. Likewise the means too should be understood, from its nature of the five actions.

Thus all of this, as external, was investigated by the non-Buddhists through the Vedas and the like. By nature all women and all men have arisen as a single human family, and for them there is no other family. This is the intended meaning in all the other tantras.

Now the recitation of mantras and so on, according to the nature of the elements, is explained in the passage beginning "the expression." Here, the expression of all beings, which is the expression of the vowels and consonants, ālī and kālī, is for that very reason said to be recitation, for the yogis of reality. Likewise, the footsteps become the maṇḍala. Because they measure the ground with their feet, they are to be called the maṇḍala. Likewise, the turning of the hands becomes the mudrā, and so does the touching of the fingers. Likewise, what beings think is the very thing to be contemplated, for by knowing the minds of others, the yogis contemplate it. The phrase "that which is to be contemplated is free of thought" means that "free of thought" is the absence of thinking; this is what is to be contemplated by the yogis.

The bliss attained at the time of the emission of a being, belonging to rebirth and at the end of dying, in the domain of the father, is itself to be eaten by oneself, by one born at the age of sixteen. A woman is to enjoy it at the age of twelve. The bliss by which one dies here, the bliss of being born and abiding in saṃsāra for those in saṃsāra, when it becomes stable, is to be called the meditative concentration of the yogis.

O Essence of Hevajra, by me all this has been taught as the summary of the meaning gathered, chapter by chapter, from each of the five hundred thousand verses of the condensed Hevajra Tantra, in the fifth chapters. Beyond this, the chapters on conduct and so on are to be understood in detail. With thirty verses in the chapter on lineages, the meaning is gathered from the first hundred thousand. With forty verses in the chapter on mantras, from the second. With twenty-four verses in the chapter on deities, from the third. With six verses in the chapter on empowerment, from the fourth. With twenty verses in the chapter on reality, from the fifth. Thus the meaning gathered in these hundred and twenty-odd verses is to be understood by yogis in the five chapters through this extensive commentary.

This is the great summary of the definitive teaching on reality in the six-thousand-verse commentary on Hevajra.

Thus the chapter on reality is complete. The commentary on the meaning of the glorious Hevajra Summary is complete. It was composed by the venerable bodhisattva, the great being Vajragarbha.

This was translated by the Indian preceptor Dānaśīla and the translator Dro Sengkar Śākya Öd. Later it was revised and finalized by the Indian preceptor Subhūti Śrī Śānti and the translator Chok-gru Tingngedzin Zangpo. Later again, in the presence of the Indian preceptor, the king's son, glorious Abhayadeva, the translator Nyel-chor, the monk Prajñākīrti, checked it against the manuscript from Central India and settled the text. Once more, through the excellent exposition of the glorious Shongton Dorje Gyaltsen and others, the translator who is a master of grammar and the sciences, the glorious Lodrö Tenpa, ... The spiritual friend Ralungpa Chödrak Palzangpo, who has great faith in the three sets of bodhisattva commentaries and whose intellectual clarity is vast, had the books of the root text and commentary of the Compendium of Reality, the great Madhyamaka work composed by the great teacher Śāntarakṣita and his disciples, copied and gave them as an offering, and urged the work again and again. In response, it was corrected, translated, checked, and finalized. The scribe was the one named Dzedön Kunga Gyaltsen. May this benefit countless sentient beings.

Here begins the commentary of Vajragarbha.

Having bowed to Hevajra, who bears the twelve limbs and uproots existence, who holds the twelve kinds of purity, I shall explain this extensive chapter on supreme conduct. Following the Five Hundred Thousand, and tormented by compassion for beings, I, Vajragarbha, shall explain it.

Here, the yogī who has entered the maṇḍala of Hevajra, diligent in not abandoning his earlier pledges, engages in conduct for the sake of all beings. Therefore the root text says:

Then I shall further explain
the conduct, the supreme perfection,
the cause that is the accomplishment of Hevajra,
by which accomplishment reaches its end.

"Then" is a connecting word, indicating that this follows the explanation of the chapter on reality. It means that he has begun the conduct in order that the three realms may be accomplished as Hevajra, by way of the cause that is the accomplishment of Hevajra. No conduct other than this is taught, since it would serve no purpose. The purpose of the conduct is "by which accomplishment reaches its end." It means that he has begun the conduct by which himself and other beings reach the final end of the accomplishment of the great seal (mahāmudrā).

This meaning should be drawn from the root tantra itself. As it says:

Even when the ritual is complete in all its limbs,
without beginning the conduct, no accomplishment arises.
Therefore this unattached conduct
is the cause of the accomplishment of the seal.
Mere compassion, utterly cut off,
cannot accomplish conduct for the sake of beings.
Therefore, endowed with compassion,
one should practice the conduct with effort.

The meaning of these lines should also be understood.

Now the yogī who wishes to practice the conduct should bind on the seals, outer and inner. The same tantra says:

The meditator should take up
fine earrings for the ears,
the wheel upon the crown,
two bracelets on the two hands,
a double girdle at the waist,
anklets likewise on the feet,
armlets on the upper arms,
and a necklace of bones about the neck.

The meaning of this is clear in the Five Hundred Thousand, so one should turn to it:

If one wishes to begin the conduct,
in order to inspire beings,
one adorns the supreme body
with the form of Akṣobhya's wheel.
One turns the wheel in the three existences.
Abiding in the supreme body, by a stream of water,
one turns the wheel of Dharma at once
and accomplishes the great benefit of beings.
With the form of Amitābha's earrings
one adorns the ears,
and one makes the three existences attached.
Thereby one will work for the benefit of beings.
With the form of the jeweled necklace
one adorns one's own neck,
and having tamed beings by flavor,
one dissolves beings like water.
With the form of Vairocana's bracelets
one adorns the hands,
and if one engages in the benefit of beings,
people will become steady like the earth.
With the form of Amoghasiddhi's girdle
one adorns the waist,
and if one tames beings by wind,
one tames beings by the unobstructed wind.
By the nature of the awakening mind
one adorns the body with ashes,
and if one practices the supreme conduct well,
one tames beings with a blissful mind.

Having understood the meaning of this fully, one should know that the definitive meaning is this: the six seminal fluids and six winds, which depend on the six wheels whose natures are called time, water, fire, wind, space, and wisdom, are stopped from flowing outward. In this way the conduct is to be practiced, and thereby one tames those hard to tame; otherwise it is not so. Therefore, what is shown as the sign of their being stopped is taught as the six, beginning with the wheel. Those who practice the conduct must also take up the outer forms.

Furthermore, it is to be connected thus: the practitioner conducts himself as a hero, with food of bliss, abiding in method and wisdom, abiding in the manner of the five families, showing himself through color, gathering everything into a single family, and making himself indistinguishable by color or by family. This is stated in this very Compendium Tantra:

His garment is the skin of a tiger,
His food is the five and a half deathless ones.
Heruka in union is the man himself,
Abiding in the five colors.
Endowed with equality with the five families,
He is to be thought of as one family alone,
Because no distinctions are to be indicated.

From the stilling of the six inner mudrās there arise this strength and splendor. Thus, even fierce and unbearable beings such as Vajrapāṇi, lord of the yakṣas, are not frightened or terrified; how much less ordinary worldly people. Therefore, in order to triumph over the unruly outer and inner māras, one outwardly wears the skin of a tiger as a garment. And in order to win the devotion of people, one eats the nectar, brought down from the realm of the gods and so on. Furthermore, for a person who has entered the way of mantra, for the sake of outer accomplishments, one purifies the head and the other such substances, and also the five and a half deathless ones, possessed of the hair called bya. One abandons conceptual thought and eats them oneself, and also gives them to others, so that beginners in the practice may come to understand. The definitive meaning of eating this nectar is clear in the great root tantra itself, so one should turn to that. As it says:

The food of the five and a half deathless ones
Has not been understood by people.
The very kinds of head and the rest,
By the conduct of the lord of yogins,
Being without cause for the one and the rest to go outward,
He relies on them, and that itself dissolves.
Supreme in celibate conduct and vows,
Brought to the end of austerity, great in austerity.

This is the definitive meaning of the Tathāgatas. Furthermore, abiding in emptiness and in compassion inseparable from it, one becomes Heruka. For the sake of taming outer people, whatever colored powders and so on abide in the five colors are shown to be endowed with the qualities of the five or six Tathāgatas. Even those difficult to tame are tamed by this and become devout. Therefore one should show oneself endowed with equality with the five families. Likewise, since he is the very nature of the dharmadhātu, his color is white; all families and colors are gathered in him, so he is to be shown thus. Hence the line "He is to be thought of as one family alone." Likewise, these teachings should be kept secret and hidden, through the manner of mantra, from all but beginners in the practice: from those abiding on the path of the śrāvakas, those abiding on the path of the pratyekabuddhas, and the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas). Therefore, by means of many colors, "no distinctions are to be indicated"; this is the meaning.

Furthermore, one who practices the supreme conduct, for the sake of sentient beings, accomplishes the hero Heruka with a mind that tames the inner unruliness, and abides as the nature of the colors, that is, of the five families. Thus, he is to enter the bodies of kings, of ministers, of brahmins, of commoners, of outcastes and so on, taking on bodies in keeping with each, and show conduct accordingly. Going into their midst, he overturns their respective families and brings them all into the single family of the vajra. Likewise, within the family of ministers he hides the signs of the four families, enters in accord with that very one, and teaches the Dharma to those to be tamed. Likewise, among all the families he acts so as not to be incongruous and teaches the Dharma. Likewise, in order to accomplish the karmamudrā, he takes the body of another family, so as to avoid the worldly reproach, and accomplishes the karmamudrā. Therefore it is said:

"Through many families, no distinctions are to be indicated."

The definitive meaning of these points is clear in the root tantra itself, so one should turn to that. As it says:

By joining emptiness with Heruka,
Earth, water, fire, wind and space
Are placed in the path, indivisibly,
With the two, semen and wind, abiding.
It is reduced to ashes by the fire at the end of the age,
Like space, with nothing to be indicated.

Here, when one abides on the path called emptiness, the splendor of wisdom blazes forth. By that splendor the five kinds of semen and the five kinds of wind become of one taste and one mixture, with nothing to be indicated, having abandoned a nature of difference. This is the definitive meaning of the Tathāgatas. Otherwise, because the mind does not rest one-pointedly, semen, urine, excrement and the like abide as different families, and this is called differing distinction.

Now, in order to speak of the special features of the place of the practitioner, the Compendium Tantra says:

A single tree, a charnel ground,
A house of the mothers, or a crossroads,
Or a secluded place, or the border of a forest:
There, meditation is said to be excellent.

The meaning of this should be understood by distinguishing outer and inner. First, the outer: if a beginner practitioner practices in a delightful place, secluded from the bustle of people, the signs of accomplishment will arise. Such places are where nonhuman beings dwell: a place where a single tree stands; a "house of the mothers," where yakṣiṇīs and piśācīs dwell; a secluded place or forest border, that is, a place where other nonhumans gather, secluded from crowds and noise. There the yogin sits, generates great compassion for all sentient beings, and offers tormas three times a day to the nonhumans who live there. He tells those nonhumans again and again, "I shall do such and such; you should accomplish this and that." Then those nonhumans too, pleased with the practitioner, carry out that work, and when it is accomplished he becomes able to work for the benefit of beings.

The definitive meaning of this point is as the root tantra says:

One's own body is the supreme mountain.
The great tree that grows from it,
With its crown ripens the gods,
With its roots ripens the nāgas and the rest.
With its branches it fully ripens humans and others.
That is the tree, and it is taught
That one abides there.
Where the breath does not move,
That is called the charnel ground.
The house of the mothers is emptiness itself.
The play of the lion is the mudrā.
It is not defiled by the adornments of signs,
And because it is hard to realize, it is the night.
It has no seed syllable, no hand emblem,
No color, shape, or family.
It is free of mudrā, fire offering, and torma.
That solitude is the final limit, the place of solitude,
And that alone is the excellent meditation.

This is the definitive meaning as it concerns the Tathāgata.

To show the measure of one's capacity to engage in the practice of conduct, it is said:

When one has gained a little warmth,
If one wishes to undertake the conduct,
Or if one wishes to attain accomplishment,
By this, one practices the conduct itself.

The meaning of this should be drawn from the root tantra itself, where it is said:

If one wishes to undertake the conduct,
The six-limbed union, whatever it may be,
Should be done by first generating
The warmth of smoke, mirage, and the like.

The meaning is this. Yogins who wish to practice the conduct for the sake of sentient beings should first generate warmth through the signs of smoke, mirage, and the like, and only then enter the conduct. Otherwise it becomes an object of ridicule for worldly people. Then, in order to tame men who are full of desire, having experienced the wisdom that does not pass away, one should, as the text says:

Examine a consort of the vajra family,
Of very beautiful face and wide eyes,
Compassionate, whom one has empowered oneself,
And take her up to practice the conduct.
If there is no one of the vajra family,
Then do it with one of the family of one's chosen deity.

The meaning of this is as follows. The girl should be of the outcaste family, not to be touched by other men, or else of any family at all, adorned with beauty and youth. She is purified with the seed of awakening. That is, one who is without any lack of faith in oneself, and one performs the act of union. With the awakening mind of bliss one purifies her mind, and goes about together with her. One tames men full of desire with vajra songs and the like. And the dance belongs to the family of Amoghasiddhi, as this very Compendium of the Tantra says:

If one takes up song out of joy,
Then one possesses the supreme vajra.
When joy has arisen,
One dances with the cause of liberation.
Then one dances with vajra feet,
Doing so in the equipoise of the yogin.

The definitive meaning of this is, as the Pentad of Hundred Thousands says:

Emptiness, unborn and peaceful,
Is called woman.
From the sixteen knots of the channels
Arise the sixteen drops of bliss.
That is also the sixteen years,
And that is also the purification of the mind.

In the same way, the resonance of bliss is the song; the dance is the movement stirred by bliss; and one abides possessed of feet that proceed by the stages of bliss. "One should perform it in the equipoise of the yogin": this is the definitive meaning.

Furthermore, regarding the passage beginning "Unshakable in the form of the wheel" and ending "On the belt dwells Amoghasiddhi" (Don yod), the meaning has already been shown. As for the meanings other than those, they are as follows:

The khaṭvāṅga is the form of wisdom,
The small drum is the form of method itself.

Here "kha" has the form of space. "Ṭvāṃ" is one who abides in austerity. In brief, the meaning is that all phenomena abide in the nature of space. The sound of the ḍamaru signifies turning the wheel of Dharma at one time for all sentient beings in the three realms. Therefore, in holding the khaṭvāṅga and the small drum, the path of the afflictions gives rise to aversion, and the breath's going out, its entering within, and its abiding are themselves the vowels and consonants (āli and kāli). Therefore, as it is said:

Song is taught as pure mantra,
Dance is what is to be meditated upon.
For that reason the yogin
Should always practice song and dance.

Likewise, "The medicine is to be eaten, and the water is always to be drunk" should be understood in two ways. Outwardly, for the sake of worldly accomplishments, one purifies and multiplies excrement and urine and the like, and eats them without conceptualization. By the power of that it is said, "He is not overpowered by the splendor of old age." The definitive meaning is as the root tantra says:

The upper and lower of the left, wisdom and method,
Free of arising and entering, is what is eaten.
The upper and lower of the right, wisdom and method,
Free of arising and entering, is what is drunk.

By this, "he is not overpowered by the splendor of old age, and is always protected." This is the definitive meaning. Otherwise, by eating and drinking external excrement and urine one would not become free of being overpowered by old age, and even worldly people would revile it. That is the definitive point.

Furthermore:

Make a crest ornament from the hair of thieves,
And fix on it what has arisen from hūṃ.
The skulls of the five buddhas
The yogin's conduct bears.
Of the portions of a skull five inches wide,
One makes a crest ornament and holds it.
From the nature of method and wisdom,
The belt of hair, twofold in its winding,
And ashes are worn as an offering of hair.
He maintains the conduct of the yogin.

As this is taught, it indicates the crest ornament by the root called "the hair of thieves," since it steals away all virtuous qualities. On it one fully holds the vajra lattice that has arisen from hūṃ, for the sake of accomplishing the many kinds of benefit for sentient beings. This is by the manner of the five and the twenty ways of manifest awakening, and also by the fifteen-day groups of the waxing fortnight, such as Full and Excellent. Therefore it should be understood through a, i, ṛ, u, ḷ, as having the qualities of water, fire, wind, and space. Thus the passages beginning "the skulls of the five buddhas," and "the portion of a skull five inches wide," are taught at length.

In the same way, for the sake of taming the arrogant worldly gods, one wears a double girdle of human hair; for the sake of the observance of the Great Lord (Maheśvara), one smears the body with ashes; for the sake of Brahmā's observance, one wears a Brahmā-cord of human hair; for the sake of the gods' joyful play, one holds the damaru drum; for the sake of the banner of the Great Lord, one holds the khaṭvāṅga; and for the sake of the observance with fragments of skulls, one should hold the vajra skull. These are to be recited and meditated upon. So it was taught.

The definitive meaning of this is also given in the root tantra, where it says:

Because emptiness and compassion are inexhaustible,
the girdle of hair is spoken of.
The ashes are taught as awakening mind.
For the sake of chastity, the semen is not emitted;
for the sake of honoring with hair, one is adorned with the topknot.
Therefore this is the supreme observance of chastity.
By the blissful sound of the damaru,
the three worlds are caused to breathe again.
In the aspect of emptiness, the khaṭvāṅga;
the skull is said to be the bliss that protects the body.
Therefore, for that reason, these
are to be recited and meditated upon.

Furthermore, if a yogin who practices the conduct has not fully abandoned shame, apprehension, and the like, then the conduct becomes an occasion for the laughter of worldly people, and it does not benefit sentient beings either. Therefore it was taught:

Abandon greed, bewilderment, fear, and anger,
and the fruit of shame as well.

Likewise, the same tantra says:

Even if an asura should appear before you,
resembling Indra himself,
do not be afraid of him;
go about in the form of a lion.

Therefore one should not be apprehensive or timid. A yogin who practices the conduct in this way should not dwell in laziness. Abandon the sleep that accompanies laziness. Great delusion dwells in sleep, and so one practices the conduct. Therefore:

Having abandoned sleep oneself,
practice the conduct without doubt.

Likewise, the Sugata too, at the time of his austerities, gave up the wealth of kingship, the retinue of queens, and even the very limit of his own body for the sake of sentient beings. The yogin who practices the conduct should also have no concern for his own body. Thus it was taught:

Having given the gift of the body,
afterward one properly begins the conduct.

And one should wear space as one's garment. Crows, cruel dogs, and wicked, ill-natured, abusive children are to be regarded as teachers who teach the Dharma, and being scorched by the sun's heat or afflicted by cold are grounds for practicing patience. As for giving other possessions, one should not distinguish among the recipients of the gift: whoever appears before one is to be given to. Thus it was taught:

Therefore, giving should not be given
by examining who is worthy and who is not.

Likewise:

In the manner of non-conceptuality,
food and likewise drink,
whatever is found, one should eat freely.
Since one does not conceive of pleasant and unpleasant,
one should not even be attached to it.
Edible and inedible things to practice with,
likewise the drinkable and the undrinkable,
the going and the not going, the mantrin
should not conceptualize them.

The meaning of this is as follows. Out of mere attachment to taste, one makes no distinction between foods and drinks. And for the sake of accomplishing the karmamudrā, one should not make distinctions regarding women of the four families, saying, "This one should be approached, that one should not."

Furthermore, the vajra master is to be revered as no different from one's own chosen deity. Therefore, even if I abide on the level of the vidyādharas, I should not discard him. Thus it was taught:

The disciple who has attained siddhi,
the illuminator of perfect wisdom,
for the sake of abandoning the cause of Avīci,
should bow directly to the master as a accomplished one.

The meaning of this repeated statement is as follows. If even the buddhas of the three times bow with reverence to their chosen master in the three times, then why should beginner disciples who have entered the path abandon him? The definitive meaning of this was already explained at length earlier.

Moreover, the practitioner of the conduct should not cling intensely to the worldly conventions of training, the seven empowerments, fire offerings, offerings, austerities, vows, and so on. Or else, he should not cling intensely to the training and fire offerings and the like of the non-Buddhist god Śiva and others. Therefore, for that reason:

Freed from training and from empowerment,
and likewise from the fruit of shame,
this nature of all things
is practiced by the yogin with great compassion.
Beyond fire offerings, offerings, and austerities,
having abandoned mantra and meditation,
liberated from the vows of samaya,
the yogin practices the conduct.

And likewise, further:

For the sake of all sentient beings,
compassion should always be drunk.
The yogin who delights in drinking yoga
should not savor any other drink.

This meaning should be understood in two ways, outer and inner. In the outer sense, in accord with worldly custom, at feasts, at the meals of the heroes, and the like, one drinks liquors made from grain and so on, but only up to the point of not becoming intoxicated. In the definitive sense, "other drink" refers to the liquors, made from grapes, rice, and the like, which intoxicate worldly people and lead them to do what should not be done. One should not drink these even to the tip of a hair. Rather, by drinking without interruption the continuous stream of the liquor of the awakening mind of the moon, one moistens one's own body and so forth with the taste of bliss. This is the definitive meaning, as spoken by the Tathāgata.

This is the sixth, the extensive explanation of the chapter on conduct, from the extensive exposition of the definitive meaning of Hevajra.

Having paid homage to the Teacher, the enjoyment body, who bears the sixteen half-measures of the drop, Vajragarbha has written the extensive explanation of the Tsotsma. Then the chapter on signs will be explained.

Having said this, it should be understood that signs are of two kinds. As the root tantra itself says:

Together with Heruka, the yogī
Should train in the signs of body and speech.
If one does not speak with the signs of body and speech,
Even a buddha will surely be ruined.

Therefore, yogīs and yoginīs who abide in the yoga of Hevajra should take to heart, with diligence, the instruction on the signs of body and speech. In the midst of a gathering, they should not speak in ordinary worldly language in front of unruly outsiders, or of messengers, male and female, of other families. When the great secret that belongs to us is expressed by signs, unfit persons and outside messengers, male and female, remain bewildered. And whoever does not speak the great secrets by signs ruins the accomplishments of both this world and the next, and will go to the hell of unceasing torment.

Of these, the signs of body come first and the signs of speech after. In this chapter only the signs of body are shown, while the signs of speech will be shown in another chapter. Moreover, both kinds of signs should be understood as being of definitive meaning and of provisional meaning. What is taught as it stands in this very condensed tantra should be understood as provisional meaning, while the definitive meaning should be understood from the root tantra.

First, the line "Whoever knows brother and sister" means that brother and sister are the distinct aspects of method and wisdom. "Know it without doubt" means to know method and wisdom exactly as they are, according to their provisional and definitive meanings.

Now, "Whoever shows one finger" should be understood thus: when a man or woman suddenly arrives in front of one and shows one finger, it is a question meaning "Have you come well?" Likewise, "With two, he has come well" means that one answers by extending two fingers, showing that one has come in comfort.

Further, "Holding the left thumb is to be known as the gesture of inquiring about illness." It is to be understood as the sign of asking someone about illness, with the meaning "Are your body, speech and mind well?"

"Whoever offers the ring finger" is the aspect of method, and "to him the little finger is given" is the aspect of wisdom. By the distinction of above and below, they should be understood as wind and fire. "Whoever shows the middle finger, to him the index finger is given" likewise, by the distinction of above and below, should be understood as water and earth. Thus these too are, outwardly and inwardly, of the nature of emptiness and compassion. This is also what the tantra called Glorious Supreme Primordial Buddha says:

In the east and west, earth and wind
Are mutually method and wisdom.
In the south and north, fire and water
Are mutually method and wisdom.

And likewise:

The channel of excrement on the lunar path,
The sun brings down the urine.
The two channels of mind, above and below,
Are the descending seed and the Dark One.

"Whoever shows the nameless finger, to him the throat is clearly shown." When one asks, "Do you know the Dharma that is like space?", the answer, showing the throat, means this: the throat wheel is the path that enjoys the six tastes, and by its power the channels expand, and it will teach the Dharma that is like space.

"Whoever shows with clothing, to him the three peaks are clearly shown." When one asks, "Do you know the characteristics of the protector?", the answer means: "I know, if I know the nature of the three channels, the essence of body, speech and mind."

"Whoever shows the breasts, to him the boundary is clearly shown." When one asks, "If this is attained through the treatises on dance, do you know it?", the answer means: "I know; I realize it from the bliss of the two organs joined."

"Whoever shows the teeth, to him the mouth is clearly shown." When one asks, "Do you know the nature of wisdom?", the answer means: "I know it, if one possesses method and wisdom."

"Whoever shows a frown, to him the loosening of the topknot is to be shown." When someone says, "Then you and I will debate over the nature of method and wisdom," the answer means: "Without fear, let us debate."

"Whoever shows the forehead, to him the back is clearly shown." When one asks, "If the supreme place of the ḍākinīs is in the body, in the head, do you know it?", the answer means: "I know it; apart from the head, other parts of the body too, such as the back, are places of the ḍākinīs."

"Whoever shows the sole of the foot, with the belly one should play." When one asks, "Then how should one travel to those places?", the answer means: "Preceded by the treatises on dance, entering equipoise with a mind full of passion, one will reach those places."

"By the answer of mudrā to mudrā, distinguish by the samaya." This means: "By knowing the signs of the body's mudrās, realize all the great secrets."

Then the yoginī said: "E ma, my son of great compassion." The meaning is: "E ma, you whose nature is great method, you have become familiar with all signs and non-signs."

"If one shows a rosary in the hand, it says 'Let us assemble.' Having released the rosary, take the vow and the observance as the fine practice of the samaya." The meaning is this: in answer to the signs, all the yoginīs, each from her own place, show a rosary in the hand, and thereby indicate: "Son of good family, you and I shall abide in the observance of an unbroken rosary of bliss."

And further it says:

There, those assembling in the outer place,
Abiding in the domain of the good,
Whatever the assembling yoginī says there,
Do all of it just so.

The meaning is: when she commands, "You are fortunate, and you know all the intentional language, outer and inner. Therefore you and we shall gather in the outer place and enjoy the feast of outer and inner bliss," that yogī accepts it all.

"Kye, Blessed One, is it a place of assembly?" is Vajragarbha and the others asking about the distinctions between outer and inner places.

The Blessed One declared:

Site and near-site,
field and near-field,
chandoha and near-chandoha,
gathering place and near-gathering place,
city and near-city,
charnel ground and near-charnel ground:
since these are the twelve grounds,
the Lord is sovereign of the ten grounds.
Therefore one should not seek elsewhere.

This is what was said. By this the text shows that all the root classes of channels, which rest on the nature of the three wheels, the six wheels, and the four wheels, are just these twelve grounds. The nature of the three wheels is divided into body, speech, and mind, and also into the levels beneath the earth, upon the earth, and above the earth; in this way the meaning of the tantra of the Wheel of Supreme Bliss is expressed. The nature of the six wheels is divided into earth, water, fire, wind, space, and wisdom; or into form, feeling, perception, formations, consciousness, and wisdom; or it is distinguished by the six syllables. This is the meaning of the tantra of the Supreme Buddha. As for what is taught as four, there are the sixty-four emanations, the eight dharmas, the sixteen enjoyments, the thirty-two great blissful ones, and so on. Thus the divisions of the four wheels, the four bodies, the four empowerments, the four joys, the four moments, the four fruits, and so on are taught in the Hevajra tantra, for it is said that everything is fourfold.

Therefore, outwardly, the goddesses who dwell in the villages and run after flesh and blood live in those outer sites. And since it is said, "As it is outside, so it is within," the inner sites are taught to be present in the body in the form of channels, and one should not look for them elsewhere. In the body they are as follows, from Jālandhara up to "the banks of the pools in the pleasure grove." As the root tantra says:

The crown of the head and the top of the head,
the right ear and the side of the ribs,
likewise the brow, the middle of the eyebrows,
the hands and the armpits,
the two breasts and the navel,
the tip of the nose and the path of the mouth,
the two shoulders and the male organ,
the sign and the anus, and the place of the chest,
the thighs and the fingers,
the soles of the feet, the big toes,
and the knees:
in this order one should view the sites and the grounds.

Therefore the goddesses who bestow on beings the fruit of conduct and of liberation, and these sites, dwell in the bodies of all embodied beings themselves, in the form of channels and seminal essence.

Now, in order to speak of the particular character of the daytime, it says:

In the Yoginī tantra, the Hevajra,
for the sake of all beings
who gather as the excellent assembly of yoginīs,
the daytime too shall be fully explained.

Here the time of day is of two kinds, outer and inner. Outwardly, on the fourteenth of the waning moon and the like, the village goddesses roam through the outer sites mentioned above in order to steal the radiance of all embodied beings. Inwardly, they scatter and gather in the particular places of the seminal essence, the winds and so on, the breath, the urine measure, the time measure, and the like. In the same way, a yogī who longs for the two kinds of accomplishment should know the distinction between outer and inner. For the sake of the common accomplishment, on the fourteenth of the waning moon and the like, he goes at night to the charnel ground and takes part in the gatherings, such as the autumn festival of the heroes.

This is why the text asks, "O Blessed One, what is the day?", meaning the outer and the inner day. The Blessed One declared:

The fourteenth of the waning half of the month,
and likewise the eighth.

Here the fourteenth of the waning half is the twenty-ninth, and likewise the eighth of the waning half is the twenty-third. The definitive meaning should be understood from the root tantra itself, which says:

This moon of awakening mind
abides as waxing and waning.
By the waxing she is the one who generates;
by the waning she is the one who destroys.
On the fourteenth of the waning moon and the like,
the ḍākinīs of awakening mind
gather through the wheel of great bliss.
This is explained as the definitive meaning.

Furthermore, regarding what should be eaten, it is said:

The banner and the one struck by a weapon,
and the one of seven times: these too should be eaten.

The banner is a man or woman, such as a thief, whom a king has punished by sentence, cut with a weapon, and hung on a corpse stake and left there. The one of seven times is some man or woman who has been born in a human body through seven successive lives. The signs of such a person are that the shadow appears sevenfold, the eyes do not blink, there are three wrinkles on the forehead, a fragrant smell comes from the body, and so on. If one sees such a person, one offers flowers into the hands, circumambulates, and says, "Great lord of yoga, now the time has come for you to do something for us." When this is said, that person abandons the body. Having abandoned it, one makes pills from the flesh, each the size of a joint of a ginger root. All of us should eat them and also distribute them to all beings as their share. This bestows the accomplishment of moving through the sky. Likewise, the flesh of ordinary people, of those hung on banners, of those struck by weapons, and so on is purified and increased and made into pills and eaten; this surely destroys wrinkles, white hair, and the like.

The definitive meaning of these is also given in the Five Hundred Thousand, which says:

By this supreme banner of the body,
the tree of channels purified from form,
if one eats the flesh of awakening mind,
the yogī will attain the supreme accomplishment.

And the definitive meaning of the one of seven times is given there as follows:

This body of all embodied beings
is of seven births.
From all the food and drink
that is eaten and drunk, endowed with the six tastes,
what melts and grows as the essence
is called the first birth.
Then from this blood arises:
it is said to be the second birth.
From this flesh arises, the third;
becoming skin is the fourth;
becoming channels is the fifth.
From this bone is the sixth,
and marrow and fat are the seventh.
So it was said earlier.

Furthermore, the text says: "Because compassion is generated with effort, the very mention of killing cannot succeed without compassion; therefore compassion must be generated." In regard to this, there is first the distinction between outer and inner. For the time being, for the sake of the outer: "One who commits the five acts of immediate retribution, or who disturbs the Buddha's teaching, belongs to the family of aversion. By means of meditative absorption, for the time being, the one to be killed should not be destroyed in every respect. But if, having concealed everything, a yogī falls away from the samaya, accomplishment can never occur." By this, all the malevolent ones are tamed. Therefore it was said, "Bringing all the malevolent ones into the fold."

Furthermore, as for the definitive meaning, the root tantra says: "The great life-breaths above and below, the great yogī kills and also nourishes. Other than these, he does not kill living beings; he always kills those malevolent ones." This is the definitive meaning.

"It is to be prevented by the chief of the rite." This pertains to both the outer and the inner life. The outer is prevented by the meditative absorption of the family of bliss and emptiness; the inner is prevented by great emptiness.

Furthermore, the times of day and night are to be understood as the division into method and wisdom. Thus: "By day, the Blessed One, the Vajra-holder; by night, wisdom is to be understood." The meaning of this is: "That which is wisdom is non-entity; entity is to be understood as method."

The definitive meaning of this too is found in the root tantra: "That which has the characteristics of the sun and moon, right and left, which makes cool and makes hot, that is both fault and virtue; that is method and wisdom."

Likewise: "There is nothing that should not be done; there is nothing that is always not to be eaten; there is nothing about this that is not to be contemplated; there is no one who does not speak of good or bad." For all this moving and not moving arises entirely from oneself. Therefore: "As one is oneself, so are others; likewise the self is the supreme reality." When one knows one's own nature just as it is, one becomes the sole sovereign who turns the wheel of the three realms.

For this very reason: "Thus the yogī of mind, who engages in eating, drinking, and so forth, whatever limbs he moves, and whatever words he utters, Heruka, dwelling in glory, declares them all to be mantras and mudrās." As it is said: "The maṇḍala deities are this very body. Other deities and maṇḍalas will never be found by searching. So the hero, the guide, has declared." "Śrī is nondual wisdom" means not grasping at either aspect of glory, one's own or another's. "He is emptiness, free of causes and so forth" means the great bliss of meditative absorption, free of causes such as action, wisdom, the binding of mudrās, and torma offerings. "Ru is freedom from gatherings" means bliss liberated from imputed characteristics. Then, to the thought of how that is to be accomplished: "Ka is dwelling nowhere." It is declared that accomplishment comes through being free of gatherings and of objects of focus, and through there being no mutual dependence. Therefore he is Heruka.

Furthermore: "By the yoga of the vajra skull" means by the yoga of sustaining the body. "Whatever beings' flesh the wise one eats, those beings come under his power." If a yogī manifests for himself the yoga of sustaining the body, even buddhas come under his power; how much more so do ordinary beings living in the three worlds do as they are commanded. Otherwise, when the yogī engages with women, if he consumes the flesh of awakening mind without letting it go outward, he gains mastery over his own body; such a one is called a skillful yogī.

This is the seventh chapter, the extensive explanation of the Teaching of Symbols and Settling, from the extensive explanation of the definitive meaning of the Hevajra.

"The awakening mind becomes the moon, whose nature is the fifteen parts. That becomes the yoginī Nairātmyā, who, transforming into the form of a human, brings the three worlds under her power. Having bowed respectfully to her, the extensive explanation of the maṇḍala of Nairātmyā is to be drawn by Vajragarbha. Then the wheel of the yoginīs will be explained." With such words he begins. The yoginī belongs to the waning moon, the fifteen lunar days; she removes the dust of darkness, destroys the dust of appearance, and is spoken of as that which is the awakening mind of saṃsāra looking downward.

As the root tantra says: "By the division of body, speech, and mind, the maṇḍala is considered in three forms. First is Hevajra himself, called the body maṇḍala. After that, Nairātmyā is taught as the mind maṇḍala. After that Kurukullā is taught, regarded as the speech maṇḍala." Therefore it is fitting now to explain the wheel of the yoginīs.

"In the realm of space, contemplate the bhaga. Contemplate in the center, with the wheel first, as appropriate, how the deities arise." Its meaning is this: the preliminary protection wheel and so forth are to be understood from what is taught for the time of generating Heruka. Here, in order to show only the key points, the words "in the expanse of space" and so forth are spoken. After offering to one's own lord, one purifies the vessel and its contents in the manner of "Oṃ śūnyatā-jñāna" and so forth, and imagines all phenomena as like the maṇḍala of space. After that, in order to represent the body, speech, and mind of all the tathāgatas, in that expanse of space one visualizes a letter E-shaped form of immeasurable extent, and within it, "the wheel first, as appropriate."

"Earth, water, fire, and wind": the maṇḍalas are generated in sequence. From their complete transformation, one visualizes the wheel of the maṇḍala of the Blessed Lady, with four gates and with a lintel. "Two rims, faultless": it is cut into two rims in the manner of goddesses of object and subject. "From the hub, one arises": in the hub and the inner rim are the goddesses of the five purified aggregates, which are the five letters of the great emptiness spoken of earlier. These are also called the five great natures, and the later rims become the outer ones.

Therefore it is said, "As for the triangle, the later is declared." Then: "In the center, in the form of the fifteen, contemplate the nature of the seat." This means that because all the elements move, riding upon the life-wind, they are imagined externally as the rasanā channel. "Upon that is the moon. Upon the moon, the seed itself." As is said in the root tantra itself: "First the semen arises from the father; consciousness, endowed with wind, enters there; and there it is joined with the mother's blood. By this meaning the generation stage is taught." Accordingly, it is taught that the rasanā channel, the moon, the seed, and the disk of the sun press down upon one another.

"From the union of the two, great bliss." This is because of the nature of semen and blood, the sequence of the life-breath's exhalation and inhalation, the lalanā and rasanā channels passing through, and the upper and lower passing through each other.

"Ālī abides in the form of the moon; kālī makes appear in the form of the sun." This means that in the external stage of generation, one contemplates the moon disk, fully transformed from ālī, as the way the life-breath comes in, and the sun disk, from kālī, as the way the life-breath goes out. This is the provisional and the definitive meaning.

"From the union of moon and sun." This means from the union of the natures of sun and moon together with consciousness, which makes three. "Renowned as Gaurī and the rest." Externally this is known as the generating of the world of beings and their environment; internally, as the generating of the sacred existence. The meaning is that all the fifteen goddesses, Gaurī and the others, arise in this way.

"The moon, endowed with the mirror-like wisdom; the seven-horsed one, endowed with equality. The seed, the hand-emblem, and the deity of one's own should each be spoken of separately. All becoming one, entirely, completeness is the purity of the dharmadhātu." This means: moved by wind, kindled by fire, ripened by water, held together by earth, and expanded by space. The throat, the palate, the crown, the lips, and the teeth are of the nature of the five wisdoms; this is called the meaning of the five awakenings. Therefore it is said, "By the rite the wise have taught, one should meditate on the five aspects." It should be understood as the provisional and the definitive meaning.

"By the equal union of ālī and kālī, the very possession by Vajrasattva." For the sake of those of childlike understanding, it is taught externally as the seat of sun and moon, fully transformed from ālī and kālī. In truth, it is said: "The syllables HŪṂ and PHAṬ, which arise from a letter and form a sphere, are not desired; one should contemplate the lord of the maṇḍala as arising from the reflection of the sattva." To contemplate the wheel of the selfless one in such a way is the definitive meaning. The meaning of this is also stated in the tantra called the Supreme Original Buddha: "By the union of ālī and kālī, the syllable HŪṂ is not desired. O King!" And in the fifth chapter of the Abridged Tantra explained by Mañjuvajra it is said: "Whichever of A, KA and the rest, the seat of hare and sun does not belong to the Vajra-holder. The letter transformed into HŪṂ, without sign, color, shape, and so forth, is not accepted here. Moreover, what arises from the unchanging, changes and is exhausted; it is endowed with the excellent faculty. Possessing the supreme of all aspects, the Victor holds manifold illusions."

The manner of the generation stage is as taught above: "Hand-emblems and faces as above; the moon, crystal, the light of a jewel." In this context it means that the hand-emblems are imagined as the curved knife and the skull cup, and all the bodily colors are imagined as pure black alone. This is clearly shown by this very text: "By the nature of means and wisdom, in this way everything is complete. The moon and sun are distinguished: ālī is wisdom, kālī is means. In this way Gaurī and the rest differ by their distinction of color." The meaning is that, in the manner of generation by the five outer and inner awakenings and so forth, the one is differentiated merely by color.

In order to show the distinctions of place: "First, in the inner additional circle, five yoginīs surely dwell. Their nature is the five aggregates; the yogic knowers should always contemplate them." This means that the sixteenth through the twentieth days are of the nature of the body. "In the east, Vajrā" means: in the eastern direction the goddess Vajrā; in the direction of Yama, Gaurī; in the direction of the lord of waters, the water yoginī; in the direction of Kubera, Vajraḍākinī; and in the center, the Blessed One, the selfless yoginī. These, gathered under the nature of body, are the goddesses of the five aggregates.

Now, in order to explain the outer additional circle, it begins "In the outer additional circle." "Gaurī, Caurī, Vetālī," and Ghasmarī: these four are placed in the four directions in order, being of the nature of speech and the essence of the twenty-fifth day. Likewise Pukkasī, Śavarī, Caṇḍālī, and Ḍombinī are placed at the intermediate points; from the twenty-sixth to the thirtieth day they are spoken of as the nature of mind, in a manner of complete purity.

"The one below and the one above, Khecarī and Bhūcarī, are spoken of." This means: the one below is the middle group of five, of the nature of speech; Khecarī is the third group of five, of the nature of mind. These two are the two kinds of life-breath of all embodied beings: the upper breath and the lower breath. From these two arose the tantras of means, such as the Guhyasamāja, and the yoginī tantras, such as the Hevajra. Of these, the tantras of means came from above, and the tantras of wisdom came from below. Therefore it was taught: "By the nature of existence and peace, these great compassionate ones abide."

"All the goddesses are dark in body color." This means that by indicating the symbol of wisdom, which is emptiness, their bodies are black. "Fierce, and adorned with the five mudrās." This means that all the goddesses, being of the nature of the pure groups of five days, are adorned: "The wheel, earrings, necklace, bracelets, and belt, these five aspects; the five buddhas also are pure, and by these five the mudrā is purified."

Again, it is said: "One-faced, with red eyes, in the hand she brandishes a curved knife and a skull cup." This shows the nature of existence and peace. As Nairātmyā is, so too the fourteen goddesses are one with her and not different. In this way: "As Nairātmyā the yoginī is, so all the others are explained." "One hand is occupied with the skull cup, in the right she holds the curved knife." The meaning of these words is to show that the colors, the hand emblems, the faces, and so on are not different from Nairātmyā herself.

"With the left she embraces the khaṭvāṅga, her lower body wrapped in a tiger skin, riding on a corpse, blazing with splendor." The definitive meaning of this has already been given above.

"Two hands, with yellow hair blazing upward." This is to show the aspect that appears as dual, for as long as it appears so. By the power of meditative absorption, all the elements blaze upward; therefore the hair is bound upward.

"The six faults, beginning with pride." These are the six, from the earth element up to the element of wisdom: the upward-moving and downward-moving winds, three engendered by the father and three engendered by the mother. These are the six that arise naturally in embodied beings.

"For the sake of cutting, the curved knife is held." In the definitive meaning, this is spoken of as emptiness, and it is said that by it the six faults are cut.

"The Māras of the four kinds of thoughts, of things and of non-things, their blood is to be drunk, for the sake of accomplishment, in the skull-cup of the head-lotus." The definitive meaning of this is as stated in the root tantra: "From the supreme lotus of one's own body, slain by the four meditative concentrations, the flesh and blood of the four Māras abide; the yogī should always drink it by the method of yoga, and will overcome birth and old age." The four states of cyclic existence are the Māras. Their emanations are the Māra of the aggregates, the Lord of Death in the heart, the Māra of the afflictions in the throat, and the Māra of the sons of the gods at the crown. Of the sixteen parts of the moon, four are slain each time; of the twenty-one thousand six hundred breaths, five thousand four hundred are slain. In this way the blood is drunk, and the conceptual thoughts of things and non-things are destroyed.

"The aspect of emptiness is the khaṭvāṅga; the corpse is conceived as method." This was explained at length earlier.

"If one meditates on the wheels by this means." It is said that when one understands the outer and the inner as provisional and definitive meaning and meditates accordingly, accomplishment is swiftly attained; this is the definitive meaning. The meaning of this too is given in the root tantra: "Having seen the three realms of beings caught in the net of conceptuality, the means of liberation for each, just as it applies, has been taught.
