Now the extensive commentary on the second chapter is to be taught. First, in order to present well the general characteristics of all consecrations, the text says "Vajragarbha requested," and so on.

The words "Blessed One" are an address to the Blessed One. "I am Vajragarbha, in whom all the buddhas are gathered as one." This means: I am Vajragarbha, whose very nature is the speech of all the buddhas gathered into one. In truth, the meaning is that he is the Lord of Secrets, permitted and praised by the Tathāgatas of the three times.

"Please explain in due order the excellent characteristics of consecration." This means: earlier the Blessed One taught the maṇḍala for the purpose of leading disciples into the maṇḍala; how should the consecration of what was taught be done?

The Blessed One declared: "Having made the fire offering as prescribed, and having raised the supreme maṇḍala..." Here, for the sake of a disciple of the mantra-holding master, and for one who wishes to consecrate a book, a relic, or a painted image, first one performs a fire offering such as the enriching one. Then one draws the colored-powder maṇḍala appropriate to the family. Then one abides in the preparatory stage. The buddhas dwelling in the realm of space are made to dissolve into the base of consecration, and that base of consecration is purified. Then, at the earlier stage, the form of the image is purified. As it is said: "Having abided in the preparatory stage, cause all the buddhas who dwell in space to enter fully into the heart of the image."

The definitive meaning of this is as the root tantra says: "In the preparatory stage granted by the guru, abide in the center of the body maṇḍala. Offer one's five aggregates in the fire offering, and cause the buddhas to dissolve in the form of seminal essence. One's five aggregates are purified as the form of the deity. The supreme blessing for consecration remains for a long time and is of great blessing. This is the supreme consecration." This is the definitive meaning.

"The yogin who is equal to the deity of his own choice has gone to the far shore of all mantras." This means: having performed the consecration in this manner, the yogin is equal to his chosen deity, Hevajra, and has reached the far shore of all mantras.

Oṃ vajra-puṣpe āḥ hūṃ svāhā is for the flowers. Oṃ vajra-dhūpe āḥ hūṃ svāhā, for the incense. Oṃ vajra-dīpe āḥ hūṃ svāhā, for the lamp. Oṃ vajra-gandhe āḥ hūṃ svāhā, for the perfume. Oṃ vajra-naivedye āḥ hūṃ svāhā, for the food offering.

Then the various offerings arise from HŪṂ, and flowers and the rest are presented. This is the order of rites in the higher tantra. The water for the face and the water for the feet are presented as above.

Here, what is presented after being blessed by one's own mantra is the Tathāgata himself. As for the definitive meaning, the root tantra says:

The pleasing flowers are the sun and moon;
the incense is moral discipline without matted locks;
the lamp is the clarity of bliss;
the supreme fragrance is satisfaction in bliss;
the body, speech and mind increase in bliss,
and absorption is the food, the supreme food offering;
gentle and pleasing speech, and one's own life-breath,
is the sweet sound of the cymbals.

This is the definitive meaning. Likewise, the water for bathing is the bliss of relying on the unspilled awakening mind, and the water for the feet is cooling, since it leads one to that very place of bliss.

Now, to teach the fire offering:

For pacifying, the fire pit is round,
at the forehead, the maṇḍala of water.
For enriching, it is square,
at the navel, the maṇḍala of earth.
For wrathful rites, it is triangular,
at the crown, the wheel whose nature is space.
In this very pit the rest is accomplished.

And for empowering, the fire pit is at the throat, belonging to the family of fire. "A cubit and a half, above and below the pacifying one" and so on, down to "above and below the wrathful one," is taught according to the sound of the words; it is not said to have a definitive meaning.

Now, to show the differences of color:

For pacifying the color becomes white,
for enriching yellow, and likewise so.
For wrathful rites the color is black,
for empowering red is well known.

It is also said that colors should be made different according to the differences of the rite. Therefore, in this context all the fire pits should be changed to those colors. And so the colors should differ according to the four wheels. Hence the white pacifying one belongs to the meditative state of water, at the forehead. The yellow enriching one belongs to the meditative state of earth, at the navel. The black wrathful one belongs to the meditative state of space, at the crown protuberance. The red empowering one belongs to the meditative state of fire, at the throat. Thus the sense is that, as with empowering one does magnetizing, and as with wrathful rites one does causing enmity and the like, the color and shape of the pit should not differ.

Now, to show the differences of the burnt substances:

For pacifying, sesame; for enriching, curds;
for wrathful rites, thorns, and likewise so.
For causing enmity and the like also thorns;
for empowering and magnetizing, utpala.

In order, then: for pacifying one first burns sesame; for enriching, first curds; for wrathful rites, first thorny wood is burned. Likewise for causing enmity and the like, first thorny wood. For empowering and magnetizing, first utpala is burned. The rest accords with the other rites.

Oṃ asnaye mahā-tejaḥ, sarva-kāmārtha-sādhakaḥ, kāruṇa-kṛtvā sattvārtha, asmin sannihito bhava. This is the mantra for inviting the fire god, both worldly and transcendent. Inwardly, the invitation is from the navel.

"I hold various jewels. O Hevajra, goddess who is offered to in wrath, you will come under my power. I draw my maṇḍala, O Cege-mo." These are words spoken at the very beginning, when one searches for a site for the fire offering and finds ground free of disease, requesting the earth goddess to be witness and guarantor.

Svārthañ ceva, parārthañ ca, sādhi-dhaṃ gaccha hyabhuka, āgamiṣyasi, yathā kāle, sarva-siddhi kuruṣā me. This is a praise to please the deity, by way of accomplishing the aims of both oneself and others.

Jaḥ HŪṂ BAṂ HOḤ. A kha raṃ. Oṃ ni ri HŪṂ khaṃ. Oṃ dhvaṃ dhvaṃ. The mantras such as these, in order, belong to the offering water, the water for the feet, and the food offerings, and they should be distinguished as outer and inner.

The definitive meaning of the Hevajra ḍākinī net and the vows has already been explained earlier. This is the first, the extensive explanation of the chapter on consecration, the second examination, from the extensive explanation of the definitive meaning of Hevajra.

Having bowed to the Sage, the supreme being,
who destroys attachment, aversion and delusion,
craving and envy,
by which chapter is the establishing of accomplishment
extensively explained?
For beings confused in the darkness of suffering,
the extensive explanation of that
will be written by Vajragarbha.

Now I shall explain the chapter that well teaches the accomplishments, worldly and transcendent.

Vajragarbha asked:

From phenomena that are like space,
like the waves of the ocean,
how will beings come to accomplishment
from the form of the deity of one's choice?

The meaning is this. The Blessed One has said again and again, at the beginning, the end and throughout, that phenomena are like space. Vajragarbha asked: "If I have understood, these beings are produced by their own conceptual thought. Just as when someone casts a thousand waves upon the great ocean and they drift without settling in any one place, so these beings abide, set in motion by their own conceptual thought. By what means of practice will they come to accomplishment?"

The Blessed One said:

One who possesses the yoga of Nairātmyā,
or the glorious, diligent Heruka,
should not, desiring accomplishment,
dwell for even an instant with any other mind.

This means: O Vajragarbha, though it is so, the yogī who stands in the first stage of the work enters the maṇḍala as taught, and at the time of generating the pride of the deity, and then, when that is fully complete, if he dwells in the yoga of Nairātmyā, he will certainly become Hevajra. Therefore, one desiring accomplishment should not dwell for even an instant with any other mind, apart from relying on the two stages.

Moreover, the beginner first dwells clinging to color and shape. When that has reached its end, he should train in the yoga of Nairātmyā. And what is she like?

Just as space is not constantly present
in all its directions,
so the Lord of Secret Mantra
does not dwell in any phenomena.
Just as things dwell in space
and are always visible,
yet space does not become their dwelling,
so too is the Lord of Secret Mantra.

Like space, and the three times, and the rest, which one wishes to be free of conditions, so too the Lord of Secret Mantra appears even though the three times do not exist. He is only a place of mere designation, free of any agent and the like. The wise apply the conventional term "space" to space. Apart from the name, nothing exists at all, just as with space. So too the Lord of Secret Mantra is no more than a conventional appearance.

He is not fire, not water, not wind, not earth, not the sun, not the moon or any of the planets, not day and not night. He is not birth and not aging, not death and not decline. He is not a moment, not an instant, not the years and the rest. Destruction and formation do not exist, nor does any count of aeons. Nor does any fruit that arises from virtue and non-virtue exist.

Whoever wishes for the state of omniscience, in the midst of the many kinds of conceptual thought, should always strive at that in which all these are absent. Thus it was taught.

Therefore, those who are able to remain constantly in this yoga of Nairātmyā always see Hevajra.

When one first begins to practice, the place that is praised, where the mind can be settled in one-pointed equipoise and accomplishment arises, is declared to be an excellent place. This means that the beginner, since kings, thieves, enemies and the like may at first cause interruptions, should seek out a secluded place and there be single-pointed; this is the certainty of the Tathāgatas.

At night, in one's own house, with the confidence of one who has already accomplished, one meditates on the yoginī, the wise one, or else on Heruka, of glorious form. This means that one may imagine, as in the night, a beloved companion of one's own, profoundly cultivated through conceptual imagining, and lead her to the place of meditation and remain there; or else, in the manner of the hero Heruka, one should remain with Nairātmyā, who arises from oneself. Such is the meaning. Thus, in a summarized way, it was taught: washing the feet, eating, and so on, up to the words "by one who keeps the discipline of wisdom."

In brief: whatever one focuses on closely, make that Hevajra. In any other manner, one should not remain even for an instant.

"Meditate on the yoginī" refers to the wisdom-yoginī taught above. Why is that which is by nature empty called "wisdom"? As it was said: that which is called the emergence of wisdom is known as samādhi; by it the mudrā is quickly accomplished, and if one meditates, it will be accomplished quickly. Thus it was taught. Therefore: in any other way, even for an instant, a malicious mind, through ignorance, is something that the wise, wishing for accomplishment, strive not to remain in for the sake of accomplishment. This should be understood from what was taught. By this it is shown that all the qualities of a buddha depend on diligence.

As for the fortunate one, once the yoga of generation is complete, he is gathered in by the first of the six limbs, withdrawal, the fire of correct wisdom, and when he practices it, these signs will arise. But if one does not wish for the true path, then even for the sake of a spectacle, one should rest one-pointedly in focus for half a month, or else for one day and night, and look. Thus it was taught, as follows:

The meditation that destroys the afflictions I, Vajragarbha, have explained. Out of love, and for the sake of accomplishment, understand it in half a month.

Here is taught the meditation on selflessness, which destroys all the poison of the arrows of latent tendencies. The meaning is: "O Vajragarbha, whose nature is love, realize it in half a month, even as a spectacle."

If one does not make an effort even for half a month, then even so:

Abandoning all thoughts, thinking of the form of the deity, without interruption for a day and night, meditate and examine.

This was taught. Those who lack the fortune to make an effort of half a month at this yoga of Nairātmyā should give up striving at other objects of focus, even for a single day and night, and if they place the mind one-pointedly on emptiness, the signs of the path that inspire confidence will arise. This holds for those gathered in by withdrawal; otherwise, children of confused wisdom would come to despise the word of the Tathāgata. Therefore, apart from the samādhi of withdrawal, meditating for a day and night while abandoning all thoughts does not bring about confidence.

"For the thorough accomplishment of one's own and others' aims, there is no other means in saṃsāra." This means that, in the definitive sense, apart from this samādhi, the aims of oneself and others are not accomplished.

"Having once cultivated the woman of awareness, one has confidence at once." By this it is meant that such a samādhi, which destroys all afflictions, is indicated by the kindness of the guru, through a woman who is for the time being designated by the name "wisdom"; otherwise there is no fortune of finding it.

For those who have thus directly found it, and later abandoned it and focus on the form of a deity, it was taught: terror and madness, likewise suffering, being tormented by grief, and strong attachment, aversion and delusion are not afflictions for the practitioner. That is, he is not utterly tormented by worldly conditions.

Moreover, likewise: since it is known with certainty now that the fruits of benefit and harm will arise, why should yogis remain in lamentation for even an instant? This was taught. For one who realizes things as pure by nature, how could lamentation and torment arise? That is not a place for it, and there is no occasion for it. Such is the meaning.

Likewise, there is yet another teaching on what one should abide in: those who commit the acts of immediate retribution, those who delight in killing living beings, those of low birth, the deluded, those who do evil deeds, those whose bodies are deformed or incomplete, if they meditate, these will be accomplished. This was taught. Even these people, if they meditate on Nairātmyā, will assuredly attain the accomplishment of Hevajra; this is the certainty of the Tathāgatas.

Elsewhere also it was taught thus: outcastes, washerwomen, and the like, those who commit the five acts of immediate retribution, having practiced the conduct of mantra, become buddhas in this very life.

Now, those who are beginners in the practice should first, at the time of entering the guru's following, be taught by these lines: "One who is practiced in the ten virtues, devoted to the guru, with the senses tamed, and free from pride and anger, will for the present be certain to succeed." Accordingly, the time of the ruin is to be understood through the words "First the restoration ceremony should be taught," and so on. They should practice as the tantra says in following the guru, and it is said that they will be certain not to incur the root downfalls and will be a suitable vessel. So these are what one should train in first.

After that, one trains in what is called "guiding the childish worldly ones" and abides in it. Later, the vajra master examines the consort, or else one takes the mudrā of an action that a deity has prophesied. At the time of guiding the childish, it is said: "By the yoga of constant familiarity, through meditative equipoise one attains accomplishment. For one month, practice in secret, as long as a consort has not been found." This means that as long as permission for a consort has not been found, one practices in secret, abiding in the equipoise taught above. This may last a month, or a year, and so forth, but the minimum, one month of training, is fixed.

"When permission is found, the yoginī should instruct the mantrin. Taking the consort, a Cegemo, one holds the vajra for the benefit of beings." In such passages the meaning is this: if she is indicated in a dream, or if she has been well examined by the master, one takes her and engages in the practice. That it becomes for the benefit of beings is the certainty of the Tathāgatas.

As for the one who is found: "Wide-eyed, adorned with beauty and youth, endowed with Siṃhala camphor, purified by the awakening mind." This teaches the characteristics of the consort. She has eyes whose white and red are distinct and somewhat wide, a waist like a vajra, and is twelve or sixteen years old, at the time of the onset of menstrual blood, delighting in the bliss of the awakening mind. Such a consort too is to be purified, though purification by introducing the awakening mind comes later. Before that: "Beginning from the ten virtues, the Dharma is taught to her; she contemplates herself in the form of the deity, and the commitment and mind are one." And it is said: "Any woman who is not purified and is joined to Hevajra violates the commitment and is ruined; therefore the Dharma should be taught to purify them."

Therefore, having trained the woman well and having her imagine herself as Nairātmyā, one leads her to a secluded place. Training in the bliss of the ruin comes at the end of the month. Hence: "If one finds a supreme woman free of all conceptual thought, within one month one will become fortunate; of this there is no doubt." On this: having found such a woman well, one settles in the center of the assembly, prepares many kinds of food and drink, songs, cymbals and dances, adorns the body well with ornaments, flowers and perfumes, arranged well in an auspicious place, and makes true wisdom appear.

Or else: "By one's own power, one summons and examines consorts from among gods, asuras, humans, yakṣas, and kinnaras." This is taught thus: for accomplishing the success of the consort, the daughters of gods, of asuras, and of humans, four kinds, are summoned by mantra from everywhere and practiced with throughout the whole maṇḍala of the earth. Thereby the fortunate yogī will accomplish the consort without fail. The body of a woman who is unclean, rotten, and stained is not suitable. This is the meaning of the words.

"Take her and engage in the practice, having made one's own leisure manifest. The conduct taught by those of terrifying form is not taught for the sake of enjoyment. Examine individually your own mind, whether it is steady or wavering." Concerning this: "This is the greedy childish one who acts carelessly with intoxicating drink, who abandons the nectar drink, and clings intensely to changing pleasure; who makes a living by mantra and consorts. This improper conduct, together with the empowerments and the all-pervading conduct, is said to be a cause of relying on one's own mind. Those who rely on mere convention and perform the ten non-virtuous actions, what destinies they will have, how could even a guide know?" Such is the meaning.

Now, to show that future childish beings become buddhas by means of the two faculties, women too should practice the empowerment and the conduct. In order to show how the accomplishment and conduct of the inner consort are to be taught, the question was asked. Vajragarbha asked: "How is the consort spoken of by one endowed with the yoga of Nairātmyā? By the two consorts, how does the accomplishment of the consort come about?" The meaning is: how should a woman endowed with the yoga of Nairātmyā accomplish the bliss of the consort? And if it arises only from a woman, then the accomplishment of method would be lacking. So he asked.

The Blessed One declared: "Abandoning the form of a woman, make it the form of the Blessed One." This means that women beginners should entirely give up the conception of themselves as women and put their faith in themselves as Hevajra in person. Likewise: "Abandoning the breasts, they become a bola; the kakkola is rightly placed in the center; the two sides become bells." Any woman who enters into the power of bliss transforms herself into a male like Hevajra, discards the white breast-bearing form and turns it into the bola, and has faith in it with her mind. Thus, at the time of bliss, she who has the male form is endowed with the kakkola; likewise the young woman transforms the two sides of her own source into bells in her faith. And likewise: "The clitoris is transformed into the bola." This is stated to indicate that the object abiding in the center of the lotus is to be generated and examined as the rain of the secret.

Therefore the remainder is the great self itself, the form of the great joy, Heruka. Heruka is the very person of union. Regarding this, it is said that the elements, the sense sources, the remainder, and the rest are of equal taste with one another, being alike. Without any need for transformation, the person is Heruka himself; there is no difference. Thus, from transformation through devotion, the supreme accomplishment of the seal is purified, and it comes to women. So the great self himself has declared. Without any effort the practitioner becomes a man. Through the yogin who is able to make it clear, the accomplishment of the seal then comes about.

This too was spoken: "The yoginī who practices union in this way, having completely exchanged her womanhood, may even obtain in this very life a man's body, handsome and perfect in every excellence." Moreover, in order that monks who hold the vajra may abandon the faults of the worldly, the fact that some young women are transformed into men and become companions in practice is the Tathāgata's definitive teaching. In this way the supreme accomplishment of the seal is attained.

Then: "Neither attachment nor destruction harms means and wisdom. That from which means arises is itself; destruction is wisdom, which ends existence. Therefore there is no true destruction." Regarding this, by such a progression: the yogin who clearly establishes wisdom and means as nondual cannot be harmed when fire, water, and the rest destroy the world. This habitual tendency toward destruction and formation is one's own imputation. This conceptualization of self and other as parts agitates the three worlds. It does not make this clarity disturbed. If one safeguards well the formation and destruction of the world of awakening mind, the great yogin is never destroyed; he moves everywhere like one governed by a wheel. He has no attachment to that itself. Some are destroyed by destruction, but there is no destructible thing and no exhaustion. This means that when the stream of awakening mind is destroyed, all destruction arises.

From the yoga of the generation stage, one meditates the elaboration of the one who keeps the vow. Having made elaboration like a dream, one makes elaboration itself free of elaboration. Regarding this, beginning from a single deity up to a hundred thousand, one should train in the manner taught in each of the tantras, strive, and have faith. As it is said: "Whatever color is taught by whomever, that itself is the maṇḍala of that deity; the forms of the deities are shown thus by the buddhas of the past." Therefore one should strive at the very beginning in the yoga of elaboration.

And as it is said: "Beings are of lesser intellect, deluded by mere activity, and attached to and delighting in the objects of things. Such will arise in the future. Not knowing this manner, because of place, time, and activity, deluded by the signs of virtue and nonvirtue, they desire results. Therefore the great beings have taught this ritual." So it is said.

"Just as when a stream of rain falls, bubbles of water arise, so too the various manifestations of mantra accomplishment have no sentient being, no life, and no agent. All things being empty, only delusion appears." By this one should realize the completion stage.

"Just as illusion, dream, and the intermediate state of existence are, so too is the maṇḍala regarded, through constant familiarization in practice." Regarding this, this completion is just this maṇḍala of one's own deity; like illusion, dream, and the being of the intermediate state, meditating free of attachment and obstruction is itself regarded as completion. Because it is the separate abandoning of desires, it is called "separate abandoning," and so on, as is extensively taught.

"By the empowerment of the great seal, the great bliss that is known thus: this is its blessing. No maṇḍala arises from anything else." That is, knowing things as like illusion, dream, and so on is itself what is called the great seal, the empowerment that liberates from worldly conduct, the Great Illusory Mother, and the Perfection of Wisdom.

For the sake of knowing this itself: "Black bliss, yellow bliss, red bliss, white bliss, green bliss, dedicated bliss, bliss moving and unmoving, all of it; wisdom bliss and means bliss." This and what follows is spoken. From the distinction of provisional and definitive meaning, in order to abandon attachment to elaboration, one generates the maṇḍala colors again and again. Activities and the rest also come to be accomplished, and these too are the six great Vajradharas. Otherwise, "wisdom, the great fire of gnosis," and so on, is taught as the sign of the path, as smoke and the like, because the bliss of withdrawal itself appears in that way. For while in bliss, at the very time of the seed's bliss, whatever form, color, moving or unmoving is observed is not seen as other than bliss.

Likewise: "Bliss that arises everywhere; bliss that is real and bliss that is unreal; bliss is declared to be Vajrasattva." Therefore, for those burned by the fire of desire, the bliss of universal arising is taught as supreme, because by its nature of being real and unreal it brings about the bliss of Vajrasattva. For those who do not waver, it is the bliss of Vajrasattva as it truly is.

Now, to resolve the intention of the statement, "Elaboration is like a dream, and the completion stage should be known," Vajragarbha asks: "This yoga of the completion stage, its bliss is called great bliss. Since there is no meditation on completion, what need is there of generation?" Regarding this, the meaning is: O Blessed One, if buddhahood is through the bliss of the two faculties alone, there is no other meditation of completion; so what need is there of a maṇḍala other than that, or of binding seals?

The Blessed One, the great Hevajra, heard those words and said:

Ema! Great bodhisattva, listen with the force of faith.

If the conventional body had no reality at all, there would be no power to teach what is called great bliss. For that reason he spoke the lines beginning "From what does bliss arise, if the body has no reality?" The meaning of the lines beginning "By what delusion do I not know?" is taught at length in the chapter that settles method and wisdom with certainty. Thus, if the body itself were nonexistent, the bliss that depends upon it would not exist either. Therefore it is said:

The bhaga of the vajra consort,
Its form the shape of the syllable E,
The buddha-jewel, a flower of enjoyment,
Ever dwells within the blissful one.

And again:

Consorts of the four castes and the rest
Give birth to a wondrous daughter.
The bhaga, the place of birth, has the form of E;
The male organ has the shape of the syllable Vaṃ.
Their union is known as the secret place,
And the semen that abides in their midst
The guide has called "buddha."

Thus, so long as there is the body of man and woman, there is great bliss.

The lines "The speaker am I, the Dharma am I, the assembly of listeners am I, the world's teacher and the one to be accomplished am I, the world and the beyond-world am I" refute those foolish people who suppose that, in the buddha, a consort, sensual pleasure, a gathered retinue, reliance on the Dharma, a teacher, listeners and so on, and the teaching of desire must definitely exist. It is to be understood that the sport of the buddha is shown so that future beings may realize it, and that in truth the buddha has a body like a vajra and is unmoving like an elephant. Even so, it is taught that the buddha arises from one's own body; for the vajra-like body, endowed with the major and minor marks, is likewise born in dependence on semen.

The text says:

The nature of innate joy,
Ending in the supreme of the first, free of joy,
Like a lamp in the darkness,
So too, though done, trust it with confidence.

This holy method, when one unites with the supreme consort and relies on the guru's instruction, is illustrated like a lamp lighting up darkness in the end of the first, supreme joy that is free of attachment. Thus:

The bhaga of the consort, the blissful one,
Is marked with the thirty-two signs of the Teacher.
The lord, endowed with the eighty minor marks,
Dwells in the form called semen.

The definitive meaning is also given in the root tantra:

"The lord adorned with the eighty minor marks and the rest is semen," it says.
With bliss that is stable and unemitting,
In ethical discipline that is without lapse,
Taking hold of the limit of the four dhyānas,
This radiant flower of the mind,
Not carried off by the wind,
Is bound, and so comes to bear the minor marks.

So it is taught in its definitive meaning.

Thus, without a body there is no semen; and without semen, how could bliss be perceived at all? Then even what is called buddha would not exist. Hence it is said:

Without that, there is no bliss;
Without bliss, there is no that.
Being without power, they depend on each other.

This was certainly taught by the Tathāgata.

Therefore what is called the perfect buddha is free from all conventions of existence and nonexistence. He has no face or color and so on, and is not a form of bliss. The four joys, the four wheels, the four moments, the four results, the four empowerments, the four stages, and so on; the bliss of man and woman; the channels, the winds, the drops, the fragment of the moon, and sounds and words and so on, none of these is the buddha. Whatever was taught in these ways was taught for beings hard to tame. For that reason the bliss of the two organs was also taught, and the phrase "the nature of the innate buddha" does not refer to the innate nature of semen; rather, the innate that abides as nature is what is called buddha.

Bliss comes from the yoga of the deity;
Therefore the buddha is not a thing,
Nor is he the manner of a nonthing.
Having the form of faces and arms,
He is supreme bliss, without form,
Innate to all beings alike.

Because bliss pervades everything, and because the innate nature pervades everything, he spoke thus. To bring about the realization of these things, seeing no other means, he praised this very form of the conventional as supreme. Hence the bliss that arises from effort is not innate, since it is adventitious, like autumn flowers and the like. For this reason too he said, "It is called the innate nature." What is other than that is a mere illustration, given to lead the childish into the teaching.

As for the line "By a mind of pure aspect, its nature is nirvāṇa itself": this is because the generation stage is by nature pure from the very beginning. Then what kind of samādhi is the generation stage to perform? He spoke the lines:

In the form of the deity's body,
Faces, arms, and colors abide;
They remain merely by being generated,
Yet with ordinary habitual tendencies.

Thus, although from the beginning it is by its very nature free, since the childish have no fortune to realize this, by showing the deity with faces, arms, and colors through ordinary habitual tendencies, the childish become liberated, and not otherwise.

Likewise, examples are plainly visible in the world:

By a mere fragment of poison,
All beings come to die.

This is directly established for worldly people. Likewise:

One who knows the very nature of poison
Destroys poison with poison.

This too is directly established for worldly people. For some person who knows poison mantras and medicines blesses a portion of poison and eats it, and it does no harm; and further, it becomes a basis for benefit in certain illnesses.

In the same way, other powers are also plainly visible. As it is said:

For example, when a wind disorder arises,
one eats a dish of lentils and beans;
but by examining the remedy that reverses it,
wind is used to strike down wind.

What this means is as follows. If someone afflicted by a wind disorder is given a broth of lentils and beans as food and drink, the illness will for a time grow worse. Afterward, a physician who knows how to destroy that very illness has butter and the like clarified seven times, mixes it with the powder of the three fruits, and gives it as food, and so uproots the wind disorder.

Just as in this example, it is said:

By conceptual thought, conceptual thought,
and by existence itself, existence is overcome.

That is, the meditative absorption of the generation stage, which is by nature conceptual, first makes use of distinctive worldly conventions: the binding of hand gestures, the manner of sitting, the manner of gazing, the special features of the rosary, and so on. Afterward, through the qualities of what follows, the earlier ones are abandoned. This is the established order of each of the various tantras. Likewise, the Condensed Tantra itself says:

Joy, freedom from joy, and joy again are existence;
therefore meditate on the innate.

And again it is said:

For example, when water has entered the ear,
it is removed by other water.

When some drops of water remain deep in the ear canal, pouring in more water makes them come out again; this is plainly established for ordinary people. In the same way:

So too the conceptual thought about things
is to be purified by conceptual thought of the mind.

That is, apart from the absorption of elaboration, the imagined dharma body, and the imagined parts of the body as the forms of deities, which have the character of existence, there is no means of becoming free. Another example plainly visible in the world is this:

For example, when one is burned by fire,
one is scorched again by fire as before.

When a limb is burned by fire, some foolish and unskilled people at that very instant touch something unsuitable, such as water, and so do further harm. In the same way:

Thus one burned by the fire of desire
is scorched again by the fire of desire.
By whatever unbearable deeds
beings are bound,
by those very same, together with skillful means,
they are freed from the bonds of existence.

The meaning of this is also given in the root tantra:

Desire and the other afflictions
bind all people, it is said.
What the hero and guide taught
was spoken of the person without skillful means.
For the person who has skillful means,
by that very thing he is freed,
as the great lord of sweetness again declared.
The means for this are these.

That is, those people of the three worlds who are attached are rendered unworthy even of their own splendid possessions by desire itself, and what keeps them in saṃsāra is just this. Since they lack reality, they are like someone riding a maddened elephant without a mahout holding the iron hook. Therefore it is said:

The world is bound by attachment,
and by desire itself it is liberated.

Through emission one is bound, whereas through the inexhaustible bliss without emission one is freed. Thus, wherever the Tathāgata spoke in blame, he meant the emitting kind; wherever he spoke in praise, he meant just such unchanging bliss. This very unchanging attachment is not the domain of the yoga of the immature. As it is said:

This meditation of reversal
is not known to buddhists or non-Buddhists.

Why? Because in an instant it becomes the nature of the Tathāgatas. Hence it is said:

Because they are of the nature of the five elements.

For in the very instant that desire arises, it takes on the nature of the five elements. How so?

All become five.

This is the time of union. It is said:

From one single great joy
it comes to divide into five kinds.

At the time of a single great joy, luminous like space, five distinct phases arise in the conventional world. As it is said:

By the union of bo la and kakkola.

This refers to the time of the joining of vajra and lotus. And where:

The very latency of hardness,
hardness is delusion, for the sake of the Dharma;
delusion is called Vairocana.

For in that instant there is solidity, and hardness is the earth element; therefore that instant is called Vairocana. Likewise:

The awakening mind, being moist,
is declared to be the water element.
Aversion, the guide, is Akṣobhya.

Akṣobhya is the deed, and the defining mark of moistness is the water element. Likewise, at the time of the union of the two, the heat that arises is Amitābha, Boundless Light:

Because desire arises from heat.

That heat of desire is the fire element. Likewise:

In kakkola, whatever is the mind,
that is of the nature of wind.
Envy is Amoghasiddhi;
Amogha arises from wind.

The moving and falling of the drop at that time is the work of wind, and since wind accomplishes all aims, it is called as it is. Further:

The desire of bliss becomes blood.
Joy is the defining mark of space.
Space, avarice, becomes Vajra;
avarice arises from space.

In whatever instant, the bliss that arises in dependence on blood is the defining mark of Ratnasambhava, and the precious jewel is a quality of space. Thus wherever the single great mind of bliss is found, it certainly abides in the form of the five, and because it arises as the nature of the five families, it is not understood by buddhists or non-Buddhists, who grow frightened as before. And so:

From just those five families
thousands upon thousands are born.

From those five principal families arise ordinary families beyond counting. Therefore the desires of desire too should be known as immeasurable. And those five families should also be shown as having the qualities of the throat, the palate, the crown, the lips, and the teeth.

Here it is. The vowels a, i, ṛ, u, and ḷ arise from five places of articulation and are regarded as five families. When these are modified by the vowel gradations, by augmentation, and by the suffix yaṇ, and are then divided into long and short, they can be multiplied without limit. To give just a brief indication: these things abide as part of method and part of wisdom, and these in turn are gathered into just the vowel series and the consonant series. The vowel series is five, taught as the five letters that arise from five places of articulation, exactly as stated above. When these same five are marked with the dot, they show the part of method. Six arise in the same way, and likewise the five are augmented by being cut off from those, so that there are again six, and these show the part of wisdom. In the same way, the consonants of some are thirty. Thus, when the thirty consonants are brought together with the six letters of method, they become one hundred and eighty, in one course. Again, when the thirty are brought together with the six letters of wisdom, they become one hundred and eighty, just as before, and so there are two courses. The two sets of one hundred and eighty, mingled in the manner of the non-duality of method and wisdom, make three hundred and sixty. Inwardly, this is the breath of one element; outwardly, it is the days of one year. When three hundred and sixty is multiplied by the five elements, it is eighteen hundred. And when this is multiplied by the twelve transits, that is, by the twelve months and twelve houses, it stands as what is called twenty-one thousand six hundred. Thus all the families come to arise from the five families. In this way, the first sixty of the twelve years, and likewise the months, days, hours, minutes, and breaths, when multiplied by the five kinds of elements, should be understood as families beyond counting. And so:

For this reason these are of one nature,
supremely beneficial, great bliss.
Through the five minds, beginning with desire,
the division goes into five.
The sands of ten Ganges rivers,
in one family, are the assemblies of tathāgatas.
In the assembly of families, there are many families;
in these families, there are a hundred kinds of families;
and in these again, hundreds of thousands of great families.
In the tens of millions of families, the number becomes unlimited.
Among them, the families without number
arise from the family of supreme joy.

This was spoken. Great bliss is the family of space, producing supreme benefit for all embodied beings. This uncontrived, innate reality has no other means of being known except the bliss of the union of the two organs, and so it is certainly secret from śrāvakas, solitary buddhas, and non-Buddhists.

This concludes the second chapter of the extensive explanation of the definitive meaning of the Hevajra, the extensive explanation of the chapter called "Establishing Accomplishment."

I bow to the syllables Evaṃ,
from which flows the continuum of the four joys.
The introduction of the tantra, in the secret language,
Vajragarbha writes as an extensive explanation.

Then the text says, "Then, to the yoginīs of the Vajra family, the method called the introduction to all the tantras was spoken," and so on. Here, the tantra is of four kinds: the teacher, the secret, wisdom, and gnosis. These four kinds are the tantra. The introduction refers to the history, the place, and the inseparable conduct. These methods are connected with the words "were spoken by the Blessed One." Likewise, in other places:

The vow, the empowerment,
likewise the symbolic language,
the joys and the division of the instants,
and other matters such as food.
The vow concerning these was spoken.

This is said as an indication. The Hevajra is the example. In the same way, for the benefit of future beings, the Blessed One spoke on all of these: what the vow of all the tantras is, what the empowerment is, how many joys there are, what the division of the instants is, and what the food of the yogin is like.

Concerning this:

The vow of all the buddhas
is firmly established as Evaṃ.
The great bliss that has the form of Evaṃ
becomes truly known through empowerment.

This meaning is also stated in the root tantra itself:

After I have passed into nirvāṇa,
so that śrāvakas and children may understand,
to Vajrapāṇi and the others,
at the beginning of the Dharma, the word Evaṃ.
"Be seated," he said, this is the provisional meaning.
I do not pass into nirvāṇa.
I have a body like a vajra.
At all times I abide,
and to children I show the passing away.

Such is the introduction of provisional meaning. As for the definitive meaning:

Evaṃ means father and mother.
Evaṃ is shown as sun and moon.
Evaṃ is blood and semen themselves.
Evaṃ is the right and the left.
Evaṃ is declared to be the liṅga and the source of birth.
Evaṃ is method and wisdom.
Evaṃ is the basis of all happiness and suffering.
E is the navel, vaṃ the crown.

This is the definitive meaning. Then the text says, "Then the yoginīs said this to the Blessed One Vajrasattva," which shows the literal sense.

Blessed Teacher, supreme among beings,
the very vow of the ḍākinīs,
what is the form of Evaṃ?
Please explain it in its proper order.

The meaning is this. The ḍākinīs, having summoned the Blessed One Hevajra, who has the nature of Vajrasattva, said: "We do not know the vow of the bliss of the ḍākinīs, nor how the meaning of the syllables Evaṃ is. Please explain this to us." Intending to answer, the Blessed One spoke, "What is the excellent form of E?" and so on. Thus: you yoginīs, beginning with Nairātmyā and Leaf-Clad One of the charnel ground, listen. E is the great secret of all the buddhas. It is within the middle of your own bodies, yoginīs, abiding in the form of a blazing triangle. In its center there dwells something like an excellent vase, which draws forth all the happiness of the three existences, adorned with a wish-fulfilling tree as its ornament and filled with stainless water.

Daughters of good family, this is
the excellent form of E,
adorned in its center by the form of Vaṃ,
the abode of all happiness,
called the treasure chest of buddha-jewels.

This is the basis and support of all the happiness of the three worlds, the abode of the vow of bliss of the yoginīs, and the foundation for the ascetics to guard their moral discipline. It is also called the palace of the jewel of bliss of the buddhas.

Therefore, even those who are beginners in the practice and who wish to attain buddhahood through the bliss of the union of the two should rely on this basis. If they divide it by means of the four moments and enter into it according to the guru's instruction, it is said that in this very life they will attain the state of the great Vajradhara. With this in mind the Blessed One also said:

From distinguishing the moments,
joy arises from that.
Knowing the moments, one knows bliss;
it is fully established in Evaṃ.

This shows the introduction and the summary. Now, in order to teach the four moments, the four joys, and so on, the text says: "The variegated, the ripening, the culminating, and the one free of characteristics." As the root tantra says:

By the difference between the forward order and the reverse order,
thirty-two variegated moments abide.
When there are sixteen ripenings,
from the abiding of eight comes the culminating itself.
Of the sixteen, four abide as free of characteristics.
Earth and water and fire and wind
should be known in order.
The forward order of arising is as follows:
it should be known as moving on the left.
The reverse order is the opposite of that;
thirty-two abide as free of characteristics.
It should be known as moving on the right.
Dependent origination is forward arising, and its reverse is the opposite.
The monk, the arhat, has exhausted the crag;
by eliminating attachment he has tamed the senses.

So it is said. Even so, "if the four moments are well understood, the yogī will know that very reality." For without knowing the distinctions among the four moments one does not arrive at suchness, since it is said, "As it is outside, so it is within."

"Embracing, kissing, and so on, are explained as the various variegated forms." That is, the moment of embracing is variegated. Likewise kissing, smiling, and so on, and scratching with the nails, biting, and the like, are all characteristics of the variegated. Ripening occurs afterward. Before it comes the culminating, which is the actual eating of the wisdom of bliss. Because bliss has been eaten, it is called ripening. It is also said, "the moment of the culminating is taken in."

The one free of characteristics is different from the other three, for it is free of eating, having eaten, and being taken in. This was taught mixed up within the tantra in order to prevent people from entering at will without a guru. Therefore it says:

The ripening is the reverse of that.
The wisdom of bliss is the eating itself;
the eating of bliss is by oneself.
Being taken in is called the culminating.
Being free of characteristics, it is other than the three.
Attachment and freedom from attachment are fully abandoned.

Thus it was spoken in the reversed manner in vajra words.

Now the four joys, which accompany the four moments, are explained:

The first, the variegated, is the joy itself;
it is shown at the wheel of the forehead.
At the eyebrows is the supreme joy;
it is shown at the wheel of the throat.
The culminating is the distinct joy;
it is declared at the wheel of the heart.
The one free of characteristics is the innate joy;
it is shown at the wheel of the navel.
By reversing the sequence, as before,
being free of characteristics is great bliss.
The master, the secret, and wisdom,
and the fourth is again the same.

Here: the water and the rest, ending with the vase, are the master's empowerment for introducing the child. The joy gained by receiving the secret is called the secret empowerment. Touching wisdom is the wisdom empowerment. The fourth is other than the three; since it says "likewise, again likewise," the fourth is not the third, and should be examined as something else. In the definitive sense this is explained as the empowerment of the vajra master. Thus the four empowerments are established as the worldly and the transworldly, the provisional and the definitive.

"The empowerments, by the count of four, should be known in the sequence of joy and so on." This should be understood as before, by the difference between forward and reverse order.

The joy gained through laughing:
in the laughing, the master himself.
The joy gained through gazing:
the gazing is the secret, likewise.
The joy gained through holding hands:
in holding hands, wisdom itself.
Bliss gained through giving of the two to the two:
the two embracing the two, likewise.
Thus the empowerments are of four kinds,
by the distinction of outer and inner.
The wise vajra master
should show them to the supreme student.
For the accomplishment of sentient beings
the empowerments are explained as fourfold.
By what is called scattering and pouring
this is to be called empowerment.

It is an empowerment because it purifies and washes, that is, it washes away the stains of the burning torment of the afflictions.

Now, in order to teach the inner empowerment of the master:

On one who has reached sixteen years of wisdom,
let him wash well with the hands.
By joining the vajra and bell together,
it is declared the master's empowerment.

That is: to one possessing the sixteen portions of the waxing moon of wisdom, through the joining of one's own vajra and its bell, bliss is certainly the master's empowerment. Likewise:

With a very fair face and wide eyes,
adorned with form and youth,
with the thumb and ring finger
let it be dropped into the student's mouth.

That is, the substance produced from such union purifies the path of the student's speech; this is the secret. After that the vajra master, having fully honored that wisdom consort with food, drink and so on, should entrust her to the student with these words:

Great being, you
take this mudrā endowed with bliss.
Having abandoned jealousy and anger,
knowing you will become a great student,
the Teacher gives him permission.

That is, he commands: "Son of the family, take this mudrā from whom arises the bliss of earth, water, fire and wind, and make offerings to her." Furthermore, "Samantabhadra-like (Kun tu ru), hold the vajra." By this, the distinctive union of the kunduru should also be well shown to that student.

Likewise, the student should say this: "Having seen the guru together with the mudrā, one should offer praise and worship properly." As follows:

Blessed Great Peaceful One (Śiva), kye!
Vajra yogī, as you wish,
unseparated one, accomplisher of the mudrā,
arising from the vajra yoga,
as your own nature is great,
so, Lord, act likewise for me.
In the mire of saṃsāra's swamps
I am sunk without refuge; protect me.

Thus the wise student should make the supplication to the guru, the vajra-holder. And with the best food and drink, beef, and if there is none, other things; likewise with incense, food offerings, garlands, bells, banners, perfumes and other offering substances, one should properly honor the vajra master.

After this, once the master is pleased and the disciple has set aside the momentary states of the various kinds, the master should speak again to that disciple as before, saying:

The Teacher said: Great being,
take hold of great bliss.
Until you reach awakening,
hold the vajra for the sake of beings.

By this, the very reality is to be shown: the place of the vital point, whose nature is existence, and the state of existence that has been completely abandoned. After that, in order to cut off the disciple's wrong conceptions, the master should speak these words:

This itself is the great wisdom.
It abides in the body of all.
The manner of the two and of non-duality,
of things and of non-things, is its very nature, its chief.
It abides, pervading the stable and the moving.

That is to say: "Son of good family, this, which has been practiced and sealed in utmost secrecy by all the buddhas of the three times, you must know to be present in your own body alone. Do not harbor doubt or hesitation about it elsewhere." Why so? Because the tantra called the Vajra Garland also says:

This changeless body of one's own
is to be known by the one supreme.
Indicated through an example,
it is to be realized from the mouth of the master.

So the master should instruct: "Son of good family, for embodied beings it abides in the form of the seminal fluid." Likewise, that very seminal fluid should be taught in a manner that restrains true craving and points to it from afar. Again, as before, he should instruct with these words:

It is held to be of the nature of an illusory form.
By the method of the maṇḍala wheel
one is always surely liberated.

Here, sons of good family, although the seminal fluid abides in one's own body, it is not a bliss shared in common with all sentient beings. Therefore actual liberation does not come through it directly. If one thought that liberation came directly, that would contradict what is plainly seen. So, as the supreme method, one should first make preliminary preparations such as the maṇḍala of colored powder, the hand seals, and the taking of vows, and cultivate the contrived yoga. When sufficient time has passed, having fully understood the maṇḍala of the body and tasted the flavor of the essence, one is shown it in the proper way and becomes liberated. This should be taught.

The preceding words themselves make this clear. Then Vajragarbha, having asked forgiveness of the yoginīs such as Parṇaśabarī who were present with him, addressed the Blessed One as follows, and so on, down to the words "how should the stages be explained?" This shows what Vajragarbha asked. After that the Blessed One gave his answer, which is shown by the words:

The maṇḍala is called the essence.
The awakening mind is great bliss.

The essence is so called because it takes up the vital substance, holds it, and brings realization. Likewise it is called the wheel because life and the seminal drops circulate in the manner of earth, water and the other elements. This is shown by:

It is called the essence of the wheel.
The awakening mind is great bliss.

For the essence takes up the vital substance, holds it, and brings realization; and it is called the wheel because life and the seminal drops circulate in the manner of earth, water and the rest. Thus it is said:

The first is called the Ender (Antaka).
It is declared to be the gathered maṇḍala.

Wherever the life-wind and the seminal drops gather, that place is called the essence. Thus it is also said:

The wheel is called the element of space.
It purifies the objects and the rest.

To show a fitting example by which this may be known, it says: "the lotus of the place, the element of space." This is to be indicated by the time when the male organ and the lotus are joined with one another. Therefore it is said:

Through the union of bola, kakkola,
one will know its bliss.

Again, Vajragarbha asked the Blessed One how those who dwell in Hevajra should abide in their vows and pledges, and the Blessed One answered:

You should kill living beings,
for life is said to be mind.
Mind is wind and seminal fluid.
The killing of living beings is the mind alone,
and not otherwise, in that place.
You should also speak falsehood.
What is called "destroying the world"
is proclaimed as the word of falsehood.
It is not that either; it is practiced otherwise.
Since one's own life-wind is heard,
all beings at once, by one cry,
to each and every one of them,
teach the fitting Dharma: this is the false word.
You should also take what is not given:
the queen's seminal fluid, not given,
the supreme woman who is one's own.
Take her though not given, not otherwise.
You should rely on another's woman.
Vajrasattva and others rely on another's woman
as supreme; otherwise,
she is not a woman endowed with stain.

By this the vows of definitive meaning are taught.

Next, the Blessed One taught that objects and subjects, the aggregates, the elements, the sense sources, and all of form, sound, smell, taste, tactile objects and the rest, being mutually gathered together, are themselves the wheel of the maṇḍala. The yoginīs did not understand the aspects of objects and subjects, of the aggregates, elements and sense sources, and so they put their question to the Blessed One. This is shown by the passage that begins "And what are these objects?" and ends "What is the nature of these?" To this the Blessed One replied. It is thus:

Form, sound, and likewise smell,
Taste, and likewise touch,
And the nature of the element of phenomena:
These are spoken of as the six objects.

This is what he declared. Of these, the object form is the syllable U, which arises from the lips. Sound is the syllable AH, which arises from the throat. Smell is the syllable A-LA, which arises from the teeth. Taste is the syllable AR, which arises from the crown of the head. Touch is the syllable E, which arises from the palate. And the element of phenomena is the syllable Ā, which arises from the throat. These six are the nature of the six objects. The six, A, I, Ṛ, U, LAṂ and AṂ, are those with the defining marks; and likewise the six, A, I, Ṛ, U, Ḷ and AḤ, are those with the manner of means and wisdom. Because these are established in what is external, in the body, and in everything else, whatever is taught as the six faculties, the six elements, the six aggregates and so on is of such a nature. In this way the six objects and the six faculties become the twelve sense sources, and likewise, by a division of the elements, the eighteen elements are established. The meaning is that these were taught as conventional things so that you yoginīs might understand.

If you ask what their nature is:

Their nature is unborn from the very beginning,
Neither false nor true, and thus
All is like the moon in water.
Let the yoginī know it so from the beginning.

Here the meaning is this. By whom, how, and from where were these objects, faculties and the rest first produced? And at the end, by whom, how, and where do they perish? There is no one who produces them or destroys them, because they are pure by nature from the very beginning. The intent is: "You too should know it so."

For example, fire suddenly arises from the drilling stick, the base-board, and the effort of a man's hands. Yet that fire does not abide in the stick, nor in the base-board, nor in the man's hands. When sought in every way, it abides in none of them. That fire is neither true nor false. In just this way, yoginīs, you should reflect on all phenomena. Thus suchness is not the aggregates, nor the elements, nor the sense sources, nor the objects, and it is not to be sought as something apart from them. That is the meaning.

Then the yoginīs, Nairātmyā and the others, all the vajra ḍākinīs, took up the five nectars and, holding the substances of the sacred bond, made offerings to the Blessed One Vajrasattva, delighted him with the union of the dance, and also drank the flavor of the five nectars. The meaning is this. Having grasped and fully understood the meaning of the vajra words spoken by the Blessed One, namely that the suchness of all phenomena neither abides in things nor fails to abide, they honored the Blessed One with the objects, and they declared that they themselves also consume objects.

Then the Blessed One, pleased, showed his empowering blessing:

Kye, kye, vajra ḍākinīs,
You who make homage to all the buddhas,
By the blessing of the vajra offering,
Which I have made secret, that very reality,
Listen well, for I shall speak as you wish.

This means that the Blessed One was pleased, having seen that the yoginīs had understood this profound way of the Dharma, taught through the two truths. Wishing to teach once more the distinctive features of conduct, he spoke those words.

The distinctive features of conduct are these:

Food and drink, however they are found,
Going and not going, one does not avoid;
One should not bathe nor practice cleanliness,
The customs of the village are wholly abandoned.
The wise one does not recite mantras,
And does not fix the mind on meditative concentration.

This is taught in the passage from there up to "Let it be eaten fully with the awakening mind." Because suchness does not arise from conceptual thought, one should entirely abandon all concepts and dwell as one pleases. Why so? Because whatever is perceived is by nature the pure essence. For this reason, for one possessed of the mind of nondual wisdom there is nothing that is not to be eaten, not to be drunk, not to be examined, not to be looked at, or not to be tasted.

Likewise:

Having found the self-arisen flower,
And placed it in the lotus vessel...

This means that the blood arising from the woman's womb is taken into a skull cup. Then:

Mixed with phlegm and with mucus,
It is drunk by one who keeps the vow.

That is, things despised by worldly people, such as phlegm and mucus, are mixed with that blood, and the childish, the bearers of the sacred bond, are also made to eat it. This also removes gray hair, wrinkles and the like. By this the yogin leads those of childish nature. Moreover, since object and subject have become one, the elements do not emerge again, and the lower portion of the day, that is, the downward movement, is the definitive meaning.

The words beginning "Lower garments of various colors" are taught at length in the chapters on conduct.

Furthermore, since it has not been shown which family each of the six sense faculties belongs to, and since they have not been purified, the Blessed One said earlier:

Since all is pure in its objects,
the Blessed One, as said before,
has certainly not made pure
the count of the six faculties.

This is the question put by Vajragarbha, and the Blessed One then gave his answer, beginning with the words "In the eye, Mohavajrā," and so on. This meaning is given in the root tantra as follows:

In the eye is Mohavajrā,
shown by the letter A.
In the ear is Dveṣavajrā,
shown by the letter Aṃ.
In the nose is she called Mātsaryā,
shown by the letter E.
In the mouth is Rāgavajrā,
shown by the letter U.
In the body is Īrṣyāvajrā,
shown by the letter Ḹ.
In the mind is the yoginī Nairātmyā,
shown by the letter A.
For the sake of purifying the faculties,
with this the great bodhisattva is armored.

This should be understood from what was spoken there.

Vajragarbha then asked:

What is called the language of intention?
What is the great pledge of the yoginīs,
unknown to śrāvakas and the like?

Here the question is this: "O Blessed One, when yogins who abide in Hevajra find themselves among śrāvakas, pratyekabuddhas, non-Buddhists, and other childish beings, how should they handle the secret expressions and the rest?" Likewise:

Laughing and gazing,
embracing, and likewise the pairs,
and the tantra too, in four kinds:
the language of intention has not been proclaimed.
May the Blessed One surely declare it.

So too the question means: "Please also explain the symbolic terms for the bliss of laughing, the bliss of gazing, the bliss of embracing, and the bliss of two giving to two, and so on, in the place where attachment is aroused."

The Blessed One answered, saying "Vajragarbha, listen," and then spoke these symbolic terms. They should be referred to the root tantra itself:

Madana is liquor, and meat
is by outer and inner distinction.
Apply this wording to all of these.
The bliss of semen is the intoxicating liquor.
The faculties explained earlier,
which do not go outward, are called meat.
Assembly is called Malayaja.
Born from family and non-family,
having the nature of the four wheels,
because they gather in their own places
it is spoken of as Malayaja.
The going is called Kheṭaro:
from whatever place, by the fire of time,
one goes to the place of darkness,
like a thousand flashes of lightning-light.
That is why it is called Kheṭaro.
The bone ornament is Nirāṃśu.
It is without parts, free of expression,
wholly beyond the realm of words.
The bliss of semen, whatever it is,
is known as Nirāṃśu.
The coming is called Phreṃkhaṇa.
The path of the moon, from the left side,
by which one comes, urged on,
ripening gods and humans and the rest,
is known as Phrekhaṇa.
Cāṅteu is called Kṛvīta.
The life-wind is the sound "cāṅteu,"
abiding firmly on the indestructible path;
what the yogin hears and obtains
is explained as Kṛvīta.
The unfortunate is called Duduru.
The yogin whose love is utterly lowly,
who lets forth sixteen parts,
is lowly in compassion,
and should be called Duduru.
The fortunate is called Kāliñjara.
Whatever the bliss of one's own skull,
which even Brahmā, Śiva, Viṣṇu, and the god of desire
cannot at all snatch away,
is held to be supreme: Kāliñja.
The untouchable is called Diṇḍimā.
Nairātmyā and the other yoginīs,
having the nature of sixteen parts,
do not cling to a self; they terrify;
this is proclaimed as Diṇḍimā.
The skull is Padmabhājana.
That by which the body is nourished in a stream,
without which it would not exist,
the sovereign of the three worlds,
is Padmabhājana.
The good is called Tṛptika.
All the things of the objects explained earlier
are consumed, though not by the tongue.
The supreme flavor of the food of meditation
is known everywhere as Tṛptika.
The phlegm is Tīndhinaṃ.
Sun, moon, Rāhu, and the rest,
the one who has abandoned the ram and Kapilavastu,
is clearly established by this path.
It is known everywhere as Tīndhinaṃ.
The feces is called Catussama.
Where sun, moon, Rāhu, and the fire of time
gather together in equality,
because the four meditations are equal,
it is remembered as "the four equal."
The urine is called Kastūrī.
Like a person accomplished in the body,
relying on which, the musk deer's
navel scent is matched,
it should be called Kastūrī.
The self-arisen is to be known as Siṃhala.
The woman whose source of birth is blood,
the skilled, intelligent yogin
should know the generator to be Siṃhala,
like sandalwood and siṃhala.
Semen is called Karpūra.
By the great fire of wisdom
the cities are thoroughly stirred up,
and the camphor-colored scent is shown.
Likewise it is called Karpūra.
The great flesh is called Śālijam.
The supreme holder of great moral discipline,
possessed of a body like a vajra,
or else others, by desire and devotion,
the sky-farer's navel is Śālijam.
The joining of the two faculties is Kunduru.
In the pure abode the great lord
is invited by "the one to be burned,"
and when the two are equally united
that is called Kunduru.
It is explained as Vajrabolla.
The water of samādhi based on it
gives rise to the wisdom body,
and the power and splendor are incomparably great;
it is explained as Bolla.
Padma is called Kakkola.
Relying on the lotus, the three worlds
are fully endowed with the nature of bliss,
and because one attains a vajra body
it is proclaimed as Kakkola.

By what was said here the meaning of this should be understood. In this order of expression, the symbols, as they stand in the Saṃpuṭa tantra by their established usage, should be kept in mind just as they are. They are to be spoken to one another according to the occasion. The definitive meanings stated above are only to be understood by the mind; they are not to be spoken just as they are.

"Divided by the distinction of colors,
the families are taught as five.
This is the secret language."

This means that the language of intention taught in this way, rich in great meaning and divided into conventional and ultimate, comes down to nothing other than the intention of the five letters. For it is said, "The five mudrās are what is condensed."

These families are as follows. The Yuṅmo is said to belong to the vajra family. Akṣobhya is the element of space. The dancer likewise belongs to the lotus family, and Amitābha is the element of fire. The Rangtsedma belongs to the jewel family, and Ratnasambhava is the element of water. The Kyegyi, the twice-born, is declared to be the Tathāgata family. The great Vairocana is the element of earth. Cauṇḍālī is of the action family, and Amoghasiddhi is the element of wind. These grant the accomplishments of the mudrās. These become the vajra semen. The one of fierce discipline should honor them and drink.

By this passage, with its distinction between the provisional and the definitive meaning, the daughters born from these families are to be fully known. Having trained well and acted in the way of bliss, one draws out the awakening mind and drinks it, and so on, and the body becomes firm like a vajra. In the definitive sense, whichever family it may be, outer or inner, the body becomes like a vajra through the fierce discipline of the absorption that does not summon seed syllables. This is the definitive meaning of the Tathāgata.

Vajragarbha, great bodhisattva, the secret language is wondrous. Whatever I explain to you, take all of it up with reverence. This was spoken as a solemn proclamation. Then he spoke these further words of warning, as follows.

Hevajra, whoever has received empowerment and does not speak the secret language, his samaya will surely be broken; of this there is no doubt.

How, then, does the sign of broken samaya appear? Through plague, thieves, spirits, epidemics, and poisons; if one does not speak the buddhas' secret language, one will be afflicted. So beginner yogis must certainly proclaim this language.

If one finds one's own samaya family and does not speak these words, the yoginī born from the four places becomes wrathful at that moment. This should be understood in terms of the provisional and the definitive meaning.

This is the third chapter, the extensive explanation of the introduction to all the tantras and of the secret language, from the extensive explanation of the definitive meaning of Hevajra.

Supreme among speakers, king of speakers, lion of speech, one who has no equal, who strikes terror into the non-Buddhists: to the Blessed Kye, I pay homage. This is the extensive commentary on the Condensed Mudrās. For sentient beings bewildered in the darkness of suffering, I have written this extensive commentary, so that a lamp may make things clear.

Then Vajragarbha and the others, and the ḍākinīs, fell into doubt, and so on. In the chapter on conduct, there are the songs and dances performed by the yogī that bestow accomplishment. In the chapter on the deities, there are the mudrās of aversion and the rest, and what is to be sealed, by whom, and how. There are the mantra syllables taught in the wheel of Nairātmyā, and what these are. There is the intended meaning of the thirty-two channels taught in the chapter on the families. About all these they asked: Blessed One, I am confused; please explain these to me purely.

The Blessed One then declared the song beginning with "Kolla irishṭi" and so on. Its meaning is to be understood here. "Kolla irishṭi abolā" shows the pīṭha and the near pīṭha. "Mummuṇi reka kakkolā" shows the field and the near field. "Ghaṇe kṛpiṭa hobā jja ikāruṇe kia inarolā" shows the chandoha. "Tahiṃ bala khājja igāḍheṃ ma aṇā bijja aī" shows the near chandoha. "Haleka liñjarpaṇi aī dunduravājje aī" shows the meeting place. "Cau usamaka cchurī sihlakāspurā lāī aī" shows the near meeting place. "Māla indhaṇa sāliñjata hi bharukhā iaī" shows the charnel ground. "Niraṃ suaṃga caḍavita hiṃ jasarāvapaṇi aī" shows the near charnel ground. "Preṃkhaṇakheṭakarante śuddhā śuddhana muṇi aī" shows the pīlava. "Malaya jo kunduru pāṭa iḍiṇḍi mata hiṇṇa bājji aī" shows the near pīlava. By this order of recitation, the meaning is to be understood from what the root tantra says: "These are shown as the ten grounds. Whoever sings this song with knowledge will have accomplishment increase, as with Heruka."

Now, in order to show the dance: the yogī whose mindfulness is not stolen dances in the form of Heruka. Free of attachment, with an undefiled mind, he meditates with a mind of desire. This means that the mind is not carried away to anything else in the bliss of passion, while great compassion is stirred; this is the dance. As is said: "He is the abandonment of causes and the rest; ru is freedom from gatherings; ka is not abiding anywhere; Heruka is thus renowned." So, undefiled by the fault of attachment through being free of it, one should stir great compassion for the sake of oneself and others. This is the meaning.

In "Vajra dharma and buddha," the vajra dharma is the nature of speech, and the buddha is the nature of body; from this force the mind is also to be understood. This shows that body, speech, and mind are to be made one.

By the yoginīs and the mothers, with these songs and dances, one sings well and dances too. By this one keeps the samaya, and likewise protects oneself. By this one brings the world under one's power.

By those songs and dances, the outer and inner samayas and the full increase of accomplishments are all clearly shown. Understanding their nature just as it is, one brings the three worlds under one's power, and one should understand that the mantra recitation is exactly this.

Wherever songs are sung with devotion, and wherever dances are performed with devotion, the lord of the gathering has first begun them. This applies either to the great feast gathering that has received the king's command, or to an ordinary feast gathering, or to a heroes' festival other than these. The meaning is that first the chief of the gathering sings, and afterwards the others sing as they please.

At the time of those gatherings and heroes' festivals, through the blessing of song and dance, the first sign will be of three kinds: a smell like that of the garlic-like plant, then in the middle a smell like that of a vulture, and finally a smell like camphor. These are to be indicated by the vajra master. In the same way, by the characteristic of song, the vocal tones at the beginning and at the end are like the sounds of the king of swans and the king of bees; they draw out the tone and rest upon the ending. In the middle is a sound like that of the finest bull, a sound like that of a goat, meaning that it is to be expanded; and in that very instant these signs will also arise in the vicinity of the maṇḍala. Therefore it is said:

The sound of swans and of bees,
and a sound like that of the bull and the cow,
should be listened for at the end of the song.
By the blessing and characteristic of song,
the outer too, in the colored powder, will be revealed.

Now the characteristic of the mudrā will be explained. Why? Because when the signs, the families, the lineage and the conduct have not been fully purified, one does not perform worldly acts such as the marriage rite and the like. If one were to engage in worldly acts without full purification, there would not even be worldly convention, and barbarism would spread everywhere. Therefore it is said in this tantra and that tantra, "As the outer, so the inner." Hence:

The mudrā is the sign and the mark;
by these its family is to be recognized.
Through the practice of meditating on a mixed-up family,
the practitioner has no accomplishment.

and so on was taught. To seal each one with the mudrā of its own family is the certainty of the Tathāgatas. The meaning of this is as the root tantra says:

For aversion, the mudrā is Nairātmyā;
the protector is Akṣobhya, of the aversion family.
For delusion, the mudrā is Vajrā;
the protector is Vairocana, of the delusion family.
For miserliness, the mudrā is Gaurī herself;
the protector of miserliness is Ratnasambhava.
For attachment, the seal is Caurī;
the protector of attachment is of the Amitābha family.
For jealousy, the mudrā is Khecarī;
jealousy is of the Amoghasiddhi family.
Likewise the goddesses of the intermediate directions and so on,
for the sake of earth, water, fire and wind,
are sealed with the mudrā in due order.

Understand the meaning from what was thus taught. Likewise, from "Again, Gaurī with aversion" up to "For desire, the mudrā is Khecarī; know the mudrā through desire," it should be understood as above; they differ only in being distinguished as object and subject.

Now, to show the seed syllables of the fifteen goddesses beginning with Nairātmyā, "The first āli is Nairātmyā" and so on was spoken. The seed syllable of Nairātmyā is A. The second āli, the seed of Vajrā, is Ā. The third, the seed of Gaurī, is I. The fourth, the seed of the yoginī of the ten, is Ī. The fifth, the seed of the vajra ḍākinī, is U. This is the addition of the inner. The addition of the outer will be explained. The sixth, the seed of Pukkasī, is Ū. The seventh, the seed of Śmaśānacārī, is Ṛ. The eighth, the seed of Śvapacī, is Ṝ. The ninth, the seed of Yungmo, is Ḷ. As it is said, "Again the five and two are Gaurī herself." The tenth, the seed of Gaurī, is Ḹ. The eleventh, the seed of Caurī, is E. The twelfth, the seed of Vetālī, is Ai. The thirteenth, the seed of Ghasmarī, is O. The fourteenth, the seed of Bhūcarī, is Au. The fifteenth, the seed of Khecarī, is Aṃ. "The very seeds of the yoginīs" means that the seeds thus named are the seeds of the fifteen goddesses beginning with Nairātmyā.

In the chapter on the families it is said:

The channels, which are sixteen and two,
are taught as one's own nature.
The channels are two by two, the yoginīs;
each is spoken of in three aspects.

Thus the thirty-two channels taught in the chapter on the families move together with life-breath and mind; by the division into method and wisdom, the channels are two by two, and each yoginī is a goddess, the nature of the fifteen goddesses. Since it is said:

The stretched one, rasanā, the all-trembling one,
are called the selfless yoginī,

the thirty-two are entirely to be given up, outer and inner, in every respect, and offered to Nairātmyā. This too:

Because there is no sixteenth part,
strive to abandon all that remains.

It is said so because there is no sixteenth lunar day. As for what is known as the sixteenth, it is the first day of the waning fortnight. Why is that? "Because it performs no purposeful function," as is clearly shown.

And this too, in this very Compendium Tantra:

Of one whose nature is the fifteen parts,
the awakening minds become the moon.
The yoginīs are its parts,
great bliss, the form of the āli.

Likewise, "the great emptiness, five syllables": at that time, it abides like the shape of the five syllables, and is adorned everywhere by the drops and by what is to be cut apart.

Yogīs of childish mind may say: Are not the āli taught as sixteen, and are not the drops and the transference of joy also taught as sixteen? Why then? The thought "abandon the sixteenth and the thirty-two" is wrong. For the Tathāgata, with childish beings in mind, divided by body, speech, mind and wisdom, taught sixteen joys with the four bodies in mind: the essential body, the Dharma body, the complete enjoyment body and the emanation body. Likewise, the sixteen āli and sixteen drops do not actually exist; since they make the two parts manifest, they are like Rāhu or like a eunuch.
