This is said so that whoever harms the Buddha's teaching, or destroys the forms of buddhas, gurus, and so on, is to be petitioned to the Sage Guru and killed through great compassion, and not otherwise.

In the same way, in the center of the maṇḍala of the sixty-four accomplishments to be achieved, one invites the co-emergent deity by the hand of the practitioner's own meditative absorption, and the practitioner himself, as the verse says, "With Heruka in equal union, by the mind that is the inner demon," becomes the Heruka maṇḍala together with Nairātmyā. By the vajra words "the color is to be differentiated according to the type of action," one generates the entire maṇḍala assembly in the color black, in the family of aversion. If one then slays, even a buddha will inevitably be destroyed.

"As when seeing the form of a beast, vomiting blood, trembling, with hair loosened, with the mouth downward, one should meditate." This means: one sees the person as like the form of a beast, vomiting blood from the mouth, the body trembling, the hair loosened, and one imagines him sitting before oneself in a crouch. In the same way, through his lower opening, one makes many forms blazing like needles of fire enter; and if one visualizes in his heart a fire maṇḍala with the letter raṃ, then without any doubt he will be destroyed in that very instant. So it was taught.

The definitive meaning of this should be understood from what the root tantra says: "The one who consumes offerings, the fire god, is stirred by the wind; the awakening mind, haṃ, looks downward. When one is intoxicated with bliss, one trembles and the face looks downward."

"In this tantra there is no fire offering, no binding of mudrās, no activity; it is accomplished by reading the great tantra; it will be accomplished by meditation alone." This means: it is not accomplished by outer fire offerings, by binding mudrās with the hands, by reading the tantra as words, or by the deity yoga alone. Rather, one binds the mudrā of the lion's play in the fire of non-anger, recites with the breath of one's own life-force, and accomplishes it through meditation like space. This is what is shown.

"The giver of nonconceptual accomplishment, delightful in the union of existence, I shall explain by the supreme secret. Listen, goddess of fair form." This means: the Blessed One Vajradhara says, "I shall explain to you, goddess, the great secret of all the buddhas, the means of utterly purifying existence itself."

"See, goddess, the garland blazing with the splendor of jewels, disarrayed. Unstrung, it is without union; strung, it gives delight." This is an example: when a garland of jewels blazes with radiant light and is delightful, if it is not strung it does not perform its function, but if it is strung it accomplishes every function.

"Thus, for one who desires saṃsāra, the jewel endowed with the five qualities, when impure, becomes poison; when pure, it becomes like nectar." This means that the natures of saṃsāra are neither pure nor impure; by the difference in the practitioners' attitudes they abide as poison or as nectar. Therefore, if one has faith, by the guru's instruction, that saṃsāra, which is like poison, is like nectar, then "the aspect of saṃsāra is Heruka, the chief liberator of beings, he who has become form." This means that existence itself is the cause of nirvāṇa.

Moreover, one should apprehend it thus: "The eye of compassion is red; from the heart of loving-kindness the limbs are black." The yogī should take such signs as these in faith and teach them to others too. Up to the passage "the bones are the four truths," it is shown in this way; and one should understand, as a provisional and as a definitive meaning, that by virtue of the qualities of its nature it is gathered into the sides of father and mother.

Now, in order, comes the method of gathering the mantras. Nairātmyā asked Hevajra, "How is the mantra to be gathered?" and so forth. The Blessed One then replied, "I shall explain the mantra to you; listen, goddess of great wisdom." The extensive meaning of this is taught at length in the root tantra of five hundred thousand verses, so one should enter into it from there.

"One who wishes to know the mantras as they are should bow to the master, bow down with supreme faith, and request, 'Grant me the reality of the mantra.' Then the fortunate guru, in a place without outsiders, should smear the ground well with pure cow dung, and draw well, with a string of powdered stone, the supreme maṇḍala of Hevajra, with eight petals and a pistil, adorned with four doors and four porticoes, fully endowed with eight pillars. Then he should lay out the two groups of syllables. In the center are Hevajra and Nairātmyā, drawn in the form of the skull cup and the curved knife. On its first petal the skilled one writes the sixteen vowels; on the second the five beginning with ka; on the third, the wise one writes the five beginning with ca; on the fourth he writes the five beginning with ṭa; likewise on the fifth the five groups beginning with ta; on the sixth the group of the waters; on the seventh he writes the semivowels; on the eighth he writes the group of the warm sounds. At the four doors he draws the hook, the noose, and the rest. Contemplating the eight goddesses well, he places the master's consort at the left. Having honored well with various kinds of offerings, he gathers the secret mantra. Then the supreme disciple is made to receive it with great reverence. Whoever takes it otherwise, on his own whim, has no result and is ruined."

"First give Vairocana": this is oṃ. "The fourth of the ūṣmas" is ha. "Adorned with the Pukkasī's consonant" means adorned with u. "Pressed down by the empty" means that its crown is adorned with a drop. "White in color; at the end add svāhā. By this, with a hundred thousand recitations, one always paralyzes beings." This means: first recite the number of syllables, one hundred thousand, according to the guru's instruction, and if one performs the preliminary approach with the ten-part fire offering, one paralyzes the three worlds.

"First place the lord of the letters": this is oṃ. "Then Khecarī" is aṃ. "At the end add svāhā. Even buddhas are brought under control." This means: if one recites according to the above instruction and even buddhas are brought under control, what need to speak of lesser beings.

"Give the first of the Vedas": this is oṃ. "The second of the first" is kha. "With the empty" means adorned with a drop.

At the end it is joined with svāhā. The buddhas themselves are driven out by one who has wisdom. This is the gathering of the mantra that expels, as before.

At the beginning place Vairocana. That is oṃ. In the third of the second set. That is ja. Joined with ra: that is, ja adorned with the letter ra. Adorned with Caurī: adorned with the vowel i. With the zero: above it, adorned with the drop. Svāhā well joined with ra. It makes all people hateful. This is the gathering of the mantra that causes hatred.

At the beginning set the lord of letters. That is oṃ. The third of the fifth set: that is ba. With the zero: adorned with the drop. Joined with the ḍākinī: at its end the vowel u is subjoined. At the end svāhā, the great fierce rite. This is the gathering of the letters of the fierce rite.

Again give the head of the letters. That is oṃ. Hūṃ is like blood: after it place hūṃ. At the end svāhā: after that, svāhā is to be joined. If one recites it, Tilottamā and Dga' bzang ma and others are always summoned. Thus it is taught. This is the gathering of the letters that summon. If one recites it according to the rite, even a divine maiden such as Tilottamā is drawn, so what need is there to mention other women?

The family of delusion is placed at the beginning. That is oṃ. Ghuḥ is well and thoroughly joined: after oṃ, ghuḥ is placed. At the end, well joined with svāhā. It kills gods and humans. That is, ghuḥ with svāhā at its end is the gathering of the letters that kill. If one recites it according to the rite, even one like a buddha can be made to fall, so what need is there to mention the lesser gods and humans of the three worlds? They would be killed.

At the beginning place Vairocana. That is oṃ. The first of the first set: that is ka. Joined with the vajra ḍākinī: below that ka, subjoin u. The second of those standing at the end: that is ra. Endowed with the vajra ḍākinī: subjoin u to that ra. Again, on the tip of the first: that is ka. Endowed with the vajra ḍākinī: subjoin the letter u to it. The third of those standing at the end: that is la. Again, well joined with that same: again subjoin that same u to it. Adorned above with Caurī: that is, adorn it above with e. After that, adorned with hrīḥ: at the end of those mantras, place hrīḥ. At the end again, svāhā is done. This is the gathering of the mantra of Kurukullā.

Oṃ at the beginning. The third of the fourth set: that is da. Adorned with Caurī: adorned with e. The fourth of those standing at the end: that is va. Join bi, cu, vajra: after va, subjoin pi, cu, vajra. The fourth of the sibilants: that is ha. Adorned with puk-ka-sī-sa: that ha is to be adorned with u. Pressed down by the zero and tripled: crown that ha with a drop, and set that same hūṃ, so adorned, three times. The second of the fifth set: that is pha. The first of the third set: that is ṭa. At the end svāhā: at the end of these mantra lines svāhā is to be joined. This is the gathering of the heart mantra of Hevajra.

Vairocana first, then jvala twice: first place oṃ, and after it join jvala twice. The fourth of the fifth set: that is bha. Joined with the first of those standing at the end: subjoin ya to that bha. Adorned with gha-sma-rī: adorn that same with o. The fourth of the sibilants: that is ha. Adorned with the vajra ḍākinī: subjoin u to the sibilant. Pressed down by the zero: press it above with a drop. Tripled: set that same letter three times. The second of the fifth set: that is pha. The first of the third: that is ṭa. At the end svāhā: at the end of the mantra lines so spoken, svāhā is to be joined. This is the gathering of the mantra of the four-armed one.

First Vairocana: that is oṃ. The first of the first: that is ka. Adorned with the inner Gaurī: adorned with i. The first of the third: that is ṭa. Adorned with the inner Gaurī: adorn it with i. Doubled: that same is recited twice. The third of the fifth set: that is ba. The third of the second set: that is ja. Adorned with fire: subjoin ra to that ja. Joining three hūṃs: after those mantra lines, three hūṃs are to be joined. The second of the fifth: that is pha. The first of the third: that is ṭa. At the end svāhā: at the end of those mantra words svāhā is to be joined. This is the gathering of the mantra of the six-armed one.

First Vairocana: that is oṃ. The first of the fourth set: that is ta. Well joined with Yamī: subjoin ra to that ta. Adorned with Vetālī: above that ta, adorn it with e. The third of those standing at the end: that is la. Adorned with gha-sma-rī: on its tip adorn it with o. The first of the first: that is ka. The first of those standing at the end: subjoin ya to ka. Adorned with Vajrā: subjoin a to it. Kṣa adorned with Caurī: adorn that letter with e. The first of the fifth: that is pa. The fourth of the sibilants: that is ha. Adorned with the vajra ḍākinī: subjoin u to ha. Pressed down by the zero: above it, adorn it with a drop. Tripled: set that same letter three times. Phaṭ, and at the end svāhā: at the end of those mantra lines phaṭ and svāhā are to be joined. This is the gathering of the mantra of the two-armed one.

Nairātmyā: that is a. The first of the first: that is ka. The first of the second: that is ca. The first of the third: that is ṭa. The first of the fourth: that is ta. The first of the fifth: that is pa. The first of those standing at the end: that is ya.

The first of the heat letters is "śa." The words "first Vairocana, at the end svāhā" mean this: at the beginning of those mantra lines place oṃ, and at the end add svāhā; recite the mantra a hundred thousand times, according to the number of its syllables, and burn the materials required for that fire offering. If one has first done the preliminary approach, one can throw into turmoil even a dense, richly arrayed city that is like the dwelling place of the buddhas, so what need is there to speak of the cities of ordinary beings of the three worlds being thrown into turmoil.

"The second of the final-standing letters" is "ra." "Kṣaye, doubling the line" means that this very letter is to be written twice. "Endowed with three forms of HŪṂ" means that after it three HŪṂs are to be added. "At the beginning Vairocana, with phaṭ, bring it inside, at the end svāhā" means that at the beginning of the preceding mantra lines oṃ is placed and at the end svāhā is added, and after that, joined with phaṭ, this is the mantra for purifying the ground. This completes the gathering of the mantra.

"First Vairocana" means oṃ. "Then that of Vajrā" means āḥ. "At the end HŪṂ" means that after this the HŪṂ itself is added. "Blessing food and drink" means that food and drink are to be blessed with the three syllables of body, speech, and mind; this is to be understood in terms of both the provisional and the definitive meaning.

"First Vairocana" means that oṃ is placed first. "Then akāro mukhaṃ" means that these are added after it.

"The third of the heat letters" is "sa." "The fourth of the final-standing letters" is "va." "Above, adorned with the form of fire" means that above it is placed "ra." "Dharmāṇāṃ, then Vajrā" means that after it dharmāṇām is added, and then āḥ is placed, and then "dyānutpannatvāt" is placed, so that these syllables are inserted. After that, again, oṃ āḥ HŪṂ phaṭ svāhā is the gathering of the mantra of the torma offering for all spirits.

So when the mantra is gathered and recited with such a ritual procedure, one will attain accomplishment as taught in the tantra, and not otherwise. This is the meaning.

This is the ninth chapter, the extensive explanation of the gathering of the mantra, from the extensive explanation of the definitive meaning of the Hevajra.

Bowing in reverence to the one who holds the vajra, who is like the weapon of Śakra (Indra), I shall set forth in detail the varieties of the rosary, so that beings may take up the path. Then the Blessed One, in whom all phenomena are gathered as one, was called Vajrasattva. Having joined in union with Nairātmyā, he taught the distinctions concerning the objects of recitation.

On the conventional level, just as fire arises from the striking of stone against iron, from rubbing wood against wood, or from a crystal exposed to moonlight or a sun-crystal exposed to sunlight, through the force of their natural qualities, so too the accomplishments of the world arise from dependent origination, it is taught. Here too the tantra should be understood in the same way.

"Recitation with crystal for pacifying; for subjugation, red sandalwood; with the thorn-apple seed for fierce rites; with nirasa for hatred" is what is taught. The person who wishes to accomplish the rite of pacifying should recite with a crystal rosary, and through the earth-kasiṇa concentration at the navel maṇḍala the accomplishment will come about. The person devoted to yoga who wishes to subjugate should recite with a red sandalwood rosary, endowed with the meditative absorption of fire at the maṇḍala of the throat, and accomplishment will come about. Likewise, one who wishes to devote himself to fierce rites should recite with thorn-apple seed, endowed with the earth concentration. The yogin devoted to the rite of causing hatred should recite with a rosary of human bone, likewise endowed with the earth concentration. One who wishes to devote himself to the rite of banishing should recite with horse bone, endowed with the wind concentration at the heart. One who wishes to devote himself to the rite of summoning should recite with the bone of a brahmin, endowed with the water concentration at the forehead. One who wishes to devote himself to the rite of bringing down rain should recite with elephant bone, endowed with the water concentration at the forehead. One who wishes to devote himself to the rite of killing should recite with buffalo bone, endowed with the space concentration at the crown of the head.

Likewise, for the rite of pacifying, because the body is to be made soft, one should partake of milk. "For subjugation, partake according to one's own wish" is what is taught: milk and so forth, whatever is suitable, and as one desires, one should partake of. "For killing, siṃhala" is taught: for the rite of killing one should partake of siṃhala or blood. Likewise, for summoning, what is called "the four equal ones"; likewise, for causing hatred, what is called sālija; and for banishing, the navel of a deer, one should partake of. Or else: "the flesh of the end" means horse flesh; "the first flesh" means dog flesh; "na, the first" means human flesh; "ga, the first" means cow flesh; and "ha, the first also" means elephant flesh. In this way one should partake of whatever is appropriate. This is the meaning.

This is the tenth chapter, the extensive explanation of the objects of recitation, from the extensive explanation of the definitive meaning of the Hevajra.

For the benefit of all sentient beings, to engage in the aims of the buddhas, I bow to Hevajra and write the extensive explanation of the practice of innate union. Embracing Kye-vajra tightly, kissing with lips pressed, "the form of the families of embodied beings": thus Nairātmyā made her request. In this and what follows, Nairātmyā, having well honored the Blessed One Hevajra with offerings that have reached the far shore of great bliss, asks: on the conventional level, by the force of natural qualities, how are the marks of the families of embodied beings to be indicated? To this the Blessed One replied to Nairātmyā: "Having placed the liṅga in the bhaga, the lord of the maṇḍala spoke thus," and so on. "I shall explain the families of embodied beings: Perfection of Wisdom, listen." This is what is taught. On the conventional level, by the force of nature and qualities, whatever mark belongs to whichever family, that I shall explain to you, goddess. "Kye, goddess, listen well and respectfully." This is the meaning.

"Of a woman or a man, at the root of whose thumb a nine-pointed vajra appears: that is surely the supreme family of Akṣobhya."

This means that when a man or a woman has, at the joint at the root of the thumb, a line like a nine-pointed vajra, that person is blessed as belonging to the family of Akṣobhya. By the force of natural qualities, that person's complexion also stays dark, and the rite of killing succeeds.

"One becomes of the wheel of Vairocana."

In the same way, when someone has a line like a white eight-spoked wheel, that person is blessed by Vairocana. By the force of natural qualities, that person's complexion also stays white, and the rite of pacifying succeeds.

"The measure of the span of Amitābha's light."

In the same way, when someone has a line like a red eight-petalled lotus, that person is blessed by Amitābha, the Boundless Light. By the force of natural qualities, that person's complexion stays white tinged with red, and the rite of attracting succeeds.

"The jewel of Ratnasambhava."

In the same way, when someone has a line like a yellow eight-cornered jewel, that person is blessed by Ratnasambhava. By the force of natural qualities, that person's complexion stays yellow, and rites such as the rite of paralyzing succeed.

"The sword of the family of Karma."

In the same way, when someone has a line like a sword, bluish-green in color, that person is blessed by Amoghasiddhi. By the force of natural qualities, that person's complexion stays bluish-green, and rites such as the rite of expelling succeed.

"The yogī who is white and yellow belongs to the family of the sattva."

In the same way, one whose complexion is white and yellow is blessed by Vajrasattva, and all rites will succeed for that person.

"People who have reached the culmination of yoga, who are not broken by pride and not puffed up with arrogance, abiding in conventional form, will belong to the family of the Sugata."

This means that, in this way, all these men and women are blessed by the buddhas of the five families, or of the six families, and so on. Therefore they should not be angry with or hostile toward one another, for without doubt they all possess the natural qualities of heroes. As this same Condensed Tantra says: "Merely by harming beings one will not attain the accomplishment of the mudrā."

Furthermore: the vajra family is at the crown; the Amitābha family is at the forehead; the jewel family is at the throat; the Amoghasiddhi family is at the heart; the Vairocana family is at the navel; the family of the sattva is at the secret place. Likewise, in the eight-petalled lotus of the heart: the east is the family of Amoghasiddhi; the south is the family of the jewel; the west is the family of Vairocana; the north is the family of Amitābha; the center is the family of Akṣobhya. Likewise, the vowel A is in the center; I is in the east; Ṛ is in the south; Ḷ is in the west; U is in the north. Furthermore, wind is in the east, fire in the south, earth in the west, and water in the north; space should be understood to be in the center.

"The characteristics of women are just as those of men."

This means that, just as the signs and colors of the heroes are described in relation to men, so they should be shown for women. Therefore, for whatever man or woman one desires the accomplishment of the karma mudrā, one should recognize the forms, marks, and signs that have been taught, and take hold of them. This is the Tathāgata's instruction.

"By way of conventional convention, one becomes also of that family."

This means that, conventionally, by the force of natural qualities, one succeeds by taking up the family appropriate to each person.

"Having made the liṅga in the bhaga, the Great Vajra, pleased with it, was addressed by Nairātmyā, who made the intention known. Listen, goddess, to the supreme worship."

This means: having recognized one another's families as described above, one should seek the supreme place of the great mudrā; listen, it will be taught. The root tantra also says that all desired accomplishments arise from the rite of joining the bhaga and the liṅga, so one should become skilled in union by the method of the bhaga and the liṅga.

Furthermore, the same tantra says: "However the nature of beings may be tamed, in just that way one should purify desire and the rest for the sake of beings." And again: "For the benefit of beings and for the sake of the buddhas' teaching, even if one conceals everything from beings, one is not stained by sin." And: "There is no bliss like desire; offer it to the Victor. By relying on another's wife for the sake of beings, one will gain merit." This and more is taught at length.

Therefore, in order to seek the innate wisdom in a consort who has the proper family, one should also practice the methods of embrace: "In a grove, in a place without people, or within one's own house, naked, having made the great mudrā, the yogī who knows should always worship." This means one should rely on her in these places, and as long as wisdom has not arisen, one should keep relying on her, with conduct that has elaboration. Otherwise, as the root tantra teaches, "Impure, abandoning the rites, without regard for food, clothing, and the like, in one's own house or elsewhere, one should always focus on this wisdom." This meaning should be thoroughly understood.

Furthermore, the characteristics of the treatise on passion, beginning "Having embraced with milk," and continuing through "looking above and below," are an extension of the meaning of the verses and a half. They should be treated in the manner of a chapter of a treatise on desire. Since this has no end, I do not elaborate here. I ask pardon of the heroes, the heroines, and the female sovereigns.

"Having obtained abundant accomplishments, one becomes equal to all the buddhas. There one should drink the camphor."

This means that, relying on whichever consorts there are, of the vajra family and so on, one should drink the stainless camphor, but not the stained; with the stained there is no accomplishment.

"For the sake of the camphor, one should eat meat, and above all, liquor."

This means that, in order to bring forth the stainless camphor in every respect, one should partake of all meats and all liquors, having purified them, realized them, and kindled them, and made them into nectar. By this the camphor will increase, such is the meaning.

Here ends the eleventh chapter, the extensive explanation of the chapter on the coemergent, from the extensive explanation of the definitive meaning of Hevajra.

I pay homage to the one who is neither existent nor nonexistent, nor both existent and nonexistent, who is freed from the four alternatives. The extensive explanation of the four, Vajragarbha has taken pains to write. Then the Vajra-holder spoke the very meaning of the four empowerments, and so on. This meaning is also clear in the root tantra of five hundred thousand verses, so one should enter into that. As it is said:

Divided into worldly and beyond the world, empowerment is spoken of as twofold. The desired fruit is twofold, and is said to arise from this very basis. Divided into body, speech, and mind, there are the vase, the secret, and the wisdom of discernment: three empowerments are taught. For the sake of body, speech, mind, and wisdom, there are four empowerments: the vase, the secret, the discerning wisdom, and the fourth, the wisdom empowerment.

Therefore:

The tantra of the Primordial Buddha, the tantras in seven, ten, and eleven parts, the great tantras of five hundred thousand verses and the rest, whatever there is, is taught there; here it is merely condensed.

Therefore the sequence, the purpose, and the purifications of the four empowerments, the eleven, and so on are made clear in those great tantras. Here the meaning is only briefly presented, so one should understand those sources.

As for the "fourth" here: by way of the provisional and the definitive, it exists in both the worldly and the transworldly. This statement gives the name "fourth" to the definitive, transworldly empowerment, the eleventh, because this very tantra says that everything is by fours.

The vajra is consecrated, the vajra itself. Hold the great bell as well. Today you become a vajra master. Make it your task to gather disciples. This is clear in the root tantra, so one should enter into that. As it is said:

You are blessed as emptiness and compassion. Emptiness and compassion are unbroken. Hold the vajra and the bell as symbols, and as you do, so teach others. You are the perfect master, you too are the venerable elder. You have become cool and unclouded, a monk, an arhat whose defilements are exhausted. Having wholly abandoned leaking desire, you have drawn out every thorn. You are the supreme person who benefits others, you are the buddha's own son made manifest.

From this, one should understand the meaning.

Just as the buddhas of the past gave empowerment to the bodhisattva sons, so by the great secret empowerment I empower the continuum of your mind. This means: just as the buddhas of the past empowered the bodhisattva sons of all buddhas with the fourth, the great secret of the buddhas, so, my son, I empower the continuum of your mind as a vajra, such is the meaning.

Goddess who grants joy and grants abiding, of varied form, pleasing to the mind: take it, take it, great being; take it and make offerings to it. Here, the one who grants joy and abiding is the emptiness of one's own awareness. "Varied" means that, abiding in this vajra path of emptiness, one teaches the Dharma with varied forms and with varied, pleasing sounds. "Take it" means to hold that in mind. "Make offerings" means that, by its power, the selfless woman of the family, with no arising of excrement, urine, and the like, becomes the offering.

"This wisdom is exceedingly subtle" means that it is hard to realize for ordinary people attached only to the bliss of the two organs. It is like the vajra, which is said to be like the expanse of space: it is hard to realize that space is without shape and substance. By clearing away the dust of the stains of affliction, such as leaking, and bestowing the state of peace, it is dustless, ultimate, and utterly peaceful. Because it arises from the limit of each realizing one's own mind, it is said, "you yourself are its father."

The consecration of the vajra and the lotus means that the ordinary bhaga and liṅga are consecrated into an indestructible nature, and that the rasanā and the lalanā channels are consecrated into the path of indestructibility, free of every kind of leaking. Oṃ means gathering in the breath.

Padma sukha dhāra mahā rāga sukha daṇḍa: this is because it bestows freedom from stain through the nature of leaking. Catura ānanda bhāga viśva hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ kāryaṃ kuru ṣāme: this is because, through the stainless empowerment of the four joys, one is able to teach the wheel of Dharma of varied kinds to all beings. Oṃ vajra mahādveṣa catura ānanda dāyaka means that, having vanquished ordinary aversion, one reveals the meaning of the four joys in great vajra aversion. Khagamukhaikāra sonātha hūṃ hūṃ hūṃ kāryaṃ kuru ṣāme: because the joy of all that is full arises in the sky, the faces of those who go there gather and become of one taste, and so one becomes the protector and refuge of the three existences; therefore it says, "Do what is to be done for me."

"At the crown, the form of oṃ" is the vajra of body, the channel through which the moon moves. "At the heart, the form of hūṃ" is the vajra of mind, the channel through which Rāhu moves. "At the navel, the form of āḥ" is the vajra of speech, the channel through which the sun moves. Thus, because everything is gathered into body, speech, and mind, it shows that one should not seek the meaning elsewhere.

The Veda, water, flowers, and the roots of dūrvā grass, the Brahmins' offering of ghee: these a buddhist should not accept. Fearing that empowerment, mantras, and the like would be harmed and fall into decline, one should not give a drink even to those of bad lineages and the like.

Vajragarbha wrote down the meaning of the explanation of Hevajra, which is the very self of the five hundred thousand verses, and also of the Great Tantra, the Supreme Primordial, the Tantra of a Hundred Thousand, the Abhidhāna, and others. What is explained here is for the most part the individual explanation of the root text. Thus whatever this tantra teaches at any time, in each case is shown clearly by this.

I have not taught this fully to hearers, who are ordinary people. Likewise it is not for the sake of solitary buddhas, nor is it for non-Buddhists. Nor is it for unruly and wicked kings, nor for those who revile the great secret. I have not taught this to those who revile the bodhisattvas or the guru.

To one who is deeply devoted to the supreme secret, who reveres the master and guru, and who knows how to conceal his own reality, to such a one, whoever he may be, I have taught this.

I am the glorious Vajragarbha, supreme lord of the tenth ground. Having been well urged by heroes and heroines, I explained this so that yogins may take up the path.

The glorious Hevajra, a jewel hard to find, is well condensed here from the root tantra of five hundred thousand verses. Through whatever stainless virtue I have gained by writing well this work called the Extensive Explanation of the Definitive Meaning of its connection, may the secret of Hevajra be well understood. May my vajra brothers and others who follow the guru who teaches this, endowed with all the commitments, speak well to one another and bear no grudge. May discussion and questioning dwell at ease through the vajra of the commitments.

Whatever people do the evil deeds of the unruly, who break their commitments, and do not seek out yogins, may they not keep company with those, may they not even see them, and may they make offerings to their own dear guru, the vajra master. In this age of strife, may those who reveal the great secret, who destroy the wrong views of wicked non-Buddhists, and who afterwards recite the views of those same people, may they be the leaders who tame them.

Apart from this explanation, one should not trust the explanations composed by arrogant people who have not seen reality. One should remain trusting in a commentary such as this one, which establishes well the definitive and the provisional meanings of all the root tantras.

The Extensive Explanation of the Definitive Meaning of Hevajra, composed by the great lord of the tenth ground, the bodhisattva Vajragarbha, is complete.

It was translated by the Tibetan translator, the monk Drogmi Śerab Drakpa, after he made many requests to the Indian preceptor and great guru Maitrī and listened to it well.

The Extensive Explanation of the Condensed Tantra of Hevajra, composed by the bodhisattva Vajragarbha. This text, hard to find, had not been translated in the earlier commentary up to the chapter on reality. Later, in the great city of the land of Nepal called Rolpa, the translator, the monk Drogmi, found it with the paṇḍita Maitrī, brought the manuscript to Tibet, and translated it at the request of the monk and yogic practitioner Wangchuk Drakpa.

Translator's notes

This is the opening of Toh 1180, the commentary on the Hevajra tantra. The introductory verses use dense symbolic language (Evaṃ, the sixteen hands as the sixteen emptinesses, the fifty syllables), which I kept close to the Tibetan. The passage on "urine and semen" is a tantric symbolic equation (the substances stand for the buddhas' mudrās) and is rendered literally. "Vajra One" at the end I take as Hevajra, speaking in answer to Nairātmyā. The verse about the bodhisattvas "in as many bodies as there are world realms" is condensed in the Tibetan, and the rendering is slightly interpretive. The phrase "those who have the limit of the bases of training" is obscure; I kept it literal. This piece is largely verse set in an unsettled, probably corrupt text, and several lines are obscure; I have rendered the most natural sense. The passage "The great illusion, the great fierce one..." and the Viṣṇu lines ("Whenever on this earth the Dharma becomes weak...") are cited as non-Buddhist views, echoing Bhagavadgītā 4.7-8; they are taken as quotations. The terms kodrava (an inferior grain) and śāli (fine rice) are kept in Sanskrit for the agricultural simile. The phrase "twelve links" refers to the twelve limbs of dependent origination. This passage is dense and its opening verses are partly obscure; I have kept the verses as verses and followed the Tibetan closely. The line on the four reliances alludes to the standard set (meaning, not letters; Dharma, not person; definitive, not provisional; wisdom, not consciousness), of which only the first is named here. The verse phrase "Essence of Hevajra" renders the speaker as Vajragarbha. The "six-thousand-verse commentary" and the "short tantra with its half" (seven hundred verses) are rendered literally. Syllable-to-deity correspondences follow the Tibetan as given; some syllable spellings (such as SMIN and RBA) are as transmitted and may reflect transliteration irregularities. The "great Hevajra" tantra's chapter lists are summarized as given. Rendering of the passage on dwelling in the "bhagas" retains the technical tantric sense of the sacred locus of the consort. The syllables in the first part are mantra-letters of the thirty-seven-deity maṇḍala, and I have kept them as transliterated sounds. Several of the deity epithets there are rendered literally as best they can be identified, for example the goddess "who makes stiffness," the goddess "of deep blue," and "Gaṇḍa's beauty." The last is uncertain. "Ender" renders the Tibetan for Antaka. The line "the exhaustion of the fourth" refers to the fourth state beyond waking, dream, and deep sleep. This is a commentary on the Hevajra tantra. The Tibetan text is difficult in places, where verse lines are quoted and glossed with some textual irregularity. In the passage on the six aggregates and the six sense sources I have followed the Tibetan as given. "Great Lord, Īśvara" renders dbang phyug che. The phrase "sbyor ba" within a verse, rendered "union," may elsewhere carry a ritual sense. This is a densely allusive commentary; the phrase "the thing, and the non-thing too" renders a deliberately cryptic verse fragment, taken here as the commentary glosses it (wisdom and means). The Sanskrit grammatical phonology (the visarga and the letter groups) is rendered as it stands. "Ḍākinījālasaṃvara" is a reconstruction of the Tibetan title referred to as the second examination chapter. The Tibetan names the zodiacal junctions by animal: chu srin is Capricorn (makara), bum pa Aquarius, nya Pisces, lug Aries, glang Taurus, 'khrig pa Gemini, karkaṭa Cancer, seng ge Leo, bu mo Virgo, srang Libra, sdig pa Scorpio (vṛścika), gzhu Sagittarius. The Sanskrit letter groupings in the opening lines are somewhat garbled in transmission and are rendered as they stand. The verse on lalanā and rasanā is quoted in the Hevajra tradition. This passage concerns the subtle-body channels in the Hevajra tradition, and the Sanskrit letters mapped to lunar days are partly garbled in the transmitted Tibetan. The Tibetan transliterations of the vowels and semivowels (for example "Lā Vā Rā Yā Hā") are given as they stand. The channel names given in Tibetan (Mi phyed ma, Phra gzugs ma, Mchog ma, G.yon ma, Thung ngu ma, Rus sbal skyes sgom, Dbang bskur ma, Skyon ldan ma, Bshang ba ma, Mtshan mo ma, Ma mo, Bsil sbyin ma, Drod ma) are left in transliteration because I could not reliably reconstruct the Sanskrit. The word "stuka" is left untranslated, since its sense is unclear. "Hanging one" renders the Tibetan for the drop-bearing channel, probably Sanskrit lambikā. Several of the channel names in the opening list (Jalmā, Varṇasthā, Spyimā, Rgyusbyinmā, Saṃyogavirahā, Sdugumā, Pācikā, Manobhadrā, Trisaṅkarā, Icchā, Gṛhā) are my reconstructions from Tibetan renderings and are uncertain. "The conqueror of Māra" for the central channel is a literal rendering of a name whose Sanskrit I could not confirm. The ordinal "fourth" for the navel state is the fourth state (turīya). Pāṇḍarā is the standard Sanskrit for the white-robed goddess. The "moments" and "joys" are rendered by their standard Hevajra sense, with "the one without characteristics" translating a term whose exact counterpart is uncertain. This is a verse-heavy exposition of the channels, wheels and fierce fire (caṇḍālī, gtum mo) practice. Some verses are cryptic, and the "cattle" (phyugs, paśu) terminology is rendered literally. The final line of the passage lists organs (eyes, ears, nose, tongue, and what "hangs," 'phyang ba) and is rendered literally; the identification of that last item is uncertain. The terms rkyang ma, ro ma and Avadhūtī correspond to lalanā, rasanā and avadhūtī, and I have kept rkyang ma and ro ma in transliteration, because the Tibetan renderings are used here as technical names. This section, on the body as a calendar, the channels, and the signs of death, is highly technical and its numbers are often obscure, so some figures are rendered close to the Tibetan. Terms such as "house," "petals," and "the three qualities" may be technical Kālacakra usage that I have not interpreted. Several lines (for example "four by four" and the passages on the five elements) are elliptical in the source. This passage is dense with Hevajra-tantra symbolic and physiological language. The sense of the lines on the Wheel of Time and the counting of breaths is somewhat compressed; I have translated closely and kept the technical terms. The phrase rendered "the sixfold treasury" and the identification of Dhruva and the Seven Sages follow the Tibetan renderings literally. The passage is cryptic and partly corrupt in its Sanskrit transcriptions. "Piṭu-vajra" is reconstructed from Tibetan pi tsu bajra and may be a variant of Pīṭhavajra or similar. "Trailokyākṣepa" follows the Tibetan trai lo kyā kṣe pa. The sentence "the recitation of the mantra's coded signs is that of the neuter" is literal and obscure. "Disc of Rāhu" renders sgra gcan. The sequence of the wrathful kings follows the standard Hevajra tradition: Yamāntaka, Prajñāntaka, Padmāntaka, Vighnāntaka, Acala, Ṭakkirāja, Nīladaṇḍa, Mahābala, Uṣṇīṣa, Sumbharāja. The phrase "with the divisions of the staff" (དབྱུ་གུའི་དབྱེ་བ) is uncertain. The final syllables of the kāli list are given as in the source; the last cluster is corrupt-looking in transliteration. These include "lungthang," "pothi leaf," and the "jewel" as a recitation instrument. Several lines that appear to correspond to Sanskrit rite terms (immobilizing, pacifying, and so on) follow the six-rite scheme. This passage is highly technical (Sanskrit phonology, elements, lunar days, zodiac). Several zodiac sign names are inferred from the Tibetan (chu srin = Capricorn, bum pa = Aquarius, nya = Pisces, lug = Aries, glang = Taurus, 'khrig = Gemini, ka rka ṭa = Cancer, seng ge = Leo, bu mo = Virgo, srang = Libra, sdig pa = Scorpio, gzhu = Sagittarius). "Empty and the rest" (stong pa sogs) refers to the vowel signs; "dbyu gu lnga" is rendered "five vowel signs". Some lines of the original are obscure, especially the section on the practitioner's two letters and the six rites of striking, bringing down, burning, drawing out, increase, and pleasing, and are rendered cautiously. The verses on mantra syllables and the five eyes are terse and the commentary's wording is elliptic. "The Protector" in the line on the five eyes may refer to the Buddha as the one who gathered the teachings; this is a judgment call. In the passage on "ཡུང་བ་དང་རྡོ་ཐལ" I took it as orpiment (or yellow substance) and ash producing red, which is the least certain sentence here. The Ananta/nāga-king passage is a rain rite; "bdud rtsi" is rendered "nectar" and the "five nectars" here are milk, curd, honey, butter and sugar as the text itself lists them. Several mantra syllables are obscure and I have transliterated them as they stand. The phrase about measures "from the middle of the eyebrow to the end of the navel" and the quotation "of those who come and go" are rendered literally; their precise sense is uncertain. "Ten-fold abiding" is a literal rendering of an unclear ritual phrase. The final planet-and-constellation line is rendered as "Pushya" (Tibetan: king of the constellations), and "full moon" for "ཉ་བ" ("fish"/lunar day), both uncertain. The last sentence is cut off mid-passage. Several names in the rites are uncertain and left in transliteration or literal form: the mantra word beṭuyā and the animal-repelling formulae, "Nor skyong dul ba" (a mudrā name), and "the Ḍoms." The goddess names in the yoga section follow the Hevajra retinue as best identified: rdo rje ma = Vajrā, gau ri = Gaurī, chu yi rnal 'byor ma = Vetālī (here "water yoginī"), bdag med ma = Nairātmyā, sa spyod ma = Bhūcarī, mkha' spyod ma = Khecarī, gtum mo = Caṇḍālī, g.yung mo = Ḍombī, ri khrod ma = Śavarī. "The pra" is left untranslated, since the term refers to a divinatory practice (pra-bringing-down) whose exact sense is uncertain. The phrase "sprinkled/spread out (bdar bas)" in the eclipse rite is uncertain. The text's Tibetan rendering of the ritual vessel measurements may be imprecise. I took "ཐུག་པས" as marking the extent of a quotation ("up to"). The identification of the mantra syllables follows the commentary's own glosses; some letter-lore is terse. The Kye-rdor "Pukkasī" gloss for the vowel u is rendered as given. The letter-puzzle language of the Hevajra tantra is rendered as literally as possible. Several coded terms are uncertain: "Yamī" for the Tibetan "byin za" in the line about ta with ra, "Tilottamā" for the Tibetan "thig le mchog ma", and the name "Dga' bzang ma", left unidentified. The sequences "puk-ka-sī-sa" and "gha-sma-rī" are kept as transliterated code terms. Some letter identifications may reflect corrupt readings in the source. The mantra syllables are given as the commentary has them; some forms (such as "dyānutpannatvāt," "siṃhala," "sālija," "nirasa") are reconstructions of Tibetan transliterations and may be uncertain. The plain-parenthetical gloss "Śakra (Indra)" is added as the house rules direct. The materials named for rosaries and substances are rendered literally as the commentary lists them. Ga bur ("camphor") is a coded term for the pure bodhicitta substance. In the source the passage is made to cohere with the Ḥevajra text, but I kept the literal "camphor." "Karma mudrā" is the actual consort, as distinguished from the visualized one. The "camphor" (karpūra) is a standard tantric term for the white, bodhicitta-like substance; kept literal. "Rasanā and lalanā" translates རྐྱང་མ་དང་རོ་མ, with the Tibetan names for the two side channels read in their Sanskrit equivalents. "Goddess who grants joy and abiding" and the mantras are given in transliteration as in the source. The first verse is compressed and its syntax is uncertain. I take "buddhist" (sangs rgyas pa) as the subject who should not accept the Brahmins' offerings. The Tibetan "ཀྱེའི་རྡོར་འབུམ་ལྔའི་བདག་ཉིད" is read as Hevajra being the essence of the five hundred thousand verses. In the colophon, "the earlier commentary up to the chapter on reality" is a literal reading of the Tibetan. Drogmi Śerab Drakpa is the Tibetan form of the translator's name; I kept it in Tibetan form since no Sanskrit equivalent is given.
